# Ghar ki Baat

## A thousand hours toward the conversations that matter

This course is for an English-speaking adult who wants to participate in a Hyderabadi family’s everyday conversations without learning Urdu or Devanagari writing. It assumes no prior Urdu or Hindi. All learner-facing language is in Roman script. You will learn to greet elders, describe your day, understand family relationships, tell stories, make plans, discuss a problem, express a view respectfully, and keep a conversation alive when words are missing.

The twelve chapters are a continuous textbook, not a phrase list. Each builds a grammatical relationship, shows how to assemble sentences, then puts the relationship into original conversations. Read the explanations, say the examples, use the exercises and their answer keys, and carry the chapter’s family mission into a real exchange. The companion website adds translation practice, grammar questions, listening transcription, voice recording, vocabulary review, and a local journal.

## What the thousand hours mean

The chapter allocations total exactly 1,000 hours. They include learning, repeated listening, retrieval, conversation, feedback, and reassessment. Reading this book once is not a thousand hours of learning. A useful rhythm is ten hours per week for one hundred weeks, or twenty hours per week for fifty weeks. Your pace can change; logged practice measures actual time rather than automatically awarding the advertised chapter allocation.

Spend the time producing and interpreting messages, rather than repeating an answer until you remember its place on the page. Return to the same grammar with new people, different objects, a fresh story, or an unexpected follow-up. Several hundred hours should involve real interaction or recorded conversation work. The browser cannot supply a relative’s reactions, local intonation, or a spontaneous interruption.

## What “Hyderabadi” means here

Shared conversational Urdu/Hindi gives you a broad grammatical foundation. Local Dakhni forms appear throughout, with explicit shared alternatives and register notes. A family might use **hau**, **nakko**, **kaiku**, or **mereku**, and still prefer shared forms in another setting. Dakhni has its own history and complex relationships with Urdu and Hindi; naming it is also a matter of identity. This course follows the learner’s requested description without trying to settle that identity question.

Follow the speech of people you know. Use **aap** with elders and unfamiliar adults until they indicate another preference. A local word is not a license to use a teasing tone with everyone. Keep an English-and-Roman notebook of expressions your family actually uses, their meaning, who said them, and an audio model where possible. This becomes the personal part of the course that no general textbook can write for you.

## Roman spelling and grammatical gender

The course uses readable Roman forms such as **main** for “I,” **mein** for “in,” **hoon**, **hai**, **hain**, **nahin**, and long vowels often written **aa**, **ee**, or **oo**. Roman Urdu has no single universal spelling. Some conventional spellings differ across chapters or family messages; listen for the spoken form, and consult the Roman guide. Exercises list selected alternatives but cannot enumerate every valid spelling or translation.

Examples identify masculine and feminine agreement where it changes a sentence. A grammatical gender describes a noun’s agreement class; it does not claim that an object has a biological gender. Speaker gender and respectful reference also affect forms. Learn a noun with a descriptive phrase and a verb in context. Ask a speaker which self-reference feels right for you when the textbook’s paired examples do not fit.

## The goal and its limits

The endpoint is conversation aligned with selected CEFR B2 spoken interaction and listening abilities: developing a view with reasons, narrating clearly, handling familiar complications, and interacting without a script. It is a learning target, not an accredited examination or a guarantee. A core of 1,000 useful words does not contain every word needed for B2. The course also teaches productive combinations, topic expansion, circumlocution, and how to keep learning from your family.

No recorded native-speaker audio is bundled. The site supports saved family recordings and synthetic Urdu/Hindi system voices where the device provides them. Synthetic speech is not a reliable model of a Hyderabadi accent. Machine-only native-script spelling may be passed to a voice internally; the learner never has to read it. Unsupported phrases fail with guidance rather than silently being read in an English voice. The speaking studio records and plays your voice; optional local recognition checks word sequences where a compatible language pack exists. Neither recognition nor the optional English-language AI reflection coach can certify pronunciation.

## Reading order

Read chapters 1–4 for the first everyday exchanges, chapters 5–8 to connect events and explanations, and chapters 9–12 to develop nuance and independence. Keep the grammar reference and Hyderabadi field guide nearby. At each milestone, use a fresh conversation and an unfamiliar listening sample, then decide what to revisit. Your most useful evidence is a conversation that becomes easier to share.

# A place to begin

Start with people, places, and immediate needs. Learn to ask for help early; meaningful repair lets you stay in a conversation before you know many words.


<a id="chapter-01"></a>

# 01. Your first family conversation

**60 hours · Starting from zero · Goal: enter a short conversation, introduce yourself, and ask for help.**

## Begin with a real reason to speak

Imagine arriving at a relative's home. Someone opens the door, welcomes you, and asks how you are. You do not need a discussion of every tense. You need a greeting, a respectful way to say “you,” a sentence about yourself, and a way to ask the person to slow down. This chapter builds that small conversation carefully enough that you can change it when a different person opens the door.

The course teaches the shared conversational grammar of Urdu and Hindi, with a deliberate focus on Hyderabadi family speech. These varieties overlap substantially in everyday conversation. Vocabulary, pronunciation, social setting, religion, education, and family history affect what any particular person uses. “Hyderabadi” does not mean that every sentence contains a special local word. A relative may switch between local Dakhni forms, broadly shared Urdu/Hindi, Telugu, and English within one conversation. Your aim is understanding and connection, followed by increasingly confident local choices.

All language examples use Roman letters. You will not need to learn another writing system. Roman spelling is a learning aid rather than a universally standardized representation. Your relatives may write the same word differently in messages. Learn the sound and meaning first, then recognize spelling variation. Keep a small personal list with three columns: our course spelling, my relative's spelling, and a recording or memory of how they say it.

## A usable first exchange

Read this exchange aloud slowly. The names are fictional. The greeting shown fits many Muslim Urdu-speaking families; choose the greetings your own family uses.

| Speaker | Roman Urdu/Hindi | English |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Samira | Assalaam alaikum, Khala. | Peace be upon you, Auntie. |
| Khala | Wa alaikum assalaam. Aap kaisi hain? | And upon you be peace. How are you? |
| Samira | Main theek hoon. Aap kaisi hain? | I am well. How are you? |
| Khala | Main bhi theek hoon. | I am well too. |
| Samira | Main Urdu seekh rahi hoon. | I am learning Urdu. |
| Khala | Achchha! | Oh, good! |
| Samira | Zara aahista boliye, please. | Please speak a little slowly. |

Khala means a mother's sister. Detailed family relationships come next chapter. For now, treat the address as one useful name for a person. Samira uses **rahi** because this example has a female speaker. A male speaker says **Main Urdu seekh raha hoon**. We will explain the entire ongoing-action construction in chapter 3; here it is a phrase you can use immediately. Learning a useful phrase before analyzing all its parts is reasonable when the meaning and situation are clear.

Notice that politeness operates in more than one place. **Aap** is the respectful “you”; **hain** matches it; **boliye** is a respectful request. Adding the English word “please” does not repair a disrespectful pronoun. Choose a respectful complete frame, then relax into family usage as people invite you to do so.

## Roman letters as sound instructions

English spelling is an unreliable guide to Urdu pronunciation. In this course, **aa** usually cues a long open vowel, as in **naam**, “name.” **Ee** cues a long high vowel, as in **theek**, “well/correct.” **Oo** cues a long rounded vowel, as in **door**, “far.” Some familiar spellings retain a single letter: **aap**, **hai**, **mein**, **Urdu**, **chai**. Follow the model audio or a relative's pronunciation rather than forcing every spelling into an English reading rule.

The contrast between short and long vowels can distinguish words. **Kal** means yesterday or tomorrow according to context; **kaal** is a different word used in other senses. You do not need the second word as active vocabulary now. You need to hear that vowel duration belongs to the word. Stretching every vowel equally makes a sentence harder to recognize. Say **aap**, then **naam**, then **Aap ka naam?** with a small pause between phrases; gradually connect them.

**Main**, meaning “I,” and **mein**, meaning “in,” both contain a nasalized vowel in ordinary pronunciation. Nasalization means some airflow passes through the nose while the vowel continues. It does not require a strong separate English “n” after the vowel. Say a comfortable vowel, then let air resonate in the nose without closing the tongue against the roof of the mouth. Compare that sensation with a full **n**, where the tongue blocks the mouth. A native model is more valuable than exaggerated imitation.

Our ordinary Roman spelling does not distinguish every sound. In particular, **t** and **d** can represent dental or retroflex sounds. For a dental sound, the tongue touches near the upper teeth. For a retroflex sound, the tongue tip curls or draws back toward the ridge behind them. English **t** is not an exact substitute for either. Ask a willing relative to say **teen**, “three,” and **theek**, “well,” while you watch and listen. Do not press hard or force the tongue. Comfortable, recognizable speech is the goal.

**Th**, **dh**, **ph**, and **bh** often indicate a consonant with a breath release, called aspiration. **Th** is not the English sound in “think.” Start with a clear **t**, release a small puff of air, and keep the two parts close together. Hold a palm near the mouth to notice the difference between a plain release and an aspirated one. **Ph** is not automatically **f**, although English loanwords and individual speakers introduce variation. **Ch** is the affricate heard in **chai**; **chh** adds stronger breath.

**Kh** in **khana**, “food/to eat,” has a breathy release after **k**. In words such as **khair**, “well-being,” some speakers use a fricative sound made with air passing through a narrow space toward the back of the mouth. **Gh** also has more than one realization across vocabulary and speakers. **Q** in words such as **waqt**, “time,” may sound farther back than **k**, or may merge with it in everyday speech. Build listening flexibility rather than judging relatives against a single accent.

## Practice sound with meaning attached

Use this short sound sequence for five minutes at a time: **naam**, **aap**, **theek**, **teen**, **do**, **door**, **hai**, **hain**, **hoon**. First hear a model; next speak along with it; finally say the word after a short silence. Speaking simultaneously is called shadowing. Repeating after silence is a different task: it makes you retrieve the sound rather than borrow its timing. Alternate them.

Record **Main theek hoon** three times at comfortable volume. Listen for three chunks: **main**, **theek**, **hoon**. Check that **theek** does not become two syllables like “thee-ik.” Check that the final vowel in **hoon** is sustained and nasalized rather than followed by an exaggerated English “n.” Compare your recording with a family member only if they are willing. Ask “Which one word is hardest to understand?” A request for one concrete correction is easier to answer than “Is my accent good?”

Browser speech is synthetic and voice availability varies. A device may have an Urdu voice, a Hindi voice, or neither. A Hindi voice is not evidence of Hyderabadi pronunciation. Local words such as **kaiku** may be misread from Roman spelling even by an Urdu voice. Use provided capability labels and, whenever possible, your family's recordings. Do not train yourself to reproduce a machine's mispronunciation.

## Build a sentence from its job

A sentence can identify someone, describe something, locate something, or report an action. Begin with identification and description. English places a linking verb between a person and a description: “I am well.” Urdu puts the linking form near the end: **Main theek hoon**. The linking verb is often called a copula. That name only means the word connects the subject to information about it.

Build the sentence in three steps. Choose **main**, “I.” Choose **theek**, “well.” Add the linking form that belongs with **main**, which is **hoon**. The result is **Main theek hoon**. Replace the person with **aap**, respectful “you.” The ending must become **hain**: **Aap theek hain**. The sentence now means “You are well.” Word replacement and ending replacement work together.

| Person | Basic linking form | Example | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| main | hoon | Main yahaan hoon. | I am here. |
| tum | ho | Tum yahaan ho. | You are here, familiar. |
| aap | hain | Aap yahaan hain. | You are here, respectful. |
| yeh | hai | Yeh chai hai. | This is tea. |
| woh | hai | Woh ghar hai. | That is a house. |
| hum | hain | Hum yahaan hain. | We are here. |
| yeh, plural | hain | Yeh log yahaan hain. | These people are here. |
| woh, plural | hain | Woh log wahaan hain. | Those people are there. |

**Yeh** can refer to this, he, or she when the person is near or part of the current conversation. **Woh** can mean that, he, she, or they according to context. These pronouns do not themselves specify male versus female. English translation requires information from context. You may hear **ye** and **vo**; these are pronunciation and spelling variants, not separate beginner pronouns to memorize.

The respectful form **aap** takes **hain** even when one person is addressed. Respectful reference to an elder can also take plural agreement: **Ammi yahaan hain**, “Mother is here.” This is grammar serving a social relationship. Do not conclude that more than one mother is present. Conversely, **tum** may refer to one familiar person or several; number and politeness overlap in this system.

## “You” is a relationship choice

Use **aap** with elders, unfamiliar adults, and anyone whose preference you do not know. **Tum** is common with friends, siblings, cousins, and people who invite familiarity, but households differ. **Tu** exists and can express intimacy, devotion, anger, or condescension depending on context. Recognize it in other people's speech. Do not adopt it as a default because an online comedy clip uses it.

When someone addresses you with **tum**, that does not automatically mean you should return it. An older relative may use familiar address to a younger relative while expecting respectful address back. If in doubt, keep **aap**. A useful question is **Main aap ko kya bulaaun?**, “What should I call you?” Learn this as a whole polite phrase. It lets your relative tell you the name or title they prefer.

**Ji** can acknowledge someone respectfully or follow a name/title: **ji**, **Ammi ji**, **haan ji**. It does not have one fixed English translation. Tone matters. A clipped, sarcastic **ji** can sound very different from a warm one. Copy the relationship and rhythm you hear, rather than attaching **ji** to every word.

## Ask, answer, and keep the turn moving

Many yes/no questions use the same order as statements with questioning intonation: **Aap theek hain?** Initial **kya** can also mark a yes/no question: **Kya aap theek hain?** Here **kya** does not ask for the name of a thing. In **Yeh kya hai?**, it does: “What is this?” The position and sentence structure help determine the job.

Use **haan**, “yes,” and **nahin**, “no/not,” in shared conversation. In many Hyderabadi families, **hau** is a local affirmative. Try **Hau, main theek hoon** with a relative who uses it. Keep **haan** as an equally useful shared choice. **Nakko** is especially useful for “don't” or “not wanted”; it is not a mechanical replacement for every **nahin**. This distinction protects you from sounding local in one word and unclear in the whole sentence.

To negate a basic description, put **nahin** before the linking form: **Main taiyaar nahin hoon**, “I am not ready.” **Yeh chai nahin hai** means “This is not tea.” In ordinary speech an ending may be omitted when it is recoverable, but keep complete forms while learning. The full structure makes contrasts visible and helps you transfer the pattern to new sentences.

For conversation, a response plus a return question is more useful than a one-word response. **Main theek hoon. Aur aap?** means “I am well. And you?” **Aur** means “and,” and in this position invites the other person to take a turn. **Bhi**, “also/too,” follows the part being included: **Main bhi theek hoon**, “I am well too.” Say it after hearing that the other person is well; it connects your answer to theirs.

## Three conversations you can adapt

### Meeting a cousin

| Roman Urdu/Hindi | English |
| --- | --- |
| Assalaam alaikum. Main Aamir hoon. | Hello. I am Aamir. |
| Wa alaikum assalaam. Main Imran hoon. | Hello. I am Imran. |
| Aap kaise hain? | How are you? |
| Main theek hoon. Tum kaise ho? | I am well. How are you? |
| Main bhi theek hoon. | I am well too. |
| Yahaan aao. | Come here, familiar. |

The final line is an invitation to a younger or familiar person. For an elder, use **Yahaan aaiye**. Learn the two complete versions rather than combining **aap** with a familiar command. “How are you?” uses **kaise** for a male addressee or mixed/plural group and **kaisi** for a female addressee. You will see adjective agreement next chapter; for now, keep the question attached to the person you are greeting.

### A short video call

| Roman Urdu/Hindi | English |
| --- | --- |
| Aap theek hain? | Are you well? |
| Haan, main theek hoon. Aap kahaan hain? | Yes, I am well. Where are you? |
| Main ghar par hoon. | I am at home. |
| Achchha. Sab theek hai? | Good. Is everything all right? |
| Haan, sab theek hai. | Yes, everything is all right. |
| Phir milte hain. | See you again. |

**Ghar par** is a location phrase, “at home.” The small word **par** follows **ghar**; the system of words after nouns comes in chapter 3. Treat this location as one chunk now. **Sab theek hai** is a useful fixed reassurance; **sab** here means everything. **Phir milte hain** is a conversational leave-taking phrase and does not require you to analyze its verb yet.

### Repairing a misunderstanding

| Roman Urdu/Hindi | English |
| --- | --- |
| Aap ka naam? | Your name? |
| Maaf kijiye. Phir se boliye. | Sorry. Please say it again. |
| Aap ka naam kya hai? | What is your name? |
| Mera naam Samira hai. | My name is Samira. |
| Aap Urdu samajhti hain? | Do you understand Urdu? |
| Thodi si. Zara aahista boliye. | A little. Please speak a little slowly. |

**Samajhti** suits a female addressee; **samajhte** suits respectful address to a male. **Thodi si** is a short answer meaning “a little” here. Repair is successful communication. It is better to request one repetition than to nod through a question you did not understand.

## A small first vocabulary that does work

Build associations around useful pairs: **yahaan/wahaan**, “here/there”; **haan/nahin**, “yes/no”; **achchha/theek**, “good/all right”; **main/aap**, “I/you”; **naam/ghar**, “name/home.” Add **Urdu**, **Hindi**, **English**, **zara**, “a little/just” in requests, **aahista**, “slowly,” **phir se**, “again,” **maaf kijiye**, “excuse me/sorry,” and **shukriya**, “thank you.”

Do not spend this week memorizing a hundred disconnected nouns. Take **Yeh kya hai?**, point at an object, hear the answer, and repeat **Yeh ... hai**. One frame makes many words useful. Pointing also checks that you and your partner mean the same thing. If you cannot identify the object from the explanation, ask for a gesture or a different example rather than guessing silently.

## Hear the question before preparing the answer

A beginner often hears **aap**, recognizes “you,” and starts preparing **Main theek hoon** before the question ends. That works for **Aap kaise hain?** but fails for **Aap kahaan hain?**, “Where are you?” Train yourself to wait for the information-bearing word. **Kaise/kaisi** asks about condition or manner in these greetings; **kahaan** asks location; **kya** asks what. The opening pronoun alone cannot identify the question.

Ask a partner to alternate **Aap kaise hain?**, **Aap kahaan hain?**, and **Aap ka naam kya hai?** First answer only in English to check meaning. Next answer in Roman Urdu/Hindi: **Main theek hoon**, **Main ghar par hoon**, or **Mera naam ... hai**. Finally have the partner change the order unpredictably. If you answer the wrong question, return to listening for the question word rather than repeating the response more quickly.

Connected speech makes word boundaries less obvious than a printed table suggests. A speaker may join **kahaan hain** closely, or shorten a familiar greeting. Listen once for the overall purpose and again for the words you can identify. You do not need to hear every sound perfectly before responding. You do need enough evidence to distinguish a request for your name from a request for your location. When that evidence is missing, **Phir se boliye** is the accurate conversational move.

## Exercises with explained answers

### A. Assemble and understand

1. Put these words in a basic sentence: **theek / hoon / main**.
2. Make “You are here” respectful: **aap / yahaan / ...**.
3. Translate **Yeh chai nahin hai**.
4. Ask a female elder how she is.
5. Translate “I am well too.”
6. Distinguish **Main yahaan hoon** from **Main ghar mein hoon**.

Answers: 1. **Main theek hoon**; the linking form closes the sentence. 2. **Aap yahaan hain**; respectful **aap** selects **hain**. 3. “This is not tea”; **nahin** negates the identification. 4. **Aap kaisi hain?** 5. **Main bhi theek hoon**; **bhi** includes the speaker. 6. “I am here” versus “I am inside the house”; **main** and **mein** are different words despite similar sounds. **Ghar par** means at home and need not emphasize being inside.

### B. Repair the interaction

You hear a question too quickly. Choose a response that keeps conversation possible: **Haan** to everything, **Zara aahista boliye**, or changing the subject. Say the best response and then add **Phir se boliye**. Now imagine the person repeats only the final word. Ask **Is ka kya matlab hai?**, “What does this mean?” Learn this as a complete phrase; its internal possession pattern becomes clearer in chapter 2.

The useful response is **Zara aahista boliye**. A successful repair specifies the problem and invites another attempt. If a person remains difficult to understand, say **English mein, please**, “In English, please,” when they share English. Returning to a shared language for one explanation is compatible with building Urdu ability.

### C. Listening and speaking

Have a partner read **Main theek hoon**, **Aap theek hain?**, **Yeh chai hai**, and **Yeh chai nahin hai** in random order. Keep the page hidden. Type what you heard in Roman letters and then its English meaning. Check meaning first, word sequence second, and spelling last. **Nahi** for **nahin** may represent the same familiar pronunciation; missing **nahin** reverses meaning and deserves immediate correction.

Then record a thirty-second self-introduction using a greeting, your name, a statement that you are learning Urdu, a request to speak slowly, and a return question. Play it back the next day. If a relative understands it without reading a transcript, you have stronger evidence than a high score on a spelling quiz.

## Use the 60 hours deliberately

| Activity | Hours | Concrete work |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| Sound and listening foundation | 10 | Short daily sound comparisons, recording, listening again after a delay. |
| Guided sentence building | 10 | Change pronouns and linking forms; explain each change aloud. |
| Core words and phrases | 8 | Retrieve the first useful chunks from situations and pictures. |
| Dialogues and shadowing | 10 | Practice the three dialogues, then replace names and locations. |
| Family interaction and repair | 12 | Many short calls or visits with consent; log one understood and one missed message. |
| Written and listening exercises | 6 | Use companion translation, grammar, and dictation; review reasons for errors. |
| Review and recorded exit task | 4 | Repeat earlier material after several days and make a new introduction. |
| **Total** | **60** | Active study and attentive conversation, not background playback. |

At five hours a week this chapter takes twelve weeks; at ten hours it takes six. Split speaking into small sessions when possible. Ten careful minutes with a willing relative can produce more learning than an hour of distracted imitation. Never require a relative to become an unpaid teacher. Ask for a brief, specific interaction, accept a refusal, and use self-recording or another partner for the rest.

## Exit task and next connection

Complete a two-minute exchange without a written script. Greet someone appropriately, give your name, ask how they are, answer, say that you are learning, and repair at least one deliberately unclear question. Acceptable performance means the partner can identify your intended meaning, your respectful forms are consistent most of the time, and you can recover when understanding breaks. Accent perfection and fast speech are not requirements.

Keep one version of the exchange in your family's preferred style. Ask which greeting, title, and affirmative sound most natural to them. Note their answer as family evidence rather than a rule for all Hyderabadis. Next chapter uses these same people to teach gender, description, possession, and the family relationships that give your conversations personal meaning.

## Reference note

The explanations of agreement and grammatical terms were checked against Rupert Snell's [Glossary of Linguistic Terms, Hindi Urdu Flagship, University of Texas at Austin](https://www.hindiurduflagship.org/assets/pdf/Glossary_Linguistic_Terms.pdf). The dialogues and teaching sequence are original. Local usage requires family confirmation; this chapter does not claim a single uniform Hyderabadi accent.


<a id="chapter-02"></a>

# 02. Family, belongings, and descriptions

**70 hours · Prerequisite: chapter 1 · Goal: talk about relatives, describe people and objects, and explain whose things they are.**

## Make the conversation about your own people

On a family call, “I am well” lasts only a moment. Someone asks who is in a photograph, whether a cousin is older or younger, whose bag is by the door, or how a grandparent is. These questions require a system for people, properties, and relationships. This chapter builds that system from small descriptions. You will learn patterns that let a few familiar words express many new meanings.

Bring a real photograph that everyone involved is comfortable discussing, or draw a fictional family. Do not upload private family pictures to use the exercises. Label people with the names and titles your own family prefers. The relationships below are common, but address conventions vary. A family may call several older relatives **Khala** or **Chacha** affectionately without using the strictly genealogical relationship. Learn both what the title usually means and whom it actually identifies in your household.

Begin with **Yeh meri behen hai**, “This is my sister.” The sentence has a person being introduced, a relationship description, and a linking form. Now compare **Yeh mera bhai hai**, “This is my brother.” The difference between **mera** and **meri** is not the speaker's gender. It follows the thing or person described as “my.” This one observation unlocks much of the chapter.

## Grammatical gender is a word's behavior

Urdu/Hindi nouns belong to masculine or feminine grammatical classes. A noun is a word naming a person, thing, place, or idea. For people, grammatical gender often corresponds to the person's gender in traditional usage, but for objects it is a grammatical property. **Ghar**, “house,” is masculine; **kursi**, “chair,” is feminine. A chair is not being described as biologically female. The classification predicts which other word forms go with it.

Learn each new noun inside a useful phrase: **mera ghar**, “my house”; **meri kursi**, “my chair.” This gives you the noun and one visible clue to its class. If you memorize only **kursi = chair**, you will later have to retrieve the meaning and guess the agreement. A phrase reduces that guesswork. Add an example that matters to you: **Meri kursi yahaan hai**, “My chair is here.”

Many masculine nouns end in **-a**, such as **ladka**, “boy,” and **kamra**, “room.” Many feminine nouns end in **-i**, such as **ladki**, “girl,” and **kursi**. These are useful tendencies, not universal laws. **Paani**, “water,” is masculine; **chai**, “tea,” is feminine. **Ammi**, “mother,” is feminine; **Abba**, “father,” is masculine. Meaning and learned usage override a spelling shortcut.

When you do not know a noun's gender, keep talking. A wrong ending is usually less disruptive than abandoning the message. Ask later **Yeh lafz kaise istemaal hota hai?**, “How is this word used?” or ask a partner to choose between **mera** and **meri** in a complete phrase. Record one corrected example. Do not turn every family conversation into a grammar interrogation.

## Agreement is a matching signal

An adjective describes a noun. **Achchha**, “good,” **bada**, “big/older,” and **chhota**, “small/younger,” change form in familiar patterns. Start with **achchha kamra**, “a good room,” and **achchhi chai**, “good tea.” The adjective changes because the noun's grammatical class changes. This matching is called agreement.

| Situation | Masculine singular | Feminine singular or plural | Masculine plural |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| good | achchha | achchhi | achchhe |
| big/older | bada | badi | bade |
| small/younger | chhota | chhoti | chhote |
| new | naya | nayi | naye |
| old | purana | purani | purane |

The table gives ordinary direct forms, used before adding the location and relationship markers studied next chapter. It is not the entire declension system. Work with one contrast first: **Mera bhai chhota hai**, “My brother is younger,” and **Meri behen chhoti hai**, “My sister is younger.” Both the possessive and adjective follow the noun. The linking form remains **hai** because each sentence describes one person with ordinary singular agreement.

Not every adjective changes. **Saaf**, “clean,” **khush**, “happy,” **pareshaan**, “worried,” **khoobsurat**, “beautiful,” and **laal**, “red,” remain visibly the same in these examples: **saaf kamra**, **saaf kursi**; **khush bhai**, **khush behen**. Do not invent a feminine ending for a word just because a different adjective has one. Learn which adjectives change, then reuse their forms.

An adjective can appear before its noun or as the main description after it: **naya ghar**, “a new house,” and **Ghar naya hai**, “The house is new.” The ending still follows the noun. English does not usually change “new” in either position; Urdu/Hindi does. Say the two versions in sequence so that word order and agreement become connected rather than separate memorized facts.

## Plurals: hear the whole phrase

Number means whether a phrase refers to one or more than one. A useful first family is **ladka → ladke**, “boy → boys,” and **kamra → kamre**, “room → rooms.” Pair the plural noun with **hain**: **Yeh kamre bade hain**, “These rooms are big.” The adjective becomes **bade**, too. That gives three signals of the plural relationship: noun, adjective, and linking form.

For many feminine nouns ending in **-i**, a direct plural uses **-iyaan**: **ladki → ladkiyaan**, **kursi → kursiyaan**. For many other feminine nouns, learn the whole plural: **behen → behnen**, “sister → sisters”; **kitaab → kitaaben**, “book → books.” The final vowel in these plural endings is nasalized. Roman spellings vary; focus on recognizing and producing the family of sounds.

Some masculine nouns do not change visibly in the direct plural: **ghar**, “house/houses,” **bhai**, “brother/brothers,” **dost**, “friend/friends.” Other words tell you the number: **Mere do bhai hain**, “I have two brothers,” literally “My two brothers are.” **Yeh mere dost hain** may introduce several friends or respectfully introduce one male friend. Context and respect can share the same grammatical form.

Avoid making every noun plural with English **-s**. English words inside family speech may take different patterns, including English plurals; that does not make the English ending a rule for Urdu vocabulary. Practice **do kamre**, **teen kursiyaan**, **do behnen**, **teen bhai** as chunks. Add **do**, “two,” and **teen**, “three,” to make the meaning unambiguous.

## Possession: follow what belongs

English “my” stays the same in “my brother,” “my sister,” and “my brothers.” The Urdu forms change: **mera bhai**, **meri behen**, **mere bhai**. Use this decision sequence: identify the possessed noun; identify its gender and number in the current phrase; choose the matching possessive. The owner's gender is not the decision point.

| Owner | With masculine singular | With feminine | With masculine plural |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| my | mera | meri | mere |
| our | hamaara | hamaari | hamaare |
| your, familiar | tumhaara | tumhaari | tumhaare |
| your, respectful | aap ka | aap ki | aap ke |
| his/her/that person's | us ka | us ki | us ke |

Say **Aap ka naam kya hai?**, “What is your name?” **Naam** is masculine singular, so use **ka**. Say **Aap ki behen kahaan hai?**, “Where is your sister?” **Behen** is feminine, so use **ki**. For respectful reference to the sister herself, many speakers use **Aap ki behen kahaan hain?** Both respect and the particular family convention matter; learn the respectful version when talking about an older sister.

With a named owner, place the owner first: **Samira ki kitaab**, “Samira's book”; **Aamir ka phone**, “Aamir's phone”; **Ammi ke dost**, “Mother's friends.” **Ka/ki/ke** relate the owner to the possessed item. A female owner's masculine noun still takes **ka**: **Samira ka ghar**. A male owner's feminine noun still takes **ki**: **Aamir ki kursi**. State the noun's class aloud if the owner's gender keeps distracting you.

Build “My younger sister's name is Aisha” in layers. Start with **behen**, “sister.” Add **meri chhoti**, making **meri chhoti behen**, “my younger sister.” Now that entire phrase is the owner of **naam**. Add **ka naam**: **meri chhoti behen ka naam**. Finish **Meri chhoti behen ka naam Aisha hai**. The **ka** matches **naam**, not **behen**, because the immediate relationship is “sister's name.”

## A family map with useful distinctions

| Relationship | Common term | Practical note |
| --- | --- | --- |
| mother | Ammi | Maa and other family choices also occur. |
| father | Abba | Abu, Papa, and other choices vary by family. |
| elder brother | bade bhai / Bhai jaan | Address may differ from description. |
| younger brother | chhota bhai | Chhota may mean younger, not physically smaller. |
| elder sister | badi behen / Aapa | Learn your household's preferred title. |
| younger sister | chhoti behen | Use the person's name if that is normal at home. |
| mother's mother | Nani | Maternal side. |
| mother's father | Nana | Maternal side. |
| father's mother | Dadi | Paternal side. |
| father's father | Dada | Paternal side. |
| mother's sister | Khala | Often a familiar community address too. |
| mother's brother | Mamu / Mama | Family preference matters. |
| father's sister | Phuppi | Roman spelling includes phuphi and phuppo variants. |
| father's younger brother | Chacha | Some families use it more broadly. |
| father's older brother | Taya | Titles such as Bade Abba may be preferred. |

Do not assume every English “aunt” is **Khala**. Urdu family vocabulary often preserves the side of the family that English leaves unspecified. When telling a story, this precision helps listeners place the person. When you genuinely do not know the exact relationship, a name or the English word “auntie” may be more accurate than a guessed kinship term.

Respectful reference often uses plural grammar: **Nani kaisi hain?**, “How is Grandmother?” **Abba ghar par hain**, “Father is at home.” For a male elder, an adjective can also take a respectful plural form: **Dada khush hain** uses an unchanging adjective, while **Dada taiyaar hain** is similarly easy. Save more variable polite descriptions for examples you have heard in your family.

## Questions that open a photograph

**Kaun** means “who”; **kya** means “what”; **kahaan** means “where”; **kaisa/kaisi/kaise** asks “what kind/how.” Ask **Yeh kaun hain?** when respectfully asking about someone in a photograph. Answer **Yeh mere Nana hain**. Here **mere** and **hain** show respectful reference to one grandfather. In **Yeh mera chhota bhai hai**, ordinary singular agreement suits a younger brother.

**Kis ka/kis ki/kis ke** asks whose. **Yeh kis ki kitaab hai?** means “Whose book is this?” The **ki** matches the feminine noun **kitaab**. Answer **Yeh meri kitaab hai**. To ask about a phone, change the matching forms: **Yeh kis ka phone hai? Yeh mera phone hai**. The question word **kis** does not tell you the owner's gender.

Question words usually remain near the information they replace. **Aap ki behen kahaan hain?** corresponds to **Meri behen ghar par hain**. You are replacing the location phrase with **kahaan** rather than moving everything into English order. This is a useful method for inventing questions: make a statement, identify the unknown part, and replace that part with a suitable question word.

### Check reference before counting endings

Compare **Yeh mere Nana hain** with **Yeh mere do dost hain**. Both contain **mere** and **hain**, but the first introduces one respected grandfather while the second introduces two friends. The numeral **do** and the relationship noun supply information that the endings alone cannot. Avoid a mechanical rule that every **hain** must become English “they are.” Decide whom the sentence refers to before choosing the English pronoun.

Now imagine a photograph with two older men. **Yeh mere Nana hain** could identify one man being pointed out. **Yeh dono mere dost hain**, “Both of these people are my friends,” explicitly includes both. **Dono** means both. A gesture may make the reference clear in person, but a phone call may need a name: **Imran mere dost hain**, “Imran is my friend,” respectfully. Clear reference is part of grammar in use; an accurate ending cannot repair an unidentified person.

## Negation, contrast, and gentle correction

Use **nahin** to deny a description or relationship: **Yeh meri behen nahin hai**, “This is not my sister.” Follow with the correction: **Yeh meri dost hai**, “This is my female friend.” **Dost** can refer to a male or female friend; agreement and context can clarify gender. A respectful reference may instead use **hain**. Do not treat a single translation as the only possible social choice.

**Lekin**, “but,” joins a contrast: **Ghar chhota hai, lekin achchha hai**, “The house is small, but it is nice.” **Aur**, “and,” adds information: **Kamra bada aur saaf hai**, “The room is big and clean.” You can communicate a great deal with accurate small clauses joined clearly. Longer is not automatically better.

For gentle correction, begin **Nahin, yeh ... hain/hai**, using a calm tone. In some Hyderabadi family conversations you may hear **nai** as a reduced negative. Recognize it, but retain **nahin** as the course spelling. Do not substitute **nakko** into every identity correction. **Yeh meri behen nakko hai** is not our taught way to say “This is not my sister.” Local vocabulary has grammatical limits.

## Three dialogues with different purposes

### Looking at a family photograph

| Roman Urdu/Hindi | English |
| --- | --- |
| Yeh kaun hain? | Who is this, respectfully? |
| Yeh meri Nani hain. | This is my maternal grandmother. |
| Aur yeh? | And this person? |
| Yeh mere Nana hain. | This is my maternal grandfather. |
| Aap ki Nani kaisi hain? | How is your grandmother? |
| Woh theek hain. | She is well. |
| Aap ka parivaar bada hai? | Is your family large? |
| Haan, hamaara khaandaan bada hai. | Yes, our extended family is large. |

**Parivaar** is widely encountered in Hindi; **khaandaan** is common in Urdu and can emphasize the extended family or lineage. **Ghar waale** is another conversational expression for one's household/family, explained further later. Learn recognition across choices, then prefer the form people around you actually use. No single word is required to authenticate a family identity.

### Returning a misplaced object

| Roman Urdu/Hindi | English |
| --- | --- |
| Yeh kis ka phone hai? | Whose phone is this? |
| Yeh mera phone nahin hai. | This is not my phone. |
| Kya yeh Aamir ka phone hai? | Is this Aamir's phone? |
| Hau, yeh us ka phone hai. | Yes, this is his phone. |
| Aur yeh kitaab? | And this book? |
| Yeh meri kitaab hai. Shukriya. | This is my book. Thank you. |

The local **hau** changes the conversational flavor without replacing the entire grammar. **Us ka** refers to Aamir because the prior turn established him. Outside that context, it could mean “his,” “her,” or “that person's.” If ambiguity matters, repeat the name.

### Introducing siblings

| Roman Urdu/Hindi | English |
| --- | --- |
| Mere do bhai hain. | I have two brothers. |
| Kya aap ki behen bhi hai? | Do you also have a sister? |
| Haan, meri ek chhoti behen hai. | Yes, I have one younger sister. |
| Us ka naam kya hai? | What is her name? |
| Us ka naam Aisha hai. | Her name is Aisha. |
| Woh kaisi hai? | What is she like?/How is she? |
| Woh khush hai, lekin abhi thaki hui hai. | She is happy, but right now she is tired. |

**Thaki hui** is a useful feminine description, “tired.” The masculine equivalent is **thaka hua**. Learn it as a phrase for now; the course later explains participles. **Woh kaisi hai?** can ask about personality or current well-being depending on the conversation. An answer and a follow-up question resolve that ambiguity more naturally than a dictionary definition.

## Practice laboratory: change one relationship at a time

Take **Yeh mera naya kamra hai**, “This is my new room.” First replace **kamra** with **kursi**. Because **kursi** is feminine, change both matching words: **Yeh meri nayi kursi hai**. Next make the chair plural: **Yeh meri nayi kursiyaan hain**. The feminine adjective and possessive do not gain a new visible plural ending here, but the noun and linking form do. Next return to masculine plural: **Yeh mere naye kamre hain**.

This exercise is more useful when you explain the change. “I used **meri** because **kursi** is feminine” is a portable reason. “I copied the answer” is not. When several changes feel overwhelming, keep the noun fixed and change only the owner: **meri kursi**, **aap ki kursi**, **Samira ki kursi**. Then hold the owner fixed and vary the noun. Separate difficulties before combining them.

For listening, ask a partner to read four descriptions with one changed word each. Identify whether the change concerns owner, size, number, or relationship. A learner who hears the endings can often understand before producing them fluently. Listening is not merely waiting for your turn to speak; it is learning what information a speaker signals with small sounds.

## Written exercises and answers

Translate these into Roman Urdu/Hindi: 1. This is my sister. 2. My house is small. 3. Your chair is new, respectful. 4. These are Samira's books. 5. Who is this, respectfully? 6. My younger brother's name is Imran. 7. This is not my phone. 8. Our rooms are clean.

Answers: 1. **Yeh meri behen hai**; respectful **hain** is possible for an older sister. 2. **Mera ghar chhota hai**; **ghar** is masculine. 3. **Aap ki kursi nayi hai**; both matching forms follow **kursi**. 4. **Yeh Samira ki kitaaben hain**; **ki** stays feminine with plural books. 5. **Yeh kaun hain?** 6. **Mere chhote bhai ka naam Imran hai**. The phrase **mere chhote bhai** is the form used before **ka**; chapter 3 explains this oblique environment. 7. **Yeh mera phone nahin hai**. 8. **Hamaare kamre saaf hain**; **saaf** does not change visibly.

Translate into English: 9. **Meri do behnen hain**. 10. **Yeh mere Abba hain**. 11. **Us ki kitaab purani hai**. 12. **Yeh kis ki kursi hai?** 13. **Woh meri dost hai**. 14. **Ghar bada hai, lekin kamra chhota hai**.

Answers: 9. “I have two sisters.” 10. “This is my father,” with respectful grammar. 11. “His/her book is old”; the possessor's gender is unspecified. 12. “Whose chair is this?” 13. “She is my friend”; **woh** can also mean “that person” with context. 14. “The house is big, but the room is small.” Notice that interpreting natural English sometimes requires more than replacing words in their original order.

For a correction task, repair **Mera behen achchha hai**. The ordinary singular answer is **Meri behen achchhi hai**. Two words change because **behen** is feminine. Repair **Samira ki ghar bada hai** as **Samira ka ghar bada hai**. The owner's identity does not determine **ka**. Repair **Yeh kursiyaan nayi hai** as **Yeh kursiyaan nayi hain**. Number affects the linking form.

## A family mission and a 70-hour plan

Create a spoken family map with six people. For each, give a name or preferred title, the relationship, one description, and one respectful question about them. Ask a relative to correct relationships first and grammar second. If a title differs from the table, record their preference without arguing. You are learning your family as well as a language.

| Activity | Hours | Evidence of active work |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| Gender and adjective instruction | 12 | Build and explain contrasting phrases with familiar objects. |
| Possessive and plural practice | 12 | Reconstruct descriptions after a delay, then compare with answers. |
| Kinship and personalized vocabulary | 10 | Make a family map; confirm address preferences with consent. |
| Dialogue, shadowing, and dictation | 10 | Alternate unseen listening with recorded spoken responses. |
| Family conversation and photo descriptions | 14 | Short unprepared exchanges using approved photos or drawings. |
| Companion exercises and error repair | 8 | Sort errors into meaning, agreement, number, and register. |
| Review and exit recording | 4 | Repeat the mission with a new photo or family arrangement. |
| **Total** | **70** | Cumulative course time: 130 hours. |

At the exit, describe six people and five belongings without reading a script. Ask whose an item is, distinguish both sides of the family for four relationships, and correct one mistaken identification politely. A partner should understand the intended relationships. Agreement may still require self-correction. Before continuing, revisit any error that changes who owns an object or which relative is meant; those errors matter more immediately than a slightly unnatural adjective.

## Reference note

The explanation of adjective agreement and direct/oblique terminology was checked against the relevant entries in Rupert Snell's [University of Texas glossary](https://www.hindiurduflagship.org/assets/pdf/Glossary_Linguistic_Terms.pdf). The chapter's original examples are pedagogical models; household kinship and respect choices should be confirmed with the people addressed.


<a id="chapter-03"></a>

# 03. Home, routines, and what is happening now

**80 hours · Prerequisites: chapters 1-2 · Goal: locate people and things, describe ordinary routines, and distinguish a habit from an action in progress.**

## Two messages that English also keeps apart

Your aunt asks whether you drink tea. You answer “I drink tea,” describing a habit. A minute later someone asks why your hands are occupied. You answer “I am drinking tea,” describing the current activity. Both involve tea and drinking; the difference is how you view the action. Urdu/Hindi makes that contrast with two visibly different constructions: **Main chai peeta hoon** and **Main chai pee raha hoon** for a male speaker. A female speaker says **Main chai peeti hoon** and **Main chai pee rahi hoon**.

Before we build these forms, we need to place the activity somewhere and sometime. Families talk about who is at home, what is in the kitchen, what time someone gets up, and whether somebody is coming now. This chapter therefore connects location phrases, a few changes to nouns, time expressions, and present-tense verbs. Each new part helps you describe a day your listener can actually picture.

Keep a simple drawing of a home nearby. It can be fictional. Label **ghar**, “home/house”; **kamra**, “room”; **rasoi**, “kitchen,” or **kitchen**, which many families use; **darwaaza**, “door”; **mez**, “table”; **kursi**, “chair”; and **baathroom**, “bathroom.” Use the words your relatives recognize. A borrowed English word is not a learning failure when it is normal in the conversation.

## Location words come after their nouns

English uses “in the room” and “on the table.” Urdu/Hindi uses **kamre mein** and **mez par**. **Mein** and **par** follow the noun; for that reason they are called postpositions. You have already used **ghar par**, “at home.” The new idea is to make the phrase yourself instead of treating each location as a memorized exception.

| Marker or phrase | Central use | Example | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| mein | inside/in | kamre mein | in the room |
| par | on/at | mez par | on the table |
| se | from/with/by | ghar se | from home |
| tak | until/as far as | ghar tak | up to the house |
| ke paas | near; with for possession | darwaaze ke paas | near the door |
| ke saamne | in front of | ghar ke saamne | in front of the house |
| ke peeche | behind | ghar ke peeche | behind the house |
| ke andar | inside | kamre ke andar | inside the room |

Do not assign one English word to **se** and use it everywhere. It can mark a source, an instrument, a comparison, or another relationship. Begin with **ghar se**, “from home,” and **phone se**, “by phone/with a phone.” Context determines the intended relationship. Later chapters develop the remaining uses as you need them.

Build “The phone is on the table” from meaning. Identify the thing: **phone**. Identify the location: **mez par**. Add the linking form: **Phone mez par hai**. Change the location to “inside the room”: **Phone kamre mein hai**. Change the thing to plural books: **Kitaaben kamre mein hain**. Location stays a phrase; number still controls **hai/hain**.

## Why kamra becomes kamre

In chapter 2, **kamra** was the direct singular form. Before a postposition, many nouns and their matching words use another form, called the oblique. The term means “the form used in this grammatical environment”; it does not mean that the object is physically tilted. For a common masculine **-a** noun, singular **-a** becomes **-e**: **kamra → kamre mein**, **darwaaza → darwaaze par**, **ladka → ladke ko**.

The adjective and possessive change with the phrase: **mera naya kamra**, “my new room,” but **mere naye kamre mein**, “in my new room.” Build this in layers. Start **kamra**; add **mein**, requiring **kamre**; add the matching **naya**, requiring **naye**; add **mera**, requiring **mere**. Then place it in a sentence: **Phone mere naye kamre mein hai**. The phone is in my new room.

The form **kamre** can also be a direct plural. Compare **Yeh kamre bade hain**, “These rooms are big,” with **Phone kamre mein hai**, “The phone is in the room.” The following marker and the rest of the sentence identify the grammatical job. A form can serve more than one function; do not force each spelling to have only one English label.

Many singular nouns do not visibly change before a postposition: **ghar mein**, **kursi par**, **kitaab mein**, **bhai se**. They are still in the relevant grammatical environment. “Oblique” names the pattern even when no sound changes. In the plural, common forms include **kamron mein**, “in the rooms”; **kursiyon par**, “on the chairs”; **kitaabon mein**, “in the books”; **bhaiyon se**, “from/with brothers.” Learn a few useful plural phrases now; expand systematically with practice.

## Pronouns also have location and relationship forms

**Yeh** becomes **is** before a marker for a nearby singular referent: **is kamre mein**, “in this room,” or **is mein**, “in this/inside it.” **Woh** becomes **us** for a more distant or established singular referent: **us ghar mein**, “in that house”; **us se**, “from/with that person.” For plural reference, **in** and **un** occur: **in kamron mein**, “in these rooms”; **un logon se**, “with/from those people.”

This also explains familiar possessives: **us ka phone** combines an oblique pronoun with **ka**. With **aap**, use **aap ko**, **aap se**, **aap ke paas**. With **main**, forms include **mujhe** or **mujh ko**, “to me,” **mujh se**, “from/with me,” and **mere paas**, “with me/in my possession.” These are high-value forms, so learn complete phrases instead of mechanically adding markers to **main**.

In some Hyderabadi households, **mereku** functions in places where shared Urdu/Hindi uses **mujhe**, and **tereku** where a familiar form such as **tumhein/tujhe** might occur. These forms are local and relationship-sensitive. **Tereku** is not a respectful substitute for **aap ko**. Recognize it in cousins' speech; use **aap ko** with elders unless the household clearly guides you otherwise. The course keeps **mereku** as a Roman spelling; relatives may write **merku**, **mere ko**, or another version.

## Ko: recipient, experiencer, and selected object

**Ko** often marks the person who receives something: **Ammi ko chai**, “tea for/to Mother.” A complete action will come shortly: **Main Ammi ko chai deta hoon**, “I give Mother tea,” for a male speaker. It can also mark an experiencer, the person who feels or needs something: **Mujhe bhook lagi hai**, “I am hungry”; **mujhe** contains the “to me” relationship. Learn this hunger phrase whole for now.

**Ko** also marks many human or specific objects: **Main Aamir ko jaanta hoon**, “I know Aamir,” for a male speaker. Do not add **ko** after every English object. **Main chai peeta hoon** is the normal beginner model for “I drink tea,” without **chai ko**. The choice depends on animacy, specificity, and the verb. At this stage, learn **person ko** in tested examples, and keep ordinary food/drink objects unmarked unless a model calls for something else.

Physical possession often uses **ke paas**: **Mere paas phone hai**, “I have a phone.” Literally the phone is “near/with me,” but natural English says “I have.” **Mere do bhai hain** describes a family relationship and normally does not need **mere paas**. Brothers are not possessions sitting with you. Match the construction to the kind of relationship you mean.

## Verbs name actions; the infinitive is a dictionary form

A verb describes an action or state. Dictionary entries often end in **-na**: **karna**, “to do”; **peena**, “to drink”; **khana**, “to eat”; **jaana**, “to go”; **aana**, “to come”; **rehna**, “to live/stay”; **bolna**, “to speak”; **samajhna**, “to understand”; **seekhna**, “to learn”; **sona**, “to sleep”; **uthna**, “to get up.” The **-na** form is called an infinitive. It does not by itself mean “I do” or “she does.”

For the regular patterns here, remove **-na** to find a working stem: **kar**, **pee**, **kha**, **jaa**, **aa**, **reh**, **bol**, **samajh**, **seekh**, **so**, **uth**. The spelling of long vowels varies in everyday messages; our expanded stems make the construction easier to see. Add endings according to the meaning you need. Be careful with **khana**: it can be the infinitive “to eat” or the masculine noun “food.” Sentence structure tells you which.

## Habits and characteristic activities

For a regular habit, combine the stem with **-ta**, **-ti**, or **-te**, then a linking form. A male speaker says **Main ghar par kaam karta hoon**, “I work at home.” A female speaker says **Main ghar par kaam karti hoon**. **Kaam karna** is literally “work do,” an ordinary noun-plus-verb expression. Keep **kaam** before **karna**; do not search for an English-style single word for every activity.

| Subject | Example | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| main, male | Main chai peeta hoon. | I drink tea. |
| main, female | Main chai peeti hoon. | I drink tea. |
| tum, male | Tum chai peete ho. | You drink tea, familiar. |
| tum, female | Tum chai peeti ho. | You drink tea, familiar. |
| aap, male | Aap chai peete hain. | You drink tea, respectful. |
| aap, female | Aap chai peeti hain. | You drink tea, respectful. |
| woh, male singular | Woh chai peeta hai. | He drinks tea. |
| woh, female singular | Woh chai peeti hai. | She drinks tea. |

For a male or mixed group **hum**, use **Hum chai peete hain**; for an all-female group, **Hum chai peeti hain** is the basic pattern. Honorific reference uses the respectful forms. Gender agreement is not something the English pronoun “I” reveals, so English-to-Urdu exercises should accept an explicitly appropriate male or female answer rather than treating one as universally correct.

Add frequency words: **roz**, “every day”; **aksar**, “often”; **kabhi kabhi**, “sometimes”; **hamesha**, “always”; **aam taur par**, “usually.” **Main roz Urdu seekhta hoon** means “I learn/study Urdu every day,” for a male speaker. **Main kabhi kabhi chai peeti hoon** means “I sometimes drink tea,” for a female speaker. Place the frequency expression before the activity phrase for a clear beginner model.

## What is happening now

For an action unfolding now, use the stem plus **raha/rahi/rahe** and the linking form: **Main chai pee raha hoon**, male; **Main chai pee rahi hoon**, female. **Woh khana kha raha hai** means “He is eating food.” **Woh khana kha rahi hai** means “She is eating food.” The two appearances of **khana/kha** do different jobs: food is the object; eating is the action.

For respectful male address, **Aap kya kar rahe hain?**, “What are you doing?” For respectful female address, **Aap kya kar rahi hain?** For familiar male address, **Tum kya kar rahe ho?** For familiar female address, **Tum kya kar rahi ho?** Compare carefully: the ongoing element reflects gender/number, while the final linking form also reflects the pronoun and level of address.

**Abhi** means “now/right now” in these examples. **Ammi abhi chai bana rahi hain**, “Mother is making tea now.” **Banaana** means “to make”; the working stem is **banaa**. **Abba abhi aaraam kar rahe hain**, “Father is resting now.” **Aaraam karna** is another noun-plus-do expression. The respectful endings treat one parent politely; the English translation stays singular.

Do not put **-ta** and **raha** together for our ordinary present progressive: **Main chai peeta raha hoon** is not the intended beginner “I am drinking tea.” Choose the view first. A repeated habit uses **peeta/peeti hoon**; an action in progress uses **pee raha/rahi hoon**. Other combinations exist for other meanings, but adding more pieces is not automatically more accurate.

## Negation and questions with actions

Use **nahin** before the main action sequence: **Main chai nahin peeta hoon**, “I do not drink tea,” male, or **Main chai nahin peeti hoon**, female. Many speakers omit the final linking form in a negative habitual: **Main chai nahin peeta**. Both are recognizable. Keep the fuller model while learning so that person and agreement remain visible.

For an ongoing negative, say **Main abhi kaam nahin kar raha hoon**, male, or **... kar rahi hoon**, female: “I am not working right now.” A yes/no question can start with **kya**: **Kya aap roz chai peete hain?** A content question uses the relevant unknown: **Aap kahaan rehte hain?**, “Where do you live?” addressed respectfully to a male, or **Aap kahaan rehti hain?** to a female.

Hyderabadi **kaiku** asks “why” in many households: **Aap kaiku hans rahe hain?**, “Why are you laughing?” to a male respectfully. Shared **kyun** works across a wider range of settings: **Aap kyun hans rahe hain?** Questions about motives can sound challenging with abrupt intonation. Begin warmly, and do not use a local word as permission to interrogate an elder.

## Time, numbers, and the shape of a day

Start numbers with **ek, do, teen, chaar, paanch, chhe, saat, aath, nau, das** for one through ten. Add **gyaarah**, eleven; **baarah**, twelve; **bees**, twenty; **tees**, thirty; **chaalees**, forty; **pachaas**, fifty; **sau**, hundred. Hindi-Urdu numbers are not completely predictable by combining tens and ones. Learn your daily times and practical quantities first, then expand with the vocabulary bank.

For a time, use **baje**: **Main saat baje uthta hoon**, “I get up at seven,” male. **Main raat ko das baje sota hoon**, “I sleep/go to bed at ten at night.” Add **subah**, “morning”; **dopahar**, “afternoon/noon period”; **shaam**, “evening”; **raat**, “night,” to disambiguate. **Subah saat baje** is seven in the morning; **shaam saat baje** is seven in the evening.

**Saadhe saat** means half past seven; **sawa saat**, quarter past seven; **paune aath**, quarter to eight. These expressions are useful listening targets but need not delay a conversation: **saat baje tees minute**, seven thirty, is transparent. **Kal** can mean yesterday or tomorrow. Present habits, future forms, past forms, or extra context resolve it. If uncertain, ask whether the person means the coming day or the previous day rather than pretending the ambiguity is yours alone.

## Three conversations across an ordinary day

### Finding someone

| Roman Urdu/Hindi | English |
| --- | --- |
| Ammi kahaan hain? | Where is Mother? |
| Woh kitchen mein hain. | She is in the kitchen. |
| Woh kya kar rahi hain? | What is she doing? |
| Woh chai bana rahi hain. | She is making tea. |
| Aur Abba? | And Father? |
| Woh kamre mein aaraam kar rahe hain. | He is resting in the room. |

The person remains understood across turns. Repeating **woh** in every sentence is useful for learning; in natural conversation it can be omitted when reference is clear. Keep it when two people could be confused. Listening requires tracking the conversation, not only recognizing isolated words.

### Comparing routines

| Roman Urdu/Hindi | English |
| --- | --- |
| Aap subah kitne baje uthte hain? | What time do you get up in the morning? |
| Main chhe baje uthta hoon. Aur aap? | I get up at six. And you? |
| Main saat baje uthti hoon. | I get up at seven. |
| Kya aap roz chai peeti hain? | Do you drink tea every day? |
| Nahin, main aksar coffee peeti hoon. | No, I often drink coffee. |
| Main kabhi kabhi coffee peeta hoon. | I sometimes drink coffee. |

This exchange has a male and female speaker. The switches in **uthte/uthta/uthti** and **peeti/peeta** follow the person addressed or speaking, not the drink. Coffee and tea do not control agreement in these present-tense subject-agreement models.

### A familiar local exchange

| Roman Urdu/Hindi | English |
| --- | --- |
| Tum abhi kya kar rahe ho? | What are you doing now, familiar male? |
| Main Urdu seekh raha hoon. | I am learning Urdu. |
| Kaiku? | Why? |
| Mere ghar waale Urdu bolte hain. | My family members speak Urdu. |
| Achchha. Mereku bhi practice chahiye. | Good. I need practice too, local familiar wording. |
| Hau, hum saath practice karte hain. | Yes, we practice together. |

**Chahiye**, “is needed,” is treated fully next chapter. The local line is a model of recognition, not a requirement to replace **mujhe**. Shared **Mujhe bhi practice chahiye** expresses the same need. **Saath**, “together,” makes the learning goal social and concrete.

## Exercises and explanatory key

Translate: 1. The book is on the table. 2. I live in Hyderabad, male speaker. 3. I am learning Urdu, female speaker. 4. Mother is resting now. 5. The phone is in my new room. 6. I have a phone. 7. Why are you laughing, respectful male? 8. I get up at seven every day, female speaker.

Answers: 1. **Kitaab mez par hai**. 2. **Main Hyderabad mein rehta hoon**. 3. **Main Urdu seekh rahi hoon**. 4. **Ammi abhi aaraam kar rahi hain**. 5. **Phone mere naye kamre mein hai**; the location marker creates the oblique phrase. 6. **Mere paas phone hai**. 7. **Aap kyun hans rahe hain?**; **kaiku** is a local alternative when appropriate. 8. **Main roz saat baje uthti hoon**. Equivalent orderings are possible when the meaning stays clear.

Explain the contrasts: 9. **Main chai peeti hoon / Main chai pee rahi hoon**. 10. **Meri kitaab / meri kitaab mein**. 11. **Yeh kamre hain / woh kamre mein hai**. 12. **Main do bhai hoon / mere do bhai hain**.

Answers: 9. Habit versus current activity, with a female subject in both. 10. “My book / in my book”; the feminine singular phrase has no visible change here. 11. “These are rooms / he or she is in the room”; **kamre** serves different grammatical jobs. 12. The first incorrectly says “I am two brothers”; the second means “I have two brothers.” Replacing English “have” requires choosing the relationship, not inserting a single universal verb.

For dictation, have a partner contrast **roz**, **abhi**, **mein**, **par**, **raha**, and **rahi** in whole sentences. Type both the Roman sentence and the English meaning. Score the location and time view before spelling. For speaking, describe a fictional household picture for ninety seconds, then answer three unscripted questions about what people are doing.

## Plan the 80 hours

| Activity | Hours | Focus |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| Location and oblique phrases | 12 | Move objects in a sketch and describe each new position. |
| Habitual instruction and retrieval | 12 | Tell real routines with changes of person and gender. |
| Progressive instruction and contrasts | 12 | Contrast a regular day with a current snapshot. |
| Time and number listening | 8 | Hear, write, and confirm practical schedules. |
| Dialogue and unseen dictation | 10 | Hear different voices; retrieve meaning after a delay. |
| Conversation missions | 16 | Explain a routine and ask relatives about theirs. |
| Companion exercises and repair | 6 | Correct the reason behind each missed form. |
| Review and exit task | 4 | Describe a new home and day without a script. |
| **Total** | **80** | Cumulative course time: 210 hours. |

Your mission is to plan a ten-minute call: ask where two people are, ask what they are doing, describe three daily habits, and confirm a suitable time for another call. Use **Aap ka matlab ... hai?**, “Do you mean ...?” as a confirmation chunk when a time is unclear. At exit, a partner should distinguish your habit statements from your current-action statements and locate four objects from your descriptions. If they cannot, revisit those contrasts before adding more tenses.

## Reference note

Postpositional phrase structure was checked against the relevant sections of [Hindi-Urdu Phrase Structure Annotation Guidelines, Rajesh Bhatt with Annahita Farudi and Owen Rambow](https://verbs.colorado.edu/hindiurdu/guidelines_docs/PhraseStructureguidelines00.pdf). The present-progressive explanation was checked against the University of Minnesota's [Continuous tenses grammar notes](https://open.lib.umn.edu/hindiurdu/chapter/grammar-notes-4-1/). These sources explain shared grammar; they do not establish a single family dialect or voice model.


<a id="chapter-04"></a>

# 04. Food, shopping, requests, and finding your way

**80 hours · Prerequisites: chapters 1-3 · Goal: ask for what you need, buy everyday items, understand simple directions, and make considerate requests.**

## Turn a need into a manageable exchange

At a family meal you may know the noun **paani**, “water,” yet still hesitate. Should you name it, demand it, ask whether it exists, or explain that you need some? Language gives you several ways to reach the same practical goal, and each creates a different relationship. **Paani!** may work as an urgent warning. **Zara paani dijiye**, “Please give me some water,” is an ordinary considerate request. **Kya mujhe thoda paani mil sakta hai?**, “Could I have some water?” is more indirect.

This chapter teaches you to choose a request that fits the person and situation. You will order food, check quantities, compare options, refuse politely, and find a destination. Accuracy matters because a missing negative can change an order, a mistaken quantity can change a purchase, and an abrupt command can change the tone. You do not need theatrical bargaining or a stock “local accent” to sound connected to Hyderabad. Clear, warm, ordinary interaction is a better starting point.

## Need and want are different constructions

**Mujhe paani chahiye** means “I need/would like water.” Think “Water is needed to me,” then use natural English to understand it. **Mujhe** identifies the person with the need; **paani** is the thing needed; **chahiye** expresses that need. The grammatical pattern is different from **Main paani peeta hoon**, “I drink water.” Do not replace **mujhe** with **main** simply because English begins with “I.”

Change the person who needs something: **Aap ko kya chahiye?**, “What do you need/would you like?” **Ammi ko chai chahiye**, “Mother would like tea.” **Hamein do cup chahiye**, “We need two cups.” **Hamein** is a common “to us” form. You will also hear **hum ko**. Both belong to the same family of relationships introduced with **ko** last chapter.

**Chaahna** means “to want.” A male speaker can say **Main chai chahta hoon**; a female speaker **Main chai chahti hoon**. In a casual order, **Mujhe chai chahiye** is often a useful model. For an action you want to do, use the infinitive: **Main Urdu seekhna chahta hoon**, male, or **... chahti hoon**, female, “I want to learn Urdu.” The wanted action **seekhna** keeps its infinitive form in these beginner models.

Compare **Mujhe chai chahiye**, “I need/would like tea,” **Mujhe chai pasand hai**, “I like tea,” and **Main chai pee raha hoon**, “I am drinking tea,” male. Need, preference, and current activity are different meanings. A person can like tea without wanting a cup now. Hearing that distinction prevents you from treating every mention of tea as an order.

## Likes, dislikes, and taste

**Pasand** expresses liking: **Mujhe biryani pasand hai**, “I like biryani.” **Aap ko kya pasand hai?**, “What do you like?” Negate it with **nahin**: **Mujhe bahut meethi chai pasand nahin hai**, “I do not like very sweet tea.” The experiencer remains **mujhe**, not **main**. **Meethi** agrees with the feminine **chai**.

Useful taste words include **meetha/meethi**, “sweet”; **teekha/teekhi**, “spicy/hot with chili”; **namkeen**, “salty/savory”; **garam**, “hot in temperature”; **thanda/thandi**, “cold”; **taaza**, “fresh”; **achchha/achchhi**, “good.” **Garam** and **namkeen** do not change like **thanda/thandi** in these phrases. Distinguish **garam chai**, hot tea, from **teekha khana**, spicy food. English “hot” hides that difference.

Build “I would like tea with less sugar.” Start with **Mujhe chai chahiye**. Specify **kam cheeni**, “less sugar.” A clear order is **Chai mein cheeni kam rakhiye, please**, “Please keep the sugar low in the tea.” An even simpler two-clause version is **Mujhe chai chahiye. Cheeni kam, please**. Both communicate. The second relies on a shared ordering context rather than a fully explicit grammatical relationship.

For an allergy or medically important restriction, use a clear complete statement and confirm that the listener understands. For example **Mujhe moongphali se allergy hai**, “I am allergic to peanuts.” Do not rely on a quiz score, a synthetic voice, or a half-understood menu when a restriction matters. Learn the exact food name and phrase with a competent speaker, and repeat the restriction when ordering. This is language practice serving a real communicative need.

## Give, take, and receive

**Dena** means “to give”; **lena**, “to take”; **milna**, “to be obtained/be available” in the useful construction here. **Main aap ko paani deta hoon**, male, “I give you water.” **Main chai leta hoon**, male, “I take/have tea.” A female speaker uses **deti hoon** and **leti hoon**. **Mujhe paani milta hai** means “I get water,” though the natural context determines the best English rendering.

In a shop, you may hear **Kya lenge?**, “What will you have/take?” The future-like form is a conventional service question; full future formation comes later. You can answer with a noun phrase: **Ek chai, please**, or **Mujhe ek chai chahiye**. At a meal, **Lijiye**, “Please take/have some,” is a considerate invitation. Reply **Shukriya** if accepting, or **Nahin, shukriya. Abhi nahin**, “No, thank you. Not now,” if declining.

Do not turn **lena** and **dena** into universal English “take” and “give” substitutions. Languages combine actions and objects differently. Learn **chai lena**, “have/take tea,” and **paani dena**, “give water,” in their actual situations. Later chapters introduce these verbs as helpers in expressions that add shades of completion or benefit; that is a separate use.

## Requests and commands have levels of address

A command or request asks someone to do something. Grammarians call this the imperative. The form changes with the relationship. Familiar **tum** forms often use **-o**: **bolo**, “speak”; **baitho**, “sit”; **aao**, “come”; **jaao**, “go”; **dekho**, “look.” Respectful **aap** forms often use **-iye**: **boliye**, **baithiye**, **aaiye**, **jaaiye**, **dekhiye**. A few very common verbs have special respectful forms: **dijiye**, give; **lijiye**, take; **kijiye**, do.

| Purpose | Respectful model | Familiar model |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Please sit. | Baithiye. | Baitho. |
| Please come here. | Yahaan aaiye. | Yahaan aao. |
| Please speak slowly. | Aahista boliye. | Aahista bolo. |
| Please look. | Dekhiye. | Dekho. |
| Please give water. | Paani dijiye. | Paani do. |
| Please take this. | Yeh lijiye. | Yeh lo. |
| Please do this. | Yeh kijiye. | Yeh karo. |

**Zara** softens many requests: **Zara yahaan dekhiye**, “Please look here for a moment.” **Meherbaani karke** means “please/kindly” more explicitly but can sound more formal. Tone, timing, and burden still matter. A polite verb ending does not make an unreasonable request considerate. Conversely, a close sibling may normally use a short familiar form affectionately. Learn your relationships from actual interaction.

To say “don't” in a shared instruction, use **mat**: **Yahaan mat baithiye**, “Please do not sit here”; **Jaldi mat kijiye**, “Please do not hurry.” **Nahin** normally denies a statement, while **mat** normally prohibits an action. A simple contrast is **Aap yahaan nahin baithte hain**, “You do not sit here habitually,” versus **Yahaan mat baithiye**, “Do not sit here.” Choosing the negative means choosing the intended speech act.

## Nakko in context

In many Hyderabadi households **nakko** expresses “don't,” “not wanted,” or refusal. **Chai nakko, shukriya** can mean “No tea, thank you.” **Fikr nakko karo** means “Don't worry” in a familiar local style; shared **Fikr mat karo** is also useful. The respectful shared form is **Fikr mat kijiye**. Follow family models before choosing local imperative combinations for elders.

Do not use **nakko** for every English “not.” For “I am not at home,” use **Main ghar par nahin hoon**. For “This is not my bag,” use **Yeh mera bag nahin hai**. The local word has its own distribution; substituting it everywhere does not produce native speech. A useful learning card has three parts: an actual local example, its shared alternative, and a situation in which you heard it.

**Hau** is a local affirmative in many homes: **Hau, thoda sa**, “Yes, a little.” **Kaiku** asks why; at a meal **Kaiku?** after a refusal may sound like curious family concern or unwanted pressure according to tone. You may decline without giving a long explanation: **Abhi nahin, shukriya**. Conversational competence includes maintaining a preference courteously.

## Count, measure, and confirm the price

Start with useful order quantities: **ek cup chai**, one cup of tea; **do rotiyaan**, two flatbreads; **aadha kilo**, half a kilogram; **ek kilo**, one kilogram; **thoda paani**, a little water; **thodi cheeni**, a little sugar. **Thoda/thodi** follows the noun's gender: masculine **paani**, feminine **cheeni**. **Thode se chaawal**, a little/some rice, uses the plural-like pattern common with **chaawal**.

**Kitna/kitni/kitne** asks how much/how many. **Kitna paani?**, how much water; **kitni cheeni?**, how much sugar; **kitne cup?**, how many cups. In a price question, **Yeh kitne ka hai?** is a common model for “How much is this?” For feminine **kitaab**, **Yeh kitaab kitne ki hai?** can be used. You will also hear **Is ki qeemat kya hai?**, “What is its price?” The latter phrase avoids some ellipsis and is broadly understandable.

Learn **rupaye**, rupees; **pachaas**, fifty; **sau**, hundred; **do sau**, two hundred; **paanch sau**, five hundred; **hazaar**, thousand. For an amount you do not recognize, ask **Kitne rupaye? Zara phir se boliye**, “How many rupees? Please say it again.” Repeat the amount back: **Do sau?**, “Two hundred?” Confirmation is a skill, not a sign that you should abandon the transaction.

**Aur kuchh?** means “Anything else?” **Bas**, “that's all/enough,” closes an order. **Bas, shukriya** is useful at both a counter and a family table. **Bill dijiye, please** requests the bill. **Cash ya card?** is a common mixed-language service question. English loans coexist with Urdu/Hindi grammar naturally; learn the local transaction rather than manufacturing formal equivalents nobody around you uses.

## Compare options without a special comparative ending

English changes “cheap” to “cheaper.” Urdu/Hindi often places the comparison standard before **se**: **Yeh us se sasta hai**, “This is cheaper than that,” for a masculine item. For a feminine item, **Yeh us se sasti hai**. Add **zyada** when needed: **Yeh zyada mehnga hai**, “This is more expensive.” **Kam**, less, can also make a comparison: **Is mein cheeni kam hai**, “There is less sugar in this.”

Build “This tea is sweeter than that tea.” Identify the topic **yeh chai**. Identify the comparison **us chai se**.
