Choose the matching adjective **meethi** and add **hai**: **Yeh chai us chai se zyada meethi hai**. In a context with two cups, **Yeh us se zyada meethi hai** is enough. A more explicit phrase is useful while learning and when a listener might confuse the referent.

**Sab se** introduces a superlative comparison, “most/of all”: **Yeh sab se sasta hai**, “This is the cheapest.” Claims about “the best biryani” are personal opinions, not course facts. Use **Mujhe yeh zyada pasand hai**, “I like this more,” to state your preference without implying that a whole city agrees. That frame is especially useful in warm family disagreements about food.

## Directions are a listening sequence

Learn **seedha**, “straight”; **daayen**, “right”; **baayen**, “left”; **paas**, “near”; **door**, “far”; **aage**, “ahead”; **peeche**, “behind”; **mod**, “turn/corner”; **raasta**, “route/way”; **sadak**, “road”; **dukaan**, “shop.” Directions often combine commands and landmarks: **Seedha jaaiye. Phir daayen mudiye**, “Go straight. Then turn right.” **Phir** orders the steps.

Ask **Dukaan kahaan hai?**, “Where is the shop?” For a more considerate opening, **Maaf kijiye, yeh dukaan kahaan hai?** Add a known name if appropriate. **Yahaan se kitni door hai?** asks “How far is it from here?” **Kya main paidal jaa sakta hoon?**, male, “Can I go on foot?” A female speaker uses **sakti hoon**. We introduce this ability frame here because it is useful; the next stages develop modals systematically.

When you hear several steps, repeat the sequence rather than each isolated noun: **Pehle seedha, phir baayen?**, “First straight, then left?” **Pehle** means first. Ask **Daayen ya baayen?**, “Right or left?” if uncertain. A route understood incorrectly at the first junction cannot be rescued by accurate pronunciation of the final shop name.

## Three practical dialogues

### At a family meal

| Roman Urdu/Hindi | English |
| --- | --- |
| Aur biryani lijiye. | Please have some more biryani. |
| Thodi si, please. Bahut achchhi hai. | A little, please. It is very good. |
| Raita bhi chahiye? | Would you like raita too? |
| Hau, thoda sa. | Yes, a little, local affirmative. |
| Chai? | Tea? |
| Abhi chai nakko, shukriya. | No tea now, thank you, local refusal. |
| Theek hai. | All right. |

The speaker accepts one thing and declines another. **Thodi** matches the understood feminine **biryani**; **thoda** matches the masculine **raita**. Natural short answers depend on the preceding turn. When practicing alone, say the question as well as the answer so you preserve the relationship.

### Ordering at a counter

| Roman Urdu/Hindi | English |
| --- | --- |
| Aap ko kya chahiye? | What would you like? |
| Do chai aur ek plate samose, please. | Two teas and one plate of samosas, please. |
| Cheeni kitni? | How much sugar? |
| Ek chai mein cheeni kam rakhiye. | Please put less sugar in one tea. |
| Aur kuchh? | Anything else? |
| Bas. Kitne rupaye? | That's all. How many rupees? |
| Do sau rupaye. | Two hundred rupees. |
| Do sau? Theek hai. | Two hundred? All right. |

The amount is an invented teaching example, not a current price. **Do chai** is common order shorthand for two servings of tea. **Ek plate samose** uses counter-style speech. A fuller phrase can specify **samoson ki ek plate**. Learn to understand ordinary ellipsis without assuming that every word-by-word shorthand is a universal formal grammar rule.

### Asking for the way

| Roman Urdu/Hindi | English |
| --- | --- |
| Maaf kijiye, pharmacy kahaan hai? | Excuse me, where is the pharmacy? |
| Seedha jaaiye. Phir baayen mudiye. | Go straight. Then turn left. |
| Pehle seedha, phir baayen? | First straight, then left? |
| Haan. Dukaan bank ke paas hai. | Yes. The shop is near the bank. |
| Yahaan se kitni door hai? | How far is it from here? |
| Zyada door nahin hai. | It is not very far. |
| Shukriya. | Thank you. |

Repeat directions on a simple drawn map with a partner. Put two possible destinations on it so that the listener cannot succeed by guessing the only visible shop. Swap roles. This creates a real information gap: each person must understand something the other knows.

## Exercises with more than one natural answer

Translate: 1. I would like water. 2. Please give me two cups of tea. 3. I like biryani. 4. Please do not sit here. 5. This room is bigger than that room. 6. Please go straight and then turn right. 7. No tea now, thank you, in a familiar Hyderabadi style. 8. Please keep the sugar low.

Answers: 1. **Mujhe paani chahiye**. 2. **Mujhe do cup chai dijiye, please**; **Do cup chai dijiye** is sufficient in context. 3. **Mujhe biryani pasand hai**. 4. **Yahaan mat baithiye**. 5. **Yeh kamra us kamre se bada hai**; the comparison phrase uses oblique **kamre** before **se**. 6. **Seedha jaaiye aur phir daayen mudiye**. 7. **Abhi chai nakko, shukriya**; shared **Abhi chai nahin chahiye, shukriya** is also natural. 8. **Cheeni kam rakhiye**.

Interpret: 9. **Mujhe chai pasand hai, lekin abhi chai nahin chahiye**. 10. **Yeh us se sasti hai**. 11. **Daayen ya baayen?** 12. **Mereku paani chahiye**, in a household that uses this local form.

Answers: 9. “I like tea, but I do not want/need tea now”; preference does not entail a current order. 10. “This is cheaper than that,” with a feminine item understood. 11. “Right or left?” 12. “I need/would like water”; shared **Mujhe paani chahiye**. The local form does not tell you the speaker's gender.

Repair these: **Main chai chahiye**; **paani dijiye** addressed with an angry bark; **Yeh mera bag nakko hai**; **Yeh kursi us se sasta hai**. The first becomes **Mujhe chai chahiye**. The second already has respectful grammar, but needs a calm delivery and sensible timing. The third becomes **Yeh mera bag nahin hai**. The fourth becomes **Yeh kursi us se sasti hai**. Language repair may concern sentence structure, social delivery, or both.

## Listening, speaking, and transfer

For a listening test, have a partner read five orders varying drink, sugar, quantity, and acceptance/refusal. Write both the Roman message and its English meaning. The partner then reads two similar routes, one turning right and one left. Draw the destination rather than merely translating. Actions reveal whether the listening succeeded.

For speaking, record a ninety-second role-play with both sides of an order. Then have a person interrupt with an unavailable item: **Chai nahin hai**, “There is no tea.” Ask **Coffee hai?**, “Is there coffee?” and change the order. This unscripted adjustment is the bridge from memorized dialogue to conversation. Do not count a perfectly memorized order as proof that you can manage an unavailable item, changed price, or misunderstood quantity.

## The 80-hour allocation and an A1-style checkpoint

| Activity | Hours | Active work |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| Need, preference, and request instruction | 12 | Compare meanings and build requests for real situations. |
| Imperative and register practice | 10 | Switch familiar and respectful models without mixing them. |
| Food, quantities, and price listening | 12 | Hear orders, confirm amounts, and correct changed requests. |
| Directions and comparison practice | 10 | Navigate maps and compare concrete options. |
| Dialogue, dictation, and shadowing | 10 | Listen before reading; repeat after a delay. |
| Family and transaction missions | 16 | With consent, order, offer, refuse, and ask for directions. |
| Companion tests and error repair | 6 | Revisit errors affecting quantity, negation, or respect. |
| Review and checkpoint | 4 | Complete a new meal, purchase, and map scenario. |
| **Total** | **80** | Cumulative course time: 290 hours. |

The checkpoint combines the first four chapters. Introduce yourself, describe four relatives, explain a daily routine, locate objects, order a simple meal, decline an extra serving, confirm a price, and follow a short route. You may request repetition. The listener should understand your main message without having to translate every word into English. This is a course checkpoint inspired by beginner communicative goals, not an accredited CEFR examination.

Ask your family which local requests sound ordinary and which sound too abrupt for you to use with elders. Record one warm offer, one refusal, and one reassurance with permission. These three little exchanges contain rhythm and relationship information that a synthetic voice cannot certify. Keep them as models while the next stage gives you the grammar to describe what happened yesterday and tell the stories behind your family's everyday life.

## Reference note

The distinction between wanting and needing was checked against the University of Minnesota's [Grammar Notes 1.2](https://open.lib.umn.edu/hindiurdu/chapter/grammar-notes-1-2-2/). The imperative page was linked by the university index but returned an access error during research, so it is not claimed as inspected. Examples, dialogues, prices, and tasks here are original teaching material. Local forms are offered with explicit household and relationship limits.


# Stories we share

Move from naming things to explaining events and intentions. The same family settings now require agreement, linked ideas, and sensitivity to another person’s perspective.


<a id="chapter-05"></a>

# 05 · Yesterday, last time, and the stories families tell

**85 hours · A2 toward B1 · Prerequisites:** the present, noun gender, postpositions, and ordinary requests from Chapters 1–4. The central task is to tell a relative what happened yesterday, answer follow-up questions, and distinguish an event from the background around it. This chapter teaches a shared Urdu/Hindi grammar baseline; it also shows how to listen when a Hyderabadi household uses a different local pattern.

## Start with a small story

**Kal main bazaar gaya. Maine sabzi khareedi. Phir main ghar aaya.** A male speaker says, “Yesterday I went to the market. I bought vegetables. Then I came home.” A female speaker changes the first and last verbs: **Kal main bazaar gayi. Maine sabzi khareedi. Phir main ghar aayi.** Notice the middle sentence stays the same. Why does the speaker’s gender affect **gaya/gayi** but not **khareedi**? Answering that question gives you control of an enormous part of ordinary conversation.

English marks past time in forms such as went, bought, and came. Urdu/Hindi also distinguishes the way the speaker views an action. A **perfective** presents an event as a bounded whole: I bought it, we ate, she arrived. It does not mean the event was quick. **Humne teen ghante kaam kiya** means “We worked for three hours”; those three hours are being treated as one completed episode. **Hum teen ghante se kaam kar rahe the** means “We had been working for three hours”; the speaker puts us inside an ongoing situation instead.

Do not begin by translating every English past-tense sentence with one memorized ending. First choose the picture: a whole event, an action in progress, a repeated old habit, or a state. Then choose the form. This makes a family story easier to follow because the listener hears which events move the story forward and which details provide background.

## Build the perfective from a verb you know

The dictionary form **karna** means “to do.” Remove **na** to find **kar**, the stem, but its perfective is irregular: **kiya**. Many other verbs behave more predictably. From **bolna**, speak, use **bola** for masculine singular, **bole** for masculine plural or polite masculine, and **boli** for feminine singular. Feminine plural is nasalized in careful speech and can be written **boleen** or **boliin** in competing Roman systems; this course usually uses the readable form with a plural auxiliary where needed, for example **vo boli theen**. Listen to a family recording rather than treating one Roman spelling as a complete sound description.

| Dictionary form | Masculine singular | Masculine plural | Feminine singular | Useful meaning |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| aana | aaya | aaye | aayi | came |
| jaana | gaya | gaye | gayi | went |
| karna | kiya | kiye | ki | did |
| lena | liya | liye | li | took |
| dena | diya | diye | di | gave |
| peena | piya | piye | pi | drank |
| khaana | khaya | khaye | khayi | ate |
| dekhna | dekha | dekhe | dekhi | saw |
| sunna | suna | sune | suni | heard |
| sona | soya | soye | soyi | slept |
| hona | hua | hue | hui | happened; became |

The table is a reference, not a request to recite every form before speaking. Begin with six events that actually happened to you. **Aaj main der se utha/uthi** means “Today I got up late.” **Meri behen aayi** means “My sister came.” **Bachche so gaye** means “The children fell asleep.” The last example contains **so gaye**, a verb combination that emphasizes the change into sleep; for now learn it as a useful phrase. Later chapters separate these combinations more systematically.

## Two kinds of event, two agreement patterns

A **transitive** use of a verb has a direct object: someone reads a book, drinks tea, or buys vegetables. An **intransitive** use does not have that kind of object: someone comes, goes, sleeps, or arrives. Ask “what did the person buy?” and you can supply **sabzi**. Asking “what did the person arrive?” does not work. A destination such as **ghar** is not automatically a direct object; it tells you where someone arrived.

With ordinary intransitive perfectives, the verb follows the subject’s gender and number. Build “My sister went home” in steps: **meri behen** is the subject; **ghar** supplies destination; **jaana** supplies the action; **behen** is feminine singular; therefore say **Meri behen ghar gayi**. With two brothers, say **Mere bhai ghar gaye**. For an elder addressed respectfully, **Aap kab aaye?** means “When did you come?” to a male addressee; **Aap kab aayi theen?** can ask a woman when she had arrived, with respectful plural auxiliary. Household preferences for feminine honorific endings vary, so copy your addressee’s preferred pattern.

With most transitive perfectives in the shared standard pattern, mark the doer with **ne**. **Main** becomes **maine**, **hum** becomes **humne**, **tum** becomes **tumne**, and **aap** becomes **aapne**. Names take a separate **ne**: **Sara ne**, **Ali ne**. Third-person pronouns use **usne** for he/she and **unhone** for they or respectful he/she. The two parts may be spaced differently in everyday Roman messages; what matters is recognizing the construction.

Once the doer has **ne**, it no longer controls ordinary verb agreement. An unmarked direct object can control it instead. **Maine chai pi**: I drank tea. **Chai** is feminine, so **pi** is feminine even if the drinker is a man. **Maine paani piya**: I drank water. **Paani** is masculine, so **piya** is masculine even if the drinker is a woman. **Maine do kele khaye**: I ate two bananas. **Kele** is masculine plural, so use **khaye**. This is a relationship between the verb and the unmarked object, not a change in the speaker’s identity.

## Work through the decision instead of guessing

To say “My brother bought a book,” begin with the message rather than English word order. The doer is **mera bhai**. Because **ne** is a postposition, the masculine **aa** noun phrase changes to oblique **mere bhai ne**. The object is **kitaab**, feminine singular. **Khareedna** is transitive in this use and the episode is complete. Put those pieces together: **Mere bhai ne ek kitaab khareedi.** The **ee** sound in **khareedi** is explained by **kitaab**, not by **bhai**.

Now change only the object: **Mere bhai ne do kele khareede** means “My brother bought two bananas.” Change the doer: **Meri behen ne do kele khareede** means “My sister bought two bananas.” The verb does not change because its agreement controller is still the same object. Change the viewpoint to an old habit: **Meri behen kele khareedti thi** means “My sister used to buy bananas.” Now there is no perfective **ne** construction and the habitual form follows the female subject. Minimal changes like these train a reliable mental model.

Some verbs require lexical learning. **Bolna** often appears without **ne**, while **kehna**, to say, normally takes **ne** in the transitive perfective pattern: **Usne kaha** means “He/she said.” **Mujhe pata chala** means “I found out”; do not force **maine** into an expression organized around **mujhe**. Learn verbs with short example sentences and their postpositions. A rule about transitivity is a useful starting point, but a dictionary label alone does not settle every expression.

## What happens when the object has ko?

You already know **ko** in **Ammi ko phone karo**, “Call Ammi.” It marks many specific human direct objects as well as recipients and experiencers in other constructions. In a perfective sentence with a **ne** doer and a **ko** direct object, neither marked noun phrase normally controls agreement. Use the default masculine singular perfective: **Maine Ammi ko dekha**, “I saw Ammi.” The form is **dekha**, although Ammi is female. **Maine apni behen ko bulaya** means “I called my sister over”; the same logic gives **bulaya**.

Compare **Maine ek film dekhi**, “I watched a film,” with **Maine Sara ko dekha**, “I saw Sara.” **Film** is feminine and unmarked, so **dekhi** agrees with it. **Sara ko** is marked, so **dekha** is default. Do not remove **ko** from a person merely to produce an ending you expected. Marking and meaning come first; agreement follows the structure you chose.

With both a thing and a recipient, identify the direct object carefully. **Maine Ammi ko kitaab di** means “I gave Ammi a book.” Here **Ammi ko** is the recipient; **kitaab** remains an unmarked direct object, so **di** agrees with **kitaab**. Replace it with **paani**: **Maine Ammi ko paani diya**. Saying that “any ko makes the verb masculine” would therefore be wrong. The question is whether there is an eligible unmarked direct object, not whether **ko** appears anywhere in the sentence.

## Yesterday and tomorrow share a word

**Kal** can mean yesterday or tomorrow. Grammar and context tell the listener which: **Kal aap kahan gaye the?** means “Where did you go yesterday?” **Kal aap kahan jayenge?** means “Where will you go tomorrow?” If an appointment matters, remove uncertainty with **guzishta din**, “the previous day,” **agle din**, “the following day,” or a specific day and date. Everyday families usually rely on context rather than using formal expressions every time.

Other anchors help organize a story: **pehle**, before/first; **phir**, then; **uske baad**, after that; **aakhir mein**, finally; **achanak**, suddenly; **tab**, then/at that time; **pichhle hafte**, last week; **do din pehle**, two days ago. In **pichhle hafte**, the ending reflects the time expression. Learn the whole phrase. Avoid placing **phir** before every single sentence. A few time anchors and clear verbs can carry the sequence more naturally.

## Was doing, used to do, and was

To move inside an action in progress in the past, use **stem + raha/rahi/rahe + tha/thi/the/theen**. **Main chai bana raha tha** is “I was making tea,” for a male speaker. A female speaker says **Main chai bana rahi thi**. **Ammi baat kar rahi theen** means “Ammi was talking,” with respectful plural auxiliary. There is no **ne** here merely because the event is in the past. The viewpoint is progressive, not perfective.

To describe an old repeated pattern, replace the present auxiliary of a habitual sentence with a past auxiliary. **Main roz Nana ko phone karta tha** means “I used to call Nana every day,” for a male speaker. **Main roz Nana ko phone karti thi** is the female version. **Hum pehle yahan rehte the** means “We used to live here.” An English “would” can also describe an old habit, but do not mechanically use a future form when that is the intended meaning.

For a state, use **tha/thi/the/theen** without a separate action verb: **Ghar khaali tha**, the house was empty; **Chai garam thi**, the tea was hot; **Hum thake hue the**, we were tired. Contrast **Chai garam thi** with **Chai garam hui**: the first describes its state, the second says it became hot. Stories need both kinds of information. **Ghar shaant tha. Bachche so rahe the. Tab darwaze par aawaaz aayi.** The house was quiet. The children were sleeping. Then there was a sound at the door.

## Have done and had done

Add a present auxiliary to connect a completed event to now: **Maine khaana khaya hai**, “I have eaten.” The agreement logic remains the same; **khaya** follows masculine **khaana**. **Vo ghar aayi hai** means “She has come home.” Add a past auxiliary to locate completion before another past reference point: **Maine khaana khaya tha**, “I had eaten”; **Vo pehle hi aa gayi thi**, “She had already arrived.” Context can also make these forms less neatly match English labels, so listen for the whole situation.

**Kya aapne khaana kha liya?** is an extremely useful household question: “Have you eaten?” The combination **kha liya** presents eating as taken care of. A friendly answer is **Hau, kha liya**, “Yes, I have,” where **hau** is a local affirmative used in some Hyderabadi families. The shared alternative is **Haan, kha liya**. Do not assume every relative wants the same local pronunciation; your target is communication with actual people, not a performance of a city identity.

## Three conversations to borrow and vary

### At the door

**Nani:** Kal tum aaye nahin? — You did not come yesterday?

**Learner:** Maaf kijiye, main kaam mein phansa hua tha. — I am sorry; I was caught up with work. A woman says **phansi hui thi**.

**Nani:** Achha, tumne phone bhi nahin kiya. — I see; you did not call either.

**Learner:** Maine message bheja tha. Shayad aapne dekha nahin. — I had sent a message. Perhaps you did not see it.

**Nani:** Hau, baad mein dekha. Aaj baitho. — Yes, I saw it later. Sit today.

The learner acknowledges the complaint before explaining. **Bhi** means “also/either” according to context. Notice **message bheja** because the borrowed noun is commonly treated as masculine here. Families can assign borrowed words different genders; retain a consistent local model when you have evidence. Replace the work explanation with **main safar mein tha/thi**, “I was traveling,” without changing the respectful tone.

### Reconstructing a meal

**Cousin:** Kal kya banaya? — What did you make yesterday?

**Learner:** Maine daal banayi aur bhai ne chaawal banaye. — I made lentils, and my brother made rice.

**Cousin:** Chai kisne banayi? — Who made the tea?

**Learner:** Ammi ne banayi. Phir hum sabne saath khaana khaya. — Ammi made it. Then we all ate together.

**Cousin:** Mereku pehle kaiku nahin bulaya? — Why did you not call me earlier? Shared form: **Mujhe pehle kyun nahin bulaya?**

**Learner:** Agli baar zaroor bulaunga/bulaungi. — Next time I will definitely invite you.

The last response is a useful future phrase for now; Chapter 6 builds it systematically. The local **mereku** marks “me,” and **kaiku** asks “why.” Neither requires changing all the grammar around it. Listen for your family’s actual preference, including **merko**, **mere ko**, or shared **mujhe**.

### A childhood memory

**Learner:** Aap bachpan mein kahan rehte the? — Where did you live as a child? Addressing a male elder.

**Nana:** Hum purane shehar mein rehte the. Shaam ko sab bahar baithte the. — We lived in the old city. In the evening everyone used to sit outside.

**Learner:** Kya aap roz cricket khelte the? — Did you play cricket every day?

**Nana:** Roz nahin. Ek baar gend kho gayi. Phir humne doosra khel khela. — Not every day. Once the ball got lost. Then we played another game.

**Learner:** Phir kya hua? — What happened then?

The repeated **the** creates a background of habits. **Kho gayi** and **khela** move to one episode. **Phir kya hua?** is a better storytelling tool than repeatedly asking unrelated vocabulary questions. Follow the event the relative has introduced.

## Practice with explanations

### A. Choose the form and explain its controller

1. Main bazaar **gaya/gayi**. Give a version for each speaker gender.
2. Ali ne chai **pi/piya**.
3. Sara ne paani **pi/piya**.
4. Humne do rotiyan **khayi/khaya**. State what controls agreement.
5. Maine Nani ko **dekhi/dekha**.
6. Bhai ne Ammi ko kitaab **di/diya**.
7. Kal main khaana **bana raha tha/banaya**, meaning “was making.”
8. Hum pehle yahan **rehte the/rahe**, meaning an old habit.

**Answers:** 1. A male speaker uses **gaya**; a female speaker uses **gayi**. No direct object controls the verb. 2. **Pi**, agreeing with unmarked feminine **chai**. 3. **Piya**, agreeing with masculine **paani**. 4. Feminine plural **khayi**, with nasalized plural pronunciation in careful speech; **rotiyan** is the unmarked object. 5. **Dekha**, default agreement because the doer and direct object are marked. 6. **Di**, agreeing with **kitaab**; **Ammi ko** is the recipient. 7. **Bana raha tha** for a male speaker, **bana rahi thi** for a female speaker. 8. **Rehte the** describes a repeated state of living there.

### B. Translate the message, then compare structure

1. I bought vegetables yesterday.
2. My sister came home late.
3. We saw your brother.
4. Ammi gave me tea.
5. I used to drink tea in the morning. Give both gender versions.
6. The children were playing when I arrived. Use two short sentences if needed.
7. What happened after that?
8. Why did you not call me? Give a shared and a family-local version.

**Possible answers:** 1. **Maine kal sabzi khareedi.** The verb follows **sabzi**. 2. **Meri behen der se ghar aayi.** The subject is feminine. 3. **Humne aapke bhai ko dekha.** The person object has **ko**. 4. **Ammi ne mujhe chai di.** The recipient **mujhe** does not block agreement with unmarked **chai**. 5. **Main subah chai peeta tha/peeti thi.** Habitual agreement follows the speaker. 6. **Bachche khel rahe the. Tab main aaya/aayi.** Chapter 8 will join these with **jab**. 7. **Uske baad kya hua?** 8. **Aapne mujhe phone kyun nahin kiya?** A familiar cousin may say **Mereku phone kaiku nahin kiya?** Use the local option only where that relationship permits it.

### C. Repair the meaning

Explain the problem in **Maine ghar gayi**, **Ali ne chai piya**, **Maine Ammi ko dekhi**, and **Main roz chai pi thi**, if the last sentence is intended to mean “I used to drink tea every day.” Corrections are **Main ghar gayi** for a female speaker, **Ali ne chai pi**, **Maine Ammi ko dekha**, and **Main roz chai peeta tha/peeti thi**. The errors are respectively an unnecessary **ne**, the wrong object agreement, agreement with a marked direct object, and the wrong aspect for an old habit. Correcting the reason matters more than copying the corrected sentence.

## Listening and speaking without depending on a transcript

Ask a willing relative to record the market story naturally, then slowly, with the same meaning. Obtain their permission before saving or sharing their voice. In the companion, a native recording is preferable; synthetic speech is a practice aid and may misread Roman text. First listen with the transcript hidden and choose the event order. Second, type the Roman words you heard. Third, write the English meaning. Finally reveal the target and classify each mismatch as a sound, a word boundary, grammar, or meaning problem. A different sensible Roman spelling is not automatically a listening error.

For speaking, record a forty-five-second account of yesterday with three completed events, one background detail, and one old habit. Replay it and mark only three corrections. Record again without reading. Then have a partner interrupt twice with **Kab?**, **Kisne?**, or **Phir kya hua?** A fluent memorized monologue alone does not show that you can converse. The ability to recover after a question is part of this chapter’s outcome.

## The 85-hour practice plan

| Work block | Hours | Concrete product |
|---|---:|---|
| Read, compare examples, and work the sentence decisions | 10 | Twenty annotated sentences with agreement controllers |
| Perfective and background listening | 15 | Ten short clips with Roman dictation and meanings |
| Controlled translation and correction | 12 | Eighty sentences, reviewed after a delay |
| Story recording and shadowing | 12 | Twelve paired first-and-second recordings |
| Partner or family conversation | 18 | Six conversations of increasing unpredictability |
| Vocabulary review in past-event contexts | 8 | Fifty useful words retrieved inside sentences |
| Childhood interview project | 6 | A respectful interview and an original retelling |
| Exit assessment and targeted repair | 4 | A fresh story, dialogue, and correction log |
| **Total** | **85** | |

Spread the hours across days. One hour of rereading the same page is not equivalent to one hour of attentive listening or live exchange. During a partner block, include a short preparation and a review, but count the minutes actually spent. If a relative is unavailable, alternate roles into a recorder and use unpredictable question cards; schedule a real interaction later rather than pretending self-recording provides the same evidence.

For the childhood interview, ask **Aap bachpan mein kya khelte the?**, **Aapke ghar mein khaana kaun banata tha?**, and **Koi yaadgaar baat batayiye**. Do not press for difficult memories. Retell one approved, ordinary episode in your own words. Preserve the difference between what the relative actually said and what you inferred. A family language course should make people feel heard.

## Exit evidence and the next connection

You are ready to move on when you can tell a two-minute story with understandable sequence; answer five unscripted questions; use several common perfectives accurately; distinguish **kiya**, **kar raha tha**, and **karta tha** in meaning; and repair a misunderstanding with **Mera matlab tha…**, “I meant…”. Aim for at least eight correct meaning choices out of ten and roughly four correct agreement choices out of five on familiar vocabulary, then repeat the task with new events. These are course checkpoints, not a certified proficiency result.

Keep one personal page of verbs you actually used, each with a present, completed, and background sentence. Do not turn a local family variant into a correction target merely because it differs from the shared baseline. Ask which form belongs in your household and which would be clearer to an unfamiliar Urdu/Hindi speaker. The next chapter uses this ability to move between plans, possibilities, and obligations: what will happen, what might happen, and what someone needs to do.

### Source note

The University of Texas at Austin’s [Hindi Grammar](https://hindi.la.utexas.edu/resources/grammar/) was inspected to check the shared tense/aspect framework and terminology. Rupert Snell’s [Hindi-Urdu verb resources](https://hindi.la.utexas.edu/resources/the-hindi-verb/) are an additional reading link: the index description was available, but the full guide did not load during this edition’s research. Examples and activities here are original. The linked university materials are broader Hindi/Urdu resources rather than a validation of every Hyderabadi household form. The **ne** discussion deliberately separates the shared teaching baseline from local usage that should be checked with your own speakers.


<a id="chapter-06"></a>

# 06 · Plans, possibilities, and what needs to happen

**85 hours · A2 toward B1 · Prerequisites:** present and past descriptions, infinitives such as **jaana**, polite address, and **mujhe/aapko**. Your goal is to arrange a family visit, explain what you can do, negotiate constraints, and change a plan politely. You will distinguish a promise from a possibility and advice from an unavoidable obligation.

## A plan is a conversation, not one future sentence

Suppose a relative asks **Kal aa sakte ho?**, “Can you come tomorrow?” A useful reply may need four pieces: **Kal kaam hai. Main shaam ko aa sakta hoon. Lekin mujhe jaldi wapas jaana padega. Kya hum saat baje mil sakte hain?** A male speaker says, “I have work tomorrow. I can come in the evening. But I will have to go back early. Can we meet at seven?” A female speaker uses **aa sakti hoon**. The sentence about having to go stays **mujhe…jaana padega** for either speaker.

Each piece performs a different job. **Kaam hai** gives a constraint. **Aa sakta hoon** expresses ability or practical possibility. **Jaana padega** presents an obligation. **Mil sakte hain?** proposes an arrangement. Learning them as interchangeable translations of English “can,” “must,” or “will” hides these differences. In conversation the difference matters: “I will come” creates a stronger expectation than “I might be able to come.”

Begin each practice exchange with a purpose. Are you promising, asking permission, offering help, making a tentative suggestion, or explaining why you cannot comply? Once you know the purpose, choose the construction. This is also how you preserve warmth in a family exchange: you can accept the relationship while negotiating the practical request.

## Build a straightforward future

The ordinary future of **karna**, to do, is **karunga** for “I will do” said by a man, and **karungi** said by a woman. **Hum karenge** means “we will do” for a masculine or mixed group; **hum karengi** can refer to an all-female group. **Tum karoge/karogi** is the familiar second person. **Aap karenge/karengi** is the respectful second person. **Vo karega/karegi** is “he/she will do”; respectful or plural **vo karenge/karengi** uses plural agreement.

| Subject | Masculine or mixed reference | Feminine reference |
|---|---|---|
| main | karunga | karungi |
| hum | karenge | karengi |
| tum | karoge | karogi |
| aap | karenge | karengi |
| vo, singular familiar reference | karega | karegi |
| vo, plural or respectful reference | karenge | karengi |

The **n** in first-person and plural forms can represent nasalization rather than a fully released consonant. Roman spelling does not capture every sound precisely; compare a trusted model’s recording. Do not lengthen every vowel merely because a word looks long. The course favors readable Roman forms, and ordinary messages may spell the same ending differently.

Use a small set of reliable future forms before extending the pattern: **jaunga/jaungi**, I will go; **aaunga/aaungi**, I will come; **loonga/loongi**, I will take; **doonga/doongi**, I will give; **hoonga/hoongi**, I will be; **khaunga/khaungi**, I will eat; **piyunga/piyungi**, I will drink. A relative may write **piyoonga**; that is a spelling choice. The learner’s task is to connect the sound and meaning, then use a consistent personal spelling.

Build “I will call you tomorrow” step by step. Choose **main** as the doer. Put the time **kal** before the verb. The person called is **aapko**. Use the familiar borrowed expression **phone karna**. The future follows the speaker, giving **Main aapko kal phone karunga/karungi**. There is no perfective **ne**: this is a future action. Change only the person making the call: **Meri behen aapko kal phone karegi**. The feminine third-person ending belongs to **behen**.

## Present forms can also refer to an arranged future

English says “I am coming tomorrow,” and Urdu/Hindi can also use a present progressive for an arrangement: **Main kal aa raha hoon/aa rahi hoon**. This often sounds like a plan already in place. **Main kal aaunga/aaungi** can be a future statement or promise. The distinction is contextual rather than a rigid rule that one form is always certain and the other uncertain. Tone, earlier agreement, and practical circumstances all contribute.

Do not add a future ending to every word in a sentence because **kal** means tomorrow. **Kal meri chhutti hai** means “I have a day off tomorrow.” It describes a scheduled fact with **hai**. **Kal dukaan band hai** means “The shop is closed tomorrow.” **Kal baarish hogi** predicts rain. Listen for what the speaker is presenting: a schedule, an arrangement, or a prediction.

To soften certainty explicitly, use **shayad**, perhaps, or **lagta hai**, it seems: **Shayad main der se aaunga/aaungi**, “Perhaps I will come late”; **Lagta hai kal baarish hogi**, “It looks like it will rain tomorrow.” Do not interpret **shayad** as a promise. When making a plan, ask a follow-up: **Toh main aapka intezaar karun?**, “So should I wait for you?”

## The subjunctive: an action being considered

A **subjunctive** presents an action as wished for, proposed, conditional, or otherwise not asserted as a fact. You do not need the label to speak, but you need the forms. With **karna**, use **main karun**, **hum karein**, **tum karo**, **aap karein**, and **vo kare** for a singular third person. The familiar **tum karo** also looks like an imperative; the surrounding sentence tells you its function. Subjunctive endings do not change for the speaker’s gender.

Compare **Main phone karunga**, “I will call,” with **Main phone karun?**, “Shall I call?” A woman says **karungi** in the future but still **karun?** in the proposal. **Hum kab chalein?** means “When shall we leave?” For an ordinary polite offer, use **Aap kya lena chahenge/chahengi?**, “What would you like to have?” **Aap kya lenge/lengi?** is an ordinary future-shaped service question, “What will you have?” Do not force every English conditional “would” into a subjunctive formula.

Useful irregular subjunctives include **main jaun**, I might/should go; **vo jaye**, he/she might/should go; **aap jayein**, you might/should go; **main aaun**, I might/should come; **vo aaye**, he/she might/should come; **main loon**, I might/should take; **aap lein**, you might/should take; **main doon**, I might/should give; **aap dein**, you might/should give; **ho**, be; **hoon**, I be; **hon**, we/you respectfully/they be. In this course **main ghar mein hoon** remains the familiar factual present “I am at home”; a subjunctive use is identified by its larger context, such as a wish or condition.

Start with proposals and conditions you can use today: **Main darwaza kholun?**, “Shall I open the door?” **Hum yahin baithen?**, “Shall we sit right here?” **Agar waqt mile, toh phone kijiye**, “If you get time, please call.” In the last sentence **mile** presents time becoming available as a condition; **kijiye** is the polite request already learned. The two clauses have different jobs and therefore need not use the same verb form.

## If this happens, then that will happen

**Agar** means “if”; **toh** often introduces the result. **Agar aap kal aayein, toh hum saath khaana khayenge** means “If you come tomorrow, we will eat together.” The first event is a condition, so **aayein** is subjunctive. The second is the predicted result, so **khayenge** is future. Informal speech may omit **agar** or **toh** when the relationship is clear; keep both while learning.

Build a condition by writing two plain ideas: “You have time” and “We can talk.” Make the first a possibility: **Agar aapke paas waqt ho**. Join the consequence: **toh hum baat kar sakte hain**. The result is **Agar aapke paas waqt ho, toh hum baat kar sakte hain**, “If you have time, we can talk.” Notice **aapke paas** expresses available possession. It does not turn into **aapko** because an English speaker might say “if you have.”

Avoid using **jab**, “when,” and **agar**, “if,” as exact synonyms. **Jab aap aayein, toh phone kijiye** means “When you arrive, please call”; the arrival is being treated as expected within the plan. **Agar aap aayein…** leaves open whether the visit will happen. Chapter 8 develops **jab…tab** as a general connection between events. Here the contrast protects a practical arrangement from misunderstanding.

## Sakna: ability and practical possibility

Put **sakna** after the bare verb stem. **Bolna** becomes **bol sakna**, being able to speak. The second verb carries the changing endings: **Main Urdu bol sakta hoon**, a man says, “I can speak Urdu”; a woman says **bol sakti hoon**. **Aap aa sakte hain?** asks a man or a mixed group whether they can come; **Aap aa sakti hain?** addresses a woman respectfully. **Hum madad kar sakte hain** means “We can help.”

Do not keep the **na** ending: **main bolna sakta hoon** is not the taught construction. Break it into meaningful pieces: **bol** names the action, **sakta/sakti** says able, and **hoon** anchors the sentence to the present first person. For past ability, **Main pehle tez daud sakta tha/sakti thi**, “I used to be able to run fast.” For future ability, **Main kal aa sakunga/sakungi**, “I will be able to come tomorrow.”

Permission can also use **sakna**, especially in ordinary polite requests: **Kya main yahan baith sakta/sakti hoon?**, “May I sit here?” More directly, **Kya main yahan baithun?** asks whether to sit here. Watch the relationship and the decision being requested. **Aap baith sakte hain** may mean “You may sit” or “You are able to sit,” so physical ability and permission require context.

## Pana: managing to do something

**Kar paana** focuses on successfully managing an action, often despite a constraint. **Main kal phone nahin kar paya/payi** means “I could not manage to call yesterday.” This is different from making a broad claim that you lacked the ability to use a phone. **Main aapki baat sun nahin paya/payi** means “I could not catch what you said.” The result did not happen in that situation.

Compare **Main Urdu bol sakta/sakti hoon**, “I can speak Urdu,” with **Main meeting mein Urdu bol paya/payi**, “I managed to speak Urdu in the meeting.” **Main samajh nahin saka/saki** is also a common expression, “I could not understand.” Natural usage overlaps; the purpose of the distinction is to give you a productive choice, not to prohibit one verb wherever English uses the same word.

In the completed ability construction, **Main kaam kar saka/saki** and **Main kaam kar paya/payi** ordinarily follow the subject. Do not add **ne** because **karna** appears inside them. The final verb and the entire construction matter. Compare plain perfective **Maine kaam kiya**, “I did the work,” with **Main kaam kar saka**, “I was able to do the work,” for a male speaker.

## Chahna and chahiye solve different problems

**Chahna** describes wanting: **Main ghar jaana chahta hoon/chahti hoon**, “I want to go home.” The person wanting is the ordinary subject, and **chahta/chahti** follows that person. **Main chai chahta hoon** is grammatically a statement that a man wants tea, but a household request usually sounds more natural as **Mujhe chai chahiye**, “I would like/need tea,” or a polite request such as **Chai milegi?**, “Could I have tea?”

With an action, **mujhe + infinitive + chahiye** commonly gives advice or a sense of what should happen: **Mujhe ab jaana chahiye**, “I should go now.” The speaker is **mujhe**, not **main**, and this sentence stays the same for male and female speakers. **Aapko aaraam karna chahiye** means “You should rest.” It is a language pattern; a real health decision belongs with qualified advice, not a grammar exercise.

With a direct object, the infinitive may agree: **Mujhe yeh kitaab padhni chahiye**, “I should read this book”; **Mujhe yeh khat padhna chahiye**, “I should read this letter.” **Kitaab** is feminine, **khat** masculine. At first, use frequent chunks such as **baat karni chahiye**, “should talk,” and **kaam karna chahiye**, “should work.” Do not change the infinitive because of the speaker’s gender in this construction.

## Padna and hona: constraints and arrangements

**Mujhe jaana padta hai** means “I have to go” as a recurring necessity. **Mujhe jaana pada** means “I had to go” on an occasion. **Mujhe jaana padega** means “I will have to go.” These use dative **mujhe** and present the action as required by circumstances. The form is not **main jaana padta hoon**. If the required action has an agreeing object, that can change the infinitive and verb: **Mujhe dawai leni padi**, “I had to take medicine,” with feminine **dawai**.

**Mujhe kal kaam karna hai** means “I have work to do tomorrow” or “I have to work tomorrow.” It often presents a scheduled task, obligation, or intention without foregrounding external pressure as strongly as **padna**. **Mujhe kal jaana hai** can mean “I am to go tomorrow” or “I need to go tomorrow.” Translation depends on context. The distinction is a conversational tendency rather than a mathematical scale of force.

Keep negative meanings separate. **Mujhe nahin jaana hai** can mean “I do not want/intend to go” in some contexts and “I am not to go” in others. **Mujhe jaana nahin padta** clearly says “I do not have to go.” **Aapko nahin jaana chahiye** means “You should not go.” Not having to do something is different from being advised not to do it. If an arrangement matters, add an explanation rather than relying on a potentially ambiguous short negative.

## Reasons and purposes

**Kyunki** gives a reason: **Main jaldi jaunga/jaungi kyunki kal kaam hai**, “I will leave early because I have work tomorrow.” **Isliye** gives a consequence: **Kal kaam hai, isliye main jaldi jaunga/jaungi**, “I have work tomorrow, so I will leave early.” Keep the relationship clear before trying a long sentence. The same information can be packaged in either order.

To give a purpose, use the oblique infinitive with **ke liye**: **Main aapse milne ke liye aaya/aayi hoon**, “I have come to meet you.” **Milna** changes to **milne** before **ke liye**. **Urdu seekhne ke liye main roz sunta/sunti hoon** means “To learn Urdu, I listen every day.” **Taaki** introduces a fuller purpose clause: **Dheere boliye taaki main samajh sakun**, “Please speak slowly so that I can understand.” It is useful but need not replace simpler sentences.

## Conversations with real negotiation

### Arranging a visit

**Khala:** Kal aaoge? — Will you come tomorrow? Addressing a male relative.

**Learner:** Hau, shaam ko aaunga. Aapko kuch chahiye? — Yes, I will come in the evening. Do you need anything?

**Khala:** Aate waqt doodh le aana. — Bring milk on your way.

**Learner:** Theek hai. Lekin mujhe aath baje nikalna padega. — All right. But I will have to leave at eight.

**Khala:** Toh saat baje aa jao. — Then come at seven.

**Learner:** Zaroor. Agar der ho, toh main phone karunga. — Certainly. If I am late, I will call.

The response accepts the visit, offers help, and states a limit early. A female learner substitutes **aaungi** and **karungi**. **Hau** is a local choice; **haan** works in wider conversation. **Aate waqt** is a useful “while/on the way” chunk; Chapter 8 will unpack such linking phrases.

### Declining without dismissing

**Cousin:** Aaj bahar chalte hain. — Let us go out today.

**Learner:** Aaj nahin ja sakta/sakti. Mujhe kaam khatam karna hai. — I cannot go today. I need to finish work.

**Cousin:** Kaiku? Thodi der ich chalenge. — Why? We will go for just a little while. Shared: **Kyun? Thodi der hi chalenge.**

**Learner:** Samajh raha/rahi hoon, lekin kal subah jaldi uthna hai. Kal shaam chalein? — I understand, but I need to get up early tomorrow. Shall we go tomorrow evening?

**Cousin:** Hau, kal theek hai. — Yes, tomorrow is fine.

**Ich** can add restrictive emphasis in local speech, somewhat like **hi**. Do not attach it to every phrase. The important interaction move is offering an alternative after a clear refusal.

### Asking for help learning

**Learner:** Kya hum roz das minute Urdu mein baat kar sakte hain? — Can we talk in Urdu for ten minutes every day?

**Relative:** Kar sakte hain. Kis baare mein? — We can. About what?

**Learner:** Ghar ki baatein. Agar main galti karun, toh pehle mujhe baat poori karne dijiye. — Household matters. If I make a mistake, please let me finish first.

**Relative:** Phir bataun? — Then shall I tell you?

**Learner:** Ji. Ek ya do galtiyan batayiye, taaki main yaad rakh sakun. — Yes. Point out one or two mistakes so that I can remember.

This asks for a manageable kind of help. A relative need not become a full-time teacher. Short, agreed practice can preserve the enjoyment of conversation and provide clearer correction.

## Exercises and explained answers

### A. Choose the intended meaning

1. A man promises to call tomorrow: **Main kal phone karun/karunga**.
2. A woman asks “Shall I call?”: **Main phone karungi/karun?**
3. “I can speak” for a woman: **Main bolna sakti/bol sakti hoon**.
4. “I had to leave”: **Mujhe jaana pada/Main gaya**.
5. “I do not have to go”: **Mujhe jaana nahin padta/Mujhe nahin jaana chahiye**.
6. “If you have time”: **Agar aapke paas waqt ho/Agar aap waqt hain**.
7. “I want to learn,” a man: **Main seekhna chahta hoon/Mujhe seekhna chahiye**.
8. “I should read this book”: **Mujhe yeh kitaab padhni chahiye/padhna chahti hoon**.

**Answers:** 1. **Karunga**, a promise. 2. **Karun?**, a proposal with no gender change. 3. **Bol sakti hoon**, with the bare action stem. 4. **Mujhe jaana pada** states necessity; **main gaya** only reports going. 5. **Mujhe jaana nahin padta** removes necessity; the other advises against going. 6. **Aapke paas waqt ho** expresses available time. 7. **Main seekhna chahta hoon** states desire; **chahiye** changes the message to should. 8. **Mujhe yeh kitaab padhni chahiye**, with feminine **padhni** following **kitaab**.

### B. Build and translate

Translate: “We will come at seven”; “Can you help me?”; “I could not manage to call yesterday”; “I need to work tomorrow”; “Shall we sit here?”; “Please speak slowly so that I can understand”; “If I get time, I will call”; and “You should not wait for me.” Make the male and female speaker changes where relevant.

**Possible answers:** **Hum saat baje aayenge** for a masculine/mixed group, or **aayengi** for an all-female group. **Kya aap meri madad kar sakte hain?** or **kar sakti hain?** according to the addressee. **Main kal phone nahin kar paya/payi.** **Mujhe kal kaam karna hai.** **Hum yahan baithen?** **Dheere boliye taaki main samajh sakun.** **Agar waqt mile, toh main phone karunga/karungi.** **Aapko mera intezaar nahin karna chahiye.** Other natural word orders can preserve the meaning; explain which word carries the relationship before judging spelling.

### C. Repair a plan

A cousin heard **Main aaunga** and prepared dinner, but you only meant that you might come. Repair it: **Maaf kijiye, mera matlab tha ki shayad main aa sakun. Maine pakka nahin kaha tha. Agli baar main saaf bataunga/bataungi.** “I am sorry; I meant that perhaps I might be able to come. I had not said it definitely. Next time I will make it clear.” This is a model, not a way to deny a promise you actually made. Practice explaining the misunderstanding and then proposing a concrete new arrangement.

## Listening, speaking, and the 85-hour plan

For listening, hide a six-line plan and hear it twice. Record who is coming, when, what they will bring, one constraint, and what happens if they are late. Then type the Roman phrases and their English meanings. Listen especially for **sakta**, **padega**, and **chahiye**: missing one of these can change a plan more than missing an adjective. Use actual family audio when available; a browser’s synthetic voice may not model Hyderabadi rhythm or pronounce Roman text reliably.

For speaking, draw three cards: an invitation, a constraint, and a possible alternative. Respond for ninety seconds without writing a full script. Your partner must change the time once and ask **Kaiku/kyun?** once. Record a second version with a different relationship: a cousin first, then an elder. Keep the practical meaning and adjust address, directness, and tone. Use **aap** for the elder rather than assuming local vocabulary removes the need for respect.

| Work block | Hours | Evidence |
|---|---:|---|
| Read and rebuild future/modal examples | 10 | Twenty comparisons with meaning labels |
| Listen to plans and conditions | 14 | Ten dictations plus planning notes |
| Translation, form retrieval, and repairs | 12 | Eighty reviewed sentences |
| Pronunciation and recorded negotiation | 12 | Twelve short role plays |
| Family or partner planning conversations | 18 | Six arrangements with follow-up confirmation |
| Vocabulary and phrase retrieval | 8 | Useful verbs recalled in three modal patterns |
| Real visit or call planning project | 7 | One agreed plan, change, and recap |
| Exit task and focused review | 4 | Fresh scenario and correction log |
| **Total** | **85** | |

For the project, arrange a modest real event with a willing person: a call, tea, or a short walk. Confirm the time and what each person will do. Later explain what changed and why. Count preparation and review honestly, and avoid manufacturing an inconvenience just to practice. If you cannot arrange a live task, use a role play and label it as such.

At exit, you should negotiate a five-minute plan; distinguish will, might, can, should, and have to; give a reason; propose an alternative; and confirm the final arrangement. Aim to convey all critical details correctly, even if some endings need repair. Repeat with an unfamiliar scenario before treating the skill as stable. Chapter 7 brings these structures into feelings, relationships, and the experience-centered sentences families use every day.

### Source note

The University of Texas at Austin’s [Hindi Grammar](https://hindi.la.utexas.edu/resources/grammar/) was checked for the distinction among **chahiye**, **hona**, and **padna** and for common compound-verb patterns. Its [relative-clause and mood teaching notes](https://hindi.la.utexas.edu/resources/nursery-rhymes/) were consulted selectively. The course’s explanations, examples, tasks, and local-family framing are original; these links do not supply native Hyderabadi audio or certify family-specific usage.


<a id="chapter-07"></a>

# 07 · Feelings, care, and talking about the people close to you

**85 hours · B1 development · Prerequisites:** **mujhe/aapko**, present and past auxiliaries, adjective agreement, and the advice/obligation patterns from Chapter 6. The central problem is expressing what someone experiences without translating every English “I am” or “I have” word for word. You will also distinguish your own belongings from someone else’s, offer practical care, and discuss a disagreement without making it worse.

## Experience can be the center of a sentence

An English speaker says “I am hungry.” A common Urdu/Hindi sentence is **Mujhe bhook lagi hai**. Its pieces are **mujhe**, to me; **bhook**, hunger, a feminine noun; **lagi**, the feminine form of **lagna**; and **hai**, the present auxiliary. Think “Hunger has come upon me” as a temporary learning bridge. The natural English translation is still “I am hungry.” The bridge explains why the speaker is **mujhe** and why **lagi** does not change when a man says it.

Similarly, **Mujhe thand lag rahi hai** means “I feel cold.” **Thand** is feminine, so **lag rahi** follows the feeling word, not the person experiencing it. A male speaker does not change it to **lag raha** to match himself. By contrast, **Main thak gaya hoon** means “I have become tired,” for a man, while a woman says **Main thak gayi hoon**. Both sentences describe a personal state, but the language organizes them differently. Learn the construction with each expression.

A **dative experiencer** is a useful name for the person marked with **ko** or a fused form such as **mujhe**, **tumhe**, **humein**, or **unhein**, when that person experiences a feeling, need, perception, or mental state. The label does not mean every feeling uses this structure. **Main khush hoon**, “I am happy,” is an ordinary subject-plus-adjective sentence. Knowing the contrast lets you speak accurately without trying to invent one universal emotional formula.

## A small set of experience patterns

| Roman sentence | Meaning | What to notice |
|---|---|---|
| Mujhe bhook lagi hai. | I am hungry. | **Bhook** is feminine. |
| Mujhe pyaas lagi hai. | I am thirsty. | **Pyaas** is feminine. |
| Mujhe thand lag rahi hai. | I feel cold. | The feeling is ongoing. |
| Mujhe garmi lag rahi hai. | I feel hot. | **Garmi** is feminine. |
| Mujhe neend aa rahi hai. | I am sleepy. | Literally sleep is coming. |
| Mujhe dar lag raha hai. | I feel afraid. | **Dar** is masculine here. |
| Mujhe gussa aa raha hai. | I am getting angry. | **Gussa** is masculine. |
| Mujhe aapki yaad aa rahi hai. | I miss you. | **Yaad** is feminine. |
| Mujhe yeh baat samajh aayi. | I understood this point. | The understood point is feminine. |
| Mujhe iska pata nahin tha. | I did not know about it. | Learn **pata hona** as a chunk. |

Do not memorize only the first column without attending to its noun. **Mujhe dar laga** and **mujhe thand lagi** differ because **dar** and **thand** have different genders. Learn each as a phrase with one natural response: **Kya hua?**, “What happened?”; **Paani lijiye**, “Have some water”; **Andar aa jaiye**, “Please come inside”; **Kya main madad karun?**, “Shall I help?” A feeling expression is most useful when you can participate in the next turn.

The local **mereku** can occupy the same role as **mujhe** in many Hyderabadi family sentences: **Mereku neend aa rahi hai**, “I am sleepy.” The shared form is **Mujhe neend aa rahi hai**. Retain the rest of the learned structure unless your family provides a different model. **Tereku** belongs to familiar relationships; **aapko** remains a clear respectful choice with elders. A local accent does not require using the most intimate pronoun with everyone.

## Move the same experience through time

Once the construction is clear, tense becomes easier. **Mujhe bhook lagi hai** is hunger now. **Mujhe bhook lagi thi** describes hunger at a past point. **Mujhe subah jaldi bhook lagti hai** means “I get hungry early in the morning,” a habitual pattern. **Mujhe baad mein bhook lagegi** means “I will get hungry later.” In all four, **bhook** remains the feminine center of agreement.

Work through “Ammi was feeling cold.” Choose the experiencer **Ammi ko**. Choose **thand**. Choose an ongoing past viewpoint **lag rahi thi**. The result is **Ammi ko thand lag rahi thi**. Some families use respectful plural auxiliaries around elders in particular constructions, but with impersonal experience expressions the feeling noun’s agreement remains important. Do not automatically force every verb into plural just because Ammi is respected. Listen to the specific construction rather than generalizing from **Ammi aa rahi hain**.

Compare **Mujhe gussa aaya**, “I became angry,” with **Main gusse mein tha/thi**, “I was angry.” The first reports anger arising; the second describes the state. **Gusse mein** has the oblique form because of **mein**. **Main gussa hoon** is also heard, but the chunk **gusse mein** is useful for a clear description. These alternatives let you say whether an event caused a reaction or merely describe how someone felt.

## Knowing, understanding, remembering, and forgetting

**Jaanna** means to know a fact or person: **Main unko jaanta/jaanti hoon**, “I know him/her/them.” **Mujhe pata hai** means “I know” in the sense that information is known to me. **Mujhe maloom hai** is another common Urdu expression. **Mujhe Urdu aati hai** means “I know Urdu/I can speak Urdu,” with the language conceived as a skill that comes to the person. **Main Urdu jaanta/jaanti hoon** is also possible, but **aati hai** is a productive skill expression.

**Mujhe samajh aaya** can mean “I understood,” with an understood masculine idea left implicit; **Mujhe yeh baat samajh aayi** explicitly uses feminine **baat**. **Main samajh gaya/gayi** describes the person coming to understand. If someone asks **Samajh aaya?**, answer **Ji, samajh aaya** or **Abhi poori tarah nahin**, “Not fully yet.” You need not repeat every component of the full sentence when the conversation already supplies it.

**Mujhe yaad hai** means “I remember.” **Mujhe yaad aaya** means “I remembered/it occurred to me.” **Main bhool gaya/gayi** means “I forgot.” These are not interchangeable pieces. **Main aapka naam bhool gaya/gayi** means “I forgot your name”; use it with an apology if appropriate. **Mujhe aapka naam yaad nahin aa raha** means “I cannot recall your name right now,” which can soften the social effect by describing temporary retrieval difficulty.

Build a repair request with these expressions: **Maaf kijiye, mujhe yeh lafz samajh nahin aaya. Iska matlab kya hai?** “Excuse me, I did not understand this word. What does it mean?” **Lafz** is masculine, so **aaya**. Replace **lafz** with feminine **baat** and use **aayi**. This is a small controlled change with an immediate use in family conversations.

## Apna keeps ownership tied to the subject

English “his” can be ambiguous. In “Ali called his brother,” is it Ali’s brother or someone else’s? Urdu/Hindi can use **apna** to mean the subject’s own: **Ali ne apne bhai ko phone kiya**, “Ali called his own brother.” **Ali ne uske bhai ko phone kiya** generally points to another person’s brother, with the exact reference supplied by context. **Apna** is called a **reflexive possessive** because it refers back to the subject of its clause.

Like **ka/ki/ke** and **mera/meri/mere**, **apna/apni/apne** follows the possessed thing and its case, not the possessor’s gender. **Sara apni kitaab padh rahi hai** means “Sara is reading her own book.” **Ali apni kitaab padh raha hai** means “Ali is reading his own book.” The same **apni** appears because **kitaab** is feminine. **Sara apna bag laayi** means “Sara brought her own bag,” with masculine **bag** in ordinary usage.

Before a postposition, the noun phrase becomes oblique: **apne bhai ko**, **apne ghar mein**, **apni behen se**. Build “I spoke to my own sister”: the doer is **main**; **baat karna** in the completed event gives **maine…baat ki**; the person spoken to uses **se**; **behen** is feminine, giving **apni behen se**. The result is **Maine apni behen se baat ki**. For now keep the possessive inside its own clause. Longer sentences can have more complex reference; when uncertain, name the person again.

**Khud** means self: **Main khud karunga/karungi**, “I will do it myself.” **Apne aap** can mean by oneself or automatically: **Darwaza apne aap khul gaya**, “The door opened by itself.” Do not substitute **apna** for **khud** everywhere. **Apna** accompanies a noun or stands for an owned thing; **khud** emphasizes the person; **apne aap** has its own adverbial uses. Each solves a different ambiguity.

## Each other, together, and looking after people

**Ek doosre** expresses reciprocity, where two or more people act toward one another. **Hum ek doosre ki madad karte hain** means “We help each other.” **Vo ek doosre se baat kar rahe hain** means “They are talking to each other.” The postposition comes from the action: **madad** uses **ki**, talking uses **se**, and calling someone can use **ko**. Learn the reciprocal phrase together with the verb’s relationship.

**Saath** means together/with: **Hum saath rehte hain**, “We live together”; **Main Ammi ke saath rehta/rehti hoon**, “I live with Ammi.” Being together is not automatically reciprocal action. Two people can **saath khaana khana**, eat together, without feeding each other. This distinction prevents elaborate but unintended sentences when a simple **saath** is enough.

Care often uses **khayal rakhna**, to look after or take care of: **Apna khayal rakhiye**, “Take care of yourself,” respectfully. **Main apne chhote bhai ka khayal rakhta/rakhti hoon**, “I look after my younger brother.” The relationship is **ka khayal**, and **rakhna** changes with the construction. **Ammi ne mera khayal rakha** means “Ammi looked after me,” with masculine **khayal** guiding the perfective form.

## Describing discomfort clearly

This section teaches communication, not diagnosis. **Mere sar mein dard hai** means “My head hurts/I have a headache.” **Sar** is masculine; **dard** is commonly masculine. **Mere gale mein dard hai**, my throat hurts; **Mere pet mein dard hai**, my stomach hurts; **Mujhe bukhar hai**, I have a fever; **Mujhe khaansi hai**, I have a cough. The useful information is location, timing, severity in ordinary words, and what help is needed.

To describe onset, use **kab se**, since when: **Aapko kab se dard hai?**, “How long have you had the pain?” Answer **Subah se**, since morning; **kal se**, since yesterday; **do din se**, for two days. **Bahut dard ho raha hai** means “It hurts a lot.” **Thoda behtar hai** means “It is a little better.” **Dard badh raha hai** means “The pain is increasing.” These phrases describe the experience without naming a cause you do not know.

**Mujhe doctor se baat karni hai** means “I need to speak with a doctor.” **Kya aap mere saath chal sakte/sakti hain?** means “Can you come with me?” **Mujhe is dawai se allergy hai** means “I am allergic to this medicine.” If you are uncertain, say **Mujhe theek se maloom nahin**, “I do not know exactly,” rather than guessing. Medication names, doses, and urgent instructions require careful confirmation in a language both parties understand. The course does not use health role plays to award diagnostic skill.

## Offer care without taking over

Begin with **Aap kaisa mehsoos kar rahe/rahi hain?**, “How are you feeling?” **Kya main kuch laun?**, “Shall I bring something?” is an offer, not an assumption about what the person needs. **Aapko kis cheez ki zaroorat hai?** asks “What do you need?” If the person declines tea, **Theek hai, zaroorat ho toh batayiye**, “All right; tell me if you need anything,” respects the answer.

**Nakko** can decline or discourage in some Hyderabadi family contexts: **Chai nakko, shukriya**, “No tea, thank you.” The shared alternative is **Chai nahin chahiye, shukriya**. Tone and relationship affect how abrupt the short form sounds. A learner who is unsure can use **Abhi nahin, shukriya**, “Not right now, thank you,” and add **Baad mein loonga/loongi**, “I will have some later,” only if that is true. Politeness is not a requirement to make a false promise.

## Disagreement, feelings, and boundaries

**Aap hamesha aisa karte hain**, “You always do this,” can turn a small problem into a claim about someone’s character. A more specific account is **Kal jab baat adhoori reh gayi, mujhe bura laga**, “Yesterday, when the conversation was left unfinished, I felt bad.” You will learn the full **jab** connection in Chapter 8; for now use two sentences: **Kal baat adhoori reh gayi. Mujhe bura laga.** Describe the event, the feeling, and a reasonable request.

**Mera matlab yeh nahin tha**, “That is not what I meant,” repairs an interpretation. **Main aapki baat samajhta/samajhti hoon, lekin…**, “I understand your point, but…,” acknowledges the other person without agreeing. **Is waqt main is baare mein baat nahin karna chahta/chahti**, “I do not want to discuss this right now,” states a limit. With an elder, keep **aap** and a calm delivery; respectful language does not require surrendering the practical meaning of your boundary.

Affection also depends on family style. **Mujhe aapki yaad aati hai**, “I miss you,” is widely useful. **Aapse baat karke achha laga**, “It was nice talking to you,” is a warm closing. **Aapne bahut madad ki, shukriya**, “You helped a lot; thank you,” names the care received. Do not memorize an emotional speech and use it regardless of the situation. Specific, truthful words often build more connection than dramatic phrases.

## Three dialogues

### Tea and a small refusal

**Phuppi:** Chai piyoge/piyogi? — Will you have tea?

**Learner:** Abhi nahin, shukriya. Mujhe neend aa rahi hai; main thodi der aaraam karunga/karungi. — Not now, thank you. I am sleepy; I will rest for a while.

**Phuppi:** Khaana khaya? — Have you eaten?

**Learner:** Hau, kha liya. Aapne khaya? — Yes, I have. Have you eaten?

**Phuppi:** Abhi nahin. — Not yet.

**Learner:** Kya main aapke liye kuch garam karun? — Shall I warm something for you?

The learner returns care rather than only declining it. **Aapke liye** means for you. A local cousin might say **Chai nakko**, but the longer refusal here fits an elder and gives a clear reason.

### Checking on a relative

**Learner:** Aaj kaisa mehsoos kar rahe hain? — How are you feeling today? Addressing a man respectfully.

**Relative:** Kal se behtar hoon, lekin gale mein dard hai. — I am better than yesterday, but my throat hurts.

**Learner:** Kya aapko paani chahiye? — Would you like water?

**Relative:** Hau, thoda sa. — Yes, a little.

**Learner:** Yeh lijiye. Agar kisi se baat karni ho, toh batayiye. — Here you are. If you need to talk to someone, tell me.

**Relative:** Shukriya. Tum apna kaam karo. — Thank you. You do your work.

The conversation does not invent a diagnosis. It checks comfort, offers a concrete action, and respects the relative’s decision. The pronoun difference is natural: an elder may use **tum** with a younger person who uses **aap** in return.

### Whose phone?

**Cousin:** Ali mera phone le gaya? — Did Ali take my phone away?

**Learner:** Nahin, vo apna phone le gaya. Tumhara phone mez par hai. — No, he took his own phone. Your phone is on the table.

**Cousin:** Achha, mereku laga mera phone tha. — Oh, I thought it was my phone. Shared: **Mujhe laga mera phone tha.**

**Learner:** Dono ek jaise hain. Main bhi pehle samajh nahin paya/payi. — Both are alike. I did not understand at first either.

**Cousin:** Theek hai. Hum ek doosre ka phone na lein! — All right. Let us not take each other’s phones!

**Apna** resolves ownership; **mujhe laga** introduces an impression; **ek doosre ka** introduces reciprocity. The dialogue combines grammar to solve a small real ambiguity rather than displaying unrelated rules.

## Exercises with answer keys

### A. Choose and explain

1. A man says he is thirsty: **Mujhe pyaas laga/lagi hai**.
2. A woman says she is afraid: **Mujhe dar lag raha/rahi hai**.
3. Ali reads his own book: **Ali apni/uski kitaab padhta hai**.
4. Sara takes her own bag: **Sara apni/apna bag leti hai**.
5. “We help each other”: **Hum saath ki/ek doosre ki madad karte hain**.
6. “I remember”: **Mujhe yaad hai/Main yaad hoon**.
7. “My stomach hurts”: **Mere pet mein dard hai/Main pet hoon**.
8. “Take care of yourself,” respectful: **Apna khayal rakhiye/Apni khayal rakho**.

**Answers:** 1. **Lagi**, because **pyaas** is feminine. 2. **Lag raha**, because **dar** is masculine, regardless of speaker. 3. **Apni** identifies Ali’s own book; **uski** would normally point elsewhere. 4. **Apna**, because **bag** is treated as masculine here. 5. **Ek doosre ki** gives the reciprocal relationship. 6. **Mujhe yaad hai** is the remembered-information construction. 7. **Mere pet mein dard hai** names location and discomfort. 8. **Apna khayal rakhiye** combines masculine **khayal** with a respectful request.

### B. Translate and vary

Translate: “I miss you”; “Ammi was feeling cold”; “I forgot my own book”; “Can you come with me?”; “I need to talk to a doctor”; “That is not what I meant”; “I do not know exactly”; “We live together”; and “It was nice talking to you.” Then change present to past in two of your answers.

**Possible answers:** **Mujhe aapki yaad aa rahi hai** describes missing the person now; **Mujhe aapki yaad aati hai** describes the recurring feeling. **Ammi ko thand lag rahi thi.** **Main apni kitaab bhool gaya/gayi.** **Kya aap mere saath chal sakte/sakti hain?** **Mujhe doctor se baat karni hai.** **Mera matlab yeh nahin tha.** **Mujhe theek se maloom nahin.** **Hum saath rehte hain.** **Aapse baat karke achha laga.** The first sentence can become **Mujhe aapki yaad aa rahi thi**, and the living arrangement can become **Hum saath rehte the**. Context decides whether a past form is appropriate; grammar is not a reason to misstate the facts.

### C. Find the reference

Compare **Sara ne apni behen ko bulaya** and **Sara ne uski behen ko bulaya**. Who is the sister in each? The first normally means Sara’s own sister. The second refers to someone else’s sister, whose identity must already be clear. Now make the sentence unambiguous for a listener who missed the previous turn: **Sara ne Ali ki behen ko bulaya**. Repeating a name can be clearer than extending a chain of pronouns. Your goal is mutual understanding rather than using the shortest possible sentence.

### D. Respond appropriately

A relative says **Mujhe neend aa rahi hai**. Offer one practical response without commanding them. A possible answer is **Kya aap aaraam karna chahenge/chahengi? Main baad mein phone kar sakta/sakti hoon**, “Would you like to rest? I can call later.” If they say no, accept the answer. A cousin says **Mereku bura laga**. Ask **Kya hua? Agar tum batana chaho, toh main sun sakta/sakti hoon**, “What happened? If you want to tell me, I can listen.” These are offers of conversation, not promises to solve someone’s problem.

## Listening and speaking work

Use four short recordings: hunger, tiredness, missing a relative, and an ownership misunderstanding. On the first pass identify the experience or problem. On the second, type the Roman sentence and an English translation. On the third, attend to **mujhe/main**, **apna/uska**, and the verb endings. These small forms carry relationships that a list of emotion vocabulary cannot teach. If a family speaker uses **mereku**, record both that phrase and a shared **mujhe** equivalent.

For speaking, record three versions of the same message: a twenty-second practical request, a one-minute explanation, and a three-minute conversation with interruptions. Example: you are tired, you need to postpone a call, and you propose another time. A partner asks why and offers a different time. Evaluate whether the message stayed respectful and clear after the interruption. Speech recognition may transcribe a dialect form poorly; use a human listener for pronunciation and do not treat a machine transcript score as a judgment about your accent.

## The 85-hour plan and exit evidence

| Work block | Hours | Product |
|---|---:|---|
| Read and rebuild experience/ownership examples | 10 | Twenty annotated sentence pairs |
| Listening and Roman dictation | 14 | Twelve short experience clips |
| Translation and reference correction | 12 | Eighty reviewed sentences |
| Recording, shadowing, and practical responses | 12 | Twelve short dialogues |
| Family or partner conversations | 18 | Six discussions with follow-up questions |
| Vocabulary retrieval inside care situations | 8 | Fifty useful words in truthful sentences |
| Care and family-language project | 7 | An agreed call, interview, or helpful task |
| Exit role play and repair | 4 | Fresh scenario plus self-review |
| **Total** | **85** | |

For the family-language project, ask how your relatives naturally say “I miss you,” “I am tired,” “Take care,” and “Do you need anything?” Save their preferred Roman spellings beside the course baseline. Ask about differences between speaking to a cousin and an elder. There may be no single answer for the whole family. Record who uses which form without labeling one generation’s speech the only authentic version.

At exit, explain three different personal experiences accurately; ask a caring follow-up; distinguish your own possession from another person’s; use **ek doosre** with an appropriate relationship; and manage a five-minute practical conversation about a changed plan or small misunderstanding. A partner should be able to identify the key facts without reading your preparation. Revisit any pattern that repeatedly changes who experienced or owned something. Chapter 8 connects these individual sentences into longer stories and explanations while keeping those references clear.

### Source note

The shared **lagna**, **aana**, and noun-plus-verb patterns were checked against the University of Texas at Austin’s [Hindi Grammar](https://hindi.la.utexas.edu/resources/grammar/). The [NYU Virtual Hindi curriculum](https://wp.nyu.edu/virtualhindi/environment-community-and-sustainability/) was consulted selectively for its treatment of reflexives and everyday communicative tasks. Original examples are written for Roman-script family conversation; neither source establishes a medical recommendation or a universal Hyderabadi family convention.


<a id="chapter-08"></a>

# 08 · Connecting ideas without losing the listener

**90 hours · B1 development · Prerequisites:** present and past events, **ne/ko/se**, future and subjunctive, and the possession/reference patterns in Chapter 7. Your task is to tell a connected story, explain a decision, identify the person or thing you mean, and report what someone said while keeping clear who did what.

## Two clear sentences are the starting point

**Mere paas ek kitaab hai. Aapko vo kitaab chahiye.** “I have a book. You need that book.” These are easy to understand, but repeating the noun becomes awkward in a longer conversation. A connected version is **Jo kitaab mere paas hai, aapko vo chahiye**, “The book that I have is the one you need.” The first part identifies a book; the second says something about that same book. The repeated relationship is what makes the sentence work.

A **clause** is a group organized around a verb, often expressing an event or state. A complete sentence can contain one clause or several. A **relative clause** helps identify something: the book that I have, the person who called, the place where we met. In Urdu/Hindi, a common pattern begins with a **j** word and resumes with a matching demonstrative such as **vo**, **vahan**, or **tab**. This pair is called **relative–correlative**. Think “the one which…, that one…” as a learning bridge.

Longer is not automatically better. If you lose track of the people or verb endings, return to two short sentences. The goal is to make the listener’s work easier. Good conversational B1/B2 development includes choosing when to connect and when to pause, repeat a name, or ask whether the listener is following.

## Jo…vo: the one who, the thing that

**Jo kal aaya tha, vo mera bhai hai** means “The person who came yesterday is my brother.” **Jo chai mez par hai, vo aapki hai** means “The tea that is on the table is yours.” The **jo** clause introduces the shared person or thing; **vo** resumes it in the main statement. The relative clause can include the noun, as in **jo chai**, which often makes the reference clearer to a learner.

Build “The food Ammi made was very good” in stages. First: **Ammi ne khaana banaya**, Ammi made food. Second: **Khaana bahut achha tha**, the food was very good. Identify the common element, **khaana**. Put it in a relative clause: **Jo khaana Ammi ne banaya tha**. Resume it: **vo bahut achha tha**. The result is **Jo khaana Ammi ne banaya tha, vo bahut achha tha**. The first **tha** places the making before the evaluation; in many contexts **banaya** alone is sufficient.

Notice that **Ammi ne** still follows the Chapter 5 pattern. A relative clause does not cancel the grammar inside it. **Jo kitaab maine padhi, vo achhi thi** means “The book I read was good.” **Padhi** follows feminine **kitaab**. **Jo aadmi aaya, vo mere chacha hain** means “The man who came is my uncle,” with respectful plural **hain** in the identification. Family speech may also use respectful agreement in the arrival clause; keep the intended respect consistent.

Everyday speakers sometimes omit the second **vo** when the reference remains clear. Learn the full pair first, then recognize shorter versions.
