Do not insert a separate English-style “that” after every noun: the course pattern is a whole construction, not a word-for-word replacement of one English relative pronoun.

## Jis, jisko, jisse, and jiska

**Jo** changes form when its role needs a postposition. The oblique singular base is **jis**; plural is **jin**. Combine it with the relationship required by the clause: **jisko**, whom/to whom; **jisse**, from whom/with whom; **jiska/jiski/jiske**, whose. These are not decorative variants. They show how the identified person or thing participates in the first clause.

**Jis aadmi ko maine dekha, vo yahin rehta hai** means “The man I saw lives right here.” The person is the marked direct object of **dekha**, so use **jis aadmi ko**. The main clause’s **vo** is the subject of **rehta hai**, so it is unmarked. The relative and correlative need not have the same case; they perform different roles in their own clauses.

**Jis se maine baat ki, vo aapki behen theen?** asks “Was the person I spoke with your sister?” The first clause needs **se** because one talks **kisi se**, with someone. The second clause identifies that person. More explicit **Jis aurat se maine baat ki…** means “The woman with whom I spoke…” If the identity is sensitive or unclear, use the person’s name once you know it rather than describing them indefinitely.

**Jiski kitaab yahan hai, usko phone kijiye** means “Call the person whose book is here.” **Jiski** uses **ki** because the possessed noun **kitaab** is feminine. In the second clause the person called is an object with **ko**, giving **usko**. You have now combined two earlier ideas: possessive agreement follows the possession, and postpositions follow each clause’s role. Work them out one at a time.

## Jahan…vahan and jab…tab

**Jahan** means where in a relative connection, and **vahan** resumes that location. **Jahan hum rehte the, vahan ek bada darakht tha** means “Where we used to live, there was a big tree.” A more compact English translation is “There was a big tree where we used to live.” The two languages can organize the same information differently without changing the relationship.

**Jahan aap baithna chahein, vahan baithiye** means “Sit wherever you would like.” The place is not identified in advance, so **chahein** is subjunctive. **Jahan hum kal baithe the, vahan chalte hain** means “Let us go where we sat yesterday,” with an actual past place. The relative word itself does not force one tense or mood; the event’s meaning determines the verb.

**Jab** links a time or occasion; **tab** resumes it. **Jab main ghar aaya/aayi, tab sab so rahe the** means “When I came home, everyone was sleeping.” The arrival is one completed event; sleeping supplies the ongoing background. This is the connected version of the two-sentence exercise from Chapter 5. **Jab main chhota tha/chhoti thi, tab hum yahan rehte the** means “When I was little, we lived here.”

For repeated events, use repeated/habitual forms: **Jab bhi baarish hoti hai, hum ghar mein rehte hain**, “Whenever it rains, we stay at home.” **Bhi** adds the “ever” sense in **jab bhi**. For a future instruction, **Jab aap pahunchein, toh phone kijiye**, “When you arrive, please call,” uses a prospective subjunctive. **Toh** often replaces **tab** in natural conditional/instructional connections. Recognize the relationship rather than insisting every pair has only one acceptable second word.

## Jitna…utna: matching amounts and degrees

**Jitna** and **utna** match an amount or degree: **Jitna waqt mile, utna abhyaas kijiye**, “Practice as much as the time you have allows.” In Urdu-focused family conversation, **practice kijiye** or **mashq kijiye** may be more natural than **abhyaas**. The grammar works with any appropriate vocabulary. Choose the word your relatives actually understand; avoiding native script does not require avoiding ordinary borrowings.

With a feminine noun, use **jitni/utni**: **Jitni chai chahiye, utni lijiye**, “Take as much tea as you want.” With plural countable nouns, use **jitne/utne** where appropriate: **Jitne log aaye, utni kursiyan nahin theen**, “There were not as many chairs as people who came.” In that sentence **jitne log** is masculine plural, while **utni kursiyan** is feminine plural. Each member follows its own noun; they do not need to look identical.

For everyday use, start with **Jitna ho sake**, “as much as possible”; **Jaldi se jaldi**, “as soon as possible”; and **Jitna chahiye, utna le lijiye**, “Take as much as you need.” The full pattern helps you recognize the relationship even when a family speaker leaves a noun unstated.

## Ki: what someone said, thought, or knew

**Ki** introduces a content clause: the message someone said, the thought they had, or the fact they knew. **Ammi ne kaha ki khaana tayyar hai** means “Ammi said that the food is ready.” The first clause tells you the source and speech event; the second gives the content. The **ki** here is not the possessive **ki** in **Ammi ki kitaab**. The spelling is the same, but the surrounding structure reveals the job.

Build “I think you are right.” Begin with **Mujhe lagta hai**, “It seems to me/I think.” Add **ki**. Then add the thought **aap theek keh rahe/rahi hain**, “you are speaking correctly/you are right.” The result is **Mujhe lagta hai ki aap theek keh rahe/rahi hain**. In casual conversation **ki** is sometimes omitted: **Mujhe lagta hai aap theek keh rahe/rahi hain**. The full form is useful when learning because it marks the boundary between the opinion frame and its content.

Do not confuse a relative clause with a content clause. **Jo baat aapne kahi, vo sahi hai** means “The thing you said is correct”: it identifies a particular statement and evaluates it. **Aapne kaha ki main sahi hoon** means “You said that I am right”: it reports the content. One begins with a relationship to a noun; the other introduces a message.

## Report speech without automatically copying English tense rules

English often changes “I am tired” to “She said she was tired.” Urdu/Hindi reporting is more flexible, and the intended time and reference must be clear. You can give direct speech: **Sara ne kaha, “Main thak gayi hoon.”** “Sara said, ‘I am tired.’” Or report the content with a changed pronoun: **Sara ne kaha ki vo thak gayi hai/thi**, “Sara said she is/was tired,” according to whether you present the state as current or past.

Do not switch **main** to **vo** and every verb to past mechanically. Ask three questions: Who originally spoke? Who does each pronoun refer to now? Is the reported situation still current, or are you describing a past state? **Ali ne kaha ki vo kal aayega** commonly reports that Ali said he would come tomorrow, but **kal** can be ambiguous when the reporting day has changed. Use **agle din**, “the next day,” or the named day when needed.

For instructions, **Ammi ne kaha ki darwaza band kar do** can report “Ammi said to close the door,” with the instruction retained. A more explicit alternative is **Ammi ne darwaza band karne ko kaha**, “Ammi asked/told [someone] to close the door.” The infinitive changes to **karne** before **ko**. Include the recipient if it matters: **Ammi ne mujhe darwaza band karne ko kaha**. That prevents a group from wondering who is responsible.

When reporting a question, retain the question relationship: **Unhone poochha ki aap kab aayenge**, “They asked when you will come.” **Poochhna** is to ask a question; **kehna** is to say/tell. **Maine unse poochha** means “I asked them.” The person asked can be marked with **se**. Use the verb and its relationships as a chunk instead of translating English “ask” with one postposition in every situation.

## Kar and karke: one person, a sequence of actions

**Khaana kha kar main nikla/nikli** means “After eating, I left.” **Haath dho kar baithiye** means “Wash your hands and sit down,” respectfully. The connecting form is usually the verb stem plus **kar** or **karke**. The main verb carries the tense and agreement: **nikla/nikli**, left; **baithiye**, please sit. The earlier action is understood from the connection.

Work through “I called Ammi and then went to sleep.” First write **Maine Ammi ko phone kiya. Phir main so gaya/gayi.** To connect the actions, use **Ammi ko phone karke main so gaya/gayi**. The **phone karke** phrase does not itself carry the **ne** perfective pattern; the final **so gaya/gayi** is intransitive and follows the subject. Do not keep **maine** from the first standalone sentence automatically after changing the structure.

The understood actor of a **kar/karke** phrase is normally the same as the main clause’s actor. If you mean “After Ammi cooked, I ate,” avoid a phrase that sounds as though you did both actions. Say **Ammi ne khaana banaya. Uske baad maine khaya**, or **Jab Ammi ne khaana bana liya, tab maine khaya**. Clear separate clauses are preferable to an elegant-looking connection with the wrong actor.

Some combinations describe manner or reason as well as sequence: **Muskura kar unhone jawab diya**, “They answered with a smile”; **Aapse baat karke achha laga**, “It was nice talking to you.” The second contains an experience evaluation with an implicit experiencer, not a simple pair of actions by the same person. Learn such common expressions as complete constructions while using the same-actor guideline for ordinary action sequences.

## Before, after, while, since, and until

Use an oblique infinitive before postpositions: **jaane se pehle**, before going; **aane ke baad**, after coming; **khaane ke liye**, for eating/to eat. **Main nikalne se pehle phone karunga/karungi** means “I will call before leaving.” **Ghar aane ke baad main aaraam karta/karti hoon** means “After coming home I rest.” The **ne** ending in **aane** is part of the oblique infinitive; it is not the separate perfective subject marker **ne**.

**Aate waqt** means while coming/on the way here; **jaate waqt**, while going/on the way away; **khaate waqt**, while eating. These use a participial form plus **waqt**, time. **Aate waqt doodh le aaiye**, “Please bring milk on your way.” Keep the direction clear: **aana** is coming toward the relevant place, **jaana** going away. A remote caller may frame direction around the family home rather than their own physical location, so listen to the shared point of view.

**Se** can mark a starting point: **subah se**, since morning; **Hyderabad se**, from Hyderabad. **Tak** marks an endpoint: **shaam tak**, until/by evening; **ghar tak**, as far as home. **Main subah se yahan hoon** means “I have been here since morning.” **Main shaam tak yahan hoon** means “I am here until evening.” **Main shaam tak kaam khatam karunga/karungi** means “I will finish the work by evening.” The same endpoint marker can translate as “until” with a continuing state and “by” with a completion.

## Contrast, concession, and keeping a turn

**Lekin** and **magar** mean but. **Phir bhi** means even so/still. **Halanki** introduces although: **Halanki baarish ho rahi thi, phir bhi hum gaye**, “Although it was raining, we went anyway.” You need not use both parts every time. **Baarish ho rahi thi, lekin hum phir bhi gaye** is also clear. First say the actual contrast; do not collect connectors merely to sound advanced.

**Ek taraf…doosri taraf…** means on one hand…on the other hand. **Ek taraf kaam zaroori hai; doosri taraf ghar walon ke liye waqt bhi chahiye**, “On one hand work matters; on the other, time for family is also needed.” This gives you a structure for a balanced opinion. Chapter 9 expands discussion and reasons. Here practice following the connection without losing the original point.

Useful turn signals include **Pehle main yeh bata doon**, “First let me explain this”; **Mera matlab yeh hai ki…**, “What I mean is…”; **Ek misaal deta/deti hoon**, “Let me give an example”; and **Toh baat yeh hai…**, “So the point is…”. Use a pause after the frame. A connector helps only if the next clause actually fulfills the expectation it creates.

## Three connected conversations

### Finding the right relative

**Learner:** Jo kal aaye the, vo aapke chacha hain? — Is the person who came yesterday your paternal uncle?

**Cousin:** Nahin, vo mere mamu hain. Chacha vo hain jo darwaze ke paas baithe hain. — No, that is my maternal uncle. My paternal uncle is the one sitting near the door.

**Learner:** Achha. Jinki kitaab mere paas hai, vohi? — Oh. The same person whose book I have?

**Cousin:** Hau, vohi. Unko kitaab kal chahiye. — Yes, that very person. He needs the book tomorrow.

**Learner:** Main ghar jaane se pehle de doonga/doongi. — I will give it back before going home.

The dialogue solves an identification problem. **Jinki** uses a respectful/plural relative base and feminine possession **kitaab**. **Vohi** combines **vo** with emphasis **hi**, “that very one.” Avoid using a kinship label before you know which side of the family the person belongs to.

### Retelling a small delay

**Relative:** Tum der se kaiku aaye/aayi? — Why did you arrive late? Local familiar question.

**Learner:** Jab main nikla/nikli, tab baarish ho rahi thi. Jis raaste se aaya/aayi, vahan traffic tha. — When I left, it was raining. There was traffic on the route I took.

**Relative:** Phone kar dete/deti. — You could have called.

**Learner:** Ji, mujhe pehle phone karna chahiye tha. Pahunch kar message bheja. — Yes, I should have called earlier. After arriving, I sent a message.

**Relative:** Theek hai, ab baitho. — All right, sit now.

The explanation supplies sequence and place, then acknowledges a missed action. A long story should not hide the practical point. **Kaiku** can be replaced with shared **kyun** without changing the explanation’s grammar.

### Reporting a plan accurately

**Learner:** Ammi ne kaha ki vo jumme ko aayengi. — Ammi said she will come on Friday.

**Sibling:** Subah ya shaam? — Morning or evening?

**Learner:** Unhone kaha ki shaam tak pahunch jayengi. Aane se pehle phone karengi. — She said she will arrive by evening. She will call before coming.

**Sibling:** Toh main khaana bana loon? — Then shall I make the food?

**Learner:** Hau, lekin pehle unse poochh lete hain ki unko kya chahiye. — Yes, but let us first ask her what she would like.

Notice that **shaam tak** means by evening because the event is arrival. The report names the day instead of relying on **kal**. The final question avoids inventing a food preference on someone else’s behalf.

## Exercises and answers

### A. Connect two ideas

1. **Mere paas chai hai. Aapko vo chai chahiye.**
2. **Main ghar aaya/aayi. Sab so rahe the.**
3. **Hum yahan rehte the. Yahan ek dukaan thi.**
4. **Maine ek aadmi se baat ki. Vo mere chacha hain.**
5. **Main haath dhoonga/dhoongi. Phir khaana khaunga/khaungi.**
6. **Ammi ne kaha: “Khaana tayyar hai.”** Report the message.

**Possible answers:** 1. **Jo chai mere paas hai, aapko vo chahiye.** 2. **Jab main ghar aaya/aayi, tab sab so rahe the.** 3. **Jahan hum rehte the, vahan ek dukaan thi.** 4. **Jis aadmi se maine baat ki, vo mere chacha hain.** 5. **Main haath dho kar khaana khaunga/khaungi.** 6. **Ammi ne kaha ki khaana tayyar hai.** Each connection preserves the relationship; alternative orders may be natural if the reference stays clear.

### B. Choose the relationship

Fill the blank: **___ aap pahunchein, phone kijiye** for “When you arrive”; **Jis aadmi ___ maine dekha…**; **Aane ___ baad**; **Subah ___ ab ___**, “from morning until now”; **Mujhe lagta hai ___ vo ghar par hain**; and **___ kitaab yahan hai, usko phone kijiye**, “Call the person whose book is here.”

**Answers:** **Jab**; **ko**, because the identified person is the direct object seen; **ke**, in **aane ke baad**; **se…tak**; **ki**, the content-clause marker; **jiski**, because **kitaab** is feminine. For the first, **agar** would change “when” into “if.” The difference can matter in a plan.

### C. Repair ambiguous reporting

On Monday Ali said **Main kal aaunga**, “I will come tomorrow.” On Wednesday you report his message. Why is **Ali ne kaha ki vo kal aayega** risky? Because **kal** may now be heard as Thursday rather than the Tuesday Ali meant. Say **Ali ne kaha tha ki vo mangal ko aayega**, or **…agle din aayega**, with enough context to identify the reference day. Named days prevent a grammar exercise from becoming a real scheduling mistake.

### D. Translation practice

Translate: “The book I read was good”; “Call before leaving”; “I have been here since morning”; “I will finish by evening”; “They asked when you will come”; “Although it was raining, we went”; “Take as much tea as you want”; and “After calling Ammi, I went to sleep.”

**Possible answers:** **Jo kitaab maine padhi, vo achhi thi.** **Nikalne se pehle phone kijiye.** **Main subah se yahan hoon.** **Main shaam tak kaam khatam karunga/karungi.** **Unhone poochha ki aap kab aayenge/aayengi.** **Halanki baarish ho rahi thi, phir bhi hum gaye.** **Jitni chai chahiye, utni lijiye.** **Ammi ko phone karke main so gaya/gayi.** In the last sentence **main**, not **maine**, belongs with the final intransitive sleep expression.

## Listening, speaking, and the 90-hour plan

Listen to a two-minute family story with its transcript hidden. First draw five boxes for events and use arrows for sequence; no artistic drawing is needed. On a second pass mark background, reason, contrast, and reported information with short English labels. Then type four Roman sentences you heard and translate them. Finally compare the transcript. A connector you missed may explain why your event order was wrong. A different Roman spelling does not automatically mean you misheard the spoken relationship.

For speaking, choose an everyday story with five events, one earlier background fact, one person to identify, one quoted message, and one consequence. Tell it in three minutes. A partner interrupts with **Kaun?**, **Kab?**, and **Kisne kaha?** Your job is to clarify the reference and return to the story. Repeat with fewer connectors if the first version became tangled. Clear pauses and a well-placed name can be more effective than one very long sentence.

| Work block | Hours | Concrete evidence |
|---|---:|---|
| Read and rebuild clause relationships | 12 | Twenty paired short/connected examples |
| Listening, event mapping, and dictation | 16 | Twelve clips and maps |
| Translation and reference repair | 12 | Eighty reviewed sentences |
| Story recording and shadowing | 12 | Twelve recordings with revised references |
| Family or partner story exchanges | 18 | Six conversations with interruptions |
| Connector and vocabulary retrieval | 8 | Fifty phrases used in meaningful relationships |
| Family story project | 8 | Approved interview and original retelling |
| Exit task and targeted repair | 4 | Fresh narrative and message relay |
| **Total** | **90** | |

For the project, ask permission to collect one ordinary family story. Record who said each piece of information, what is certain, and what is remembered approximately. Retell it to another willing relative without exposing private details. Ask whether the sequence and references were clear. Do not claim exact words if you paraphrased. Reporting responsibly is both a language skill and a way of honoring a family’s stories.

At exit, relay a practical message with all critical details intact; identify an unfamiliar person or object using a relative clause; tell a three-minute story with clear event order and background; and explain one reason and one contrast. A partner should recover the same sequence and participants without seeing your notes. This is evidence of course progress rather than a formal CEFR award. The next stage uses these connections to sustain opinions, respond to disagreement, and discuss more abstract family and community topics.

### Source note

The University of Texas at Austin’s [relative–correlative teaching notes](https://hindi.la.utexas.edu/resources/nursery-rhymes/) were inspected specifically for the **jo/vo**, **jahan/vahan**, and **jab/tab** framework and for sequential verb connections. The [Hindi Grammar overview](https://hindi.la.utexas.edu/resources/grammar/) was checked for verb-combination terminology. The examples and family activities are original; the source page contains unrelated songs that are not reproduced here.


# A voice of your own

Use the foundation to develop ideas, interpret nuance, and handle unexpected turns. Local speech becomes something to understand in context, alongside increasingly independent conversation.


<a id="chapter-09"></a>

# 09. Opinions, conditions, and negotiation

**Time: 90 hours. Course total after this chapter: 725 hours. Target: developing B2 spoken interaction.**

You can tell your cousin what happened yesterday. Now the family wants to choose between visiting someone, staying home, and going out. Nobody has the same schedule. Conversation becomes interesting when you can say what would work, explain why, acknowledge another person's reason, and change your proposal. This chapter turns familiar grammar into those social actions.

The goal is not winning arguments. It is making your meaning clear while preserving the relationship. Begin with the shared conversational forms. Add the Hyderabadi choices your relatives actually use. A respectful sentence containing ordinary Urdu/Hindi can sound entirely at home in Hyderabad; a string of local catchphrases does not by itself make a conversation natural.

## From an observation to an opinion

Consider **Yeh jagah door hai**: this place is far away. It presents distance as a description. If you say **Mere khayaal mein yeh jagah door hai**, you frame the same description as your view: in my opinion, this place is far away. The added phrase gives other people room to disagree. **Mujhe lagta hai ki yeh jagah door hai** means I think this place is far away. Here *mujhe lagta hai* is a useful fixed frame, literally something like “it seems to me.” It does not mean that you physically stick to something.

Build a fuller view in steps:

1. **Yeh jagah achchhi hai.** This place is good.
2. **Mujhe lagta hai ki yeh jagah achchhi hai.** I think this place is good.
3. **Mujhe lagta hai ki yeh jagah achchhi hai kyunki yahaan bachchon ke liye jagah hai.** I think this place is good because there is space for the children here.
4. **Lekin agar baarish ho to mushkil hogi.** But if it rains, there will be a problem.

The third step supplies a reason that your listener can evaluate. The fourth recognises a limitation. You now sound more engaged because you are responding to a real choice, rather than reciting a preference. A useful first practice is to give one opinion, one reason, and one limitation about somewhere familiar.

**Mere hisaab se** means according to my assessment or as I see it. **Meri raay mein** also introduces an opinion and can sound more deliberate. **Mujhe lagta hai** is a good everyday default. Do not introduce every sentence with an opinion marker. Once the viewpoint is clear, continue with the reason and example. Too many disclaimers make the main point difficult to hear.

When you are unsure, adjust certainty explicitly. **Shaayad woh masroof hain** means perhaps they are busy. **Ho sakta hai ki woh masroof hon** means it may be that they are busy. In the second sentence, *hon* is the respectful/plural subjunctive of *hona*. The uncertainty is in the situation, not in whether you respect the person.

## What an if-sentence does

A condition connects two situations: the situation you are considering and the consequence that follows. In **Agar waqt hai to chai pi lete hain**, “if there is time, let's have some tea,” *agar waqt hai* supplies the condition and *to chai pi lete hain* supplies the proposal. English often drops “then”; Urdu/Hindi often keeps *to*. Listen for the two halves rather than translating each word as it arrives.

For an open possibility, begin with a condition that could genuinely hold:

| Condition | Result | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Agar aap free hain | to hum abhi baat kar sakte hain | If you are free, we can talk now. |
| Agar kal baarish hui | to hum ghar par rahenge | If it rains tomorrow, we will stay home. |
| Agar aap chaahen | to hum jagah badal sakte hain | If you wish, we can change the place. |
| Agar der ho rahi hai | to pehle phone kar dijiye | If it is getting late, please call first. |

The different verb forms are meaningful. *Hain* asks about a present state. *Hui* presents the rain as an event that would have occurred by the relevant future decision point; a perfective form does not force the whole conversation into the past. *Chaahen* leaves a respectful person's preference open. *Ho rahi hai* describes a developing situation. There is no requirement that every *agar* clause use exactly one tense.

Work through the second row carefully. Start with **Kal baarish hogi**, it will rain tomorrow. You do not actually know that it will. Change the assertion into an open condition: **Agar kal baarish hui**. Then say what you will do: **hum ghar par rahenge**. Join them with *to*. Your future commitment is staying home under the stated condition; you have not predicted certain rain.

In listening, distinguish *agar* from *magar*: if versus but. Their sound is similar enough to matter in a fast conversation. Put both into short recordings: **Agar waqt hai to aaiye** and **Waqt hai, magar main thaka hua hoon**. The first invites someone conditionally; the second explains an obstacle despite having time.

## Imagining a different situation

Suppose your cousin asks what you would do if you lived closer. **Agar main paas rehta to roz milta** means if I lived nearby, I would meet you every day, with a male speaker. A female speaker says **Agar main paas rehti to roz milti**. The habitual-looking forms can describe an imagined alternative when the context makes that reading clear. They are not statements about your current daily routine.

Now use *hona* to imagine possession. **Agar mere paas gaari hoti to main aapko chhor deta** means if I had a car, I would drop you off, with a male speaker. *Gaari* is feminine, so the condition contains *hoti*. The result *chhor deta* reflects the male subject. A female speaker says *chhor deti*. The two agreement decisions belong to different clauses. Do not copy the final vowel from one clause into the other.

**Agar main aapki jagah hota to pehle phone karta** means if I were in your place, I would call first, said by a male speaker. A female speaker uses *hoti* and *karti*. This is an advice frame, but it can still sound intrusive. Ask **Kya main ek mashwara doon?**—may I offer a suggestion?—before giving advice about a sensitive family decision.

For an unrealised past, anchor the time clearly: **Agar aapne kal bataya hota to main aa jaata**: if you had told me yesterday, I would have come, with a male speaker. A female speaker says *aa jaati*. First identify the action that did not happen, *aapne bataya hota*. Then identify its lost consequence, *main aa jaata*. The sentence often implies that the person did not tell you. That implication can sound blaming even when the grammar is correct.

Compare a gentler practical version: **Kal pata chal jaata to achchha hota. Ab hum kya kar sakte hain?** It would have been good to find out yesterday. What can we do now? This does not erase disappointment, but it moves the conversation toward a solution. Practice both the grammatical form and its interpersonal effect.

## Wishes, proposals, and respectful uncertainty

You met subjunctive forms with plans and wishes in chapter six. Here they help people make a decision together. **Hum kahaan milen?** means where should we meet? **Kya main pehle unse baat karoon?** means shall I speak to them first? **Aap bataayen** means please tell me, in a respectful invitation to contribute. The form presents an action for consideration instead of announcing that it definitely happens.

Regular forms are easiest to hear as small families: *main karoon*, *hum karen*, *aap karen*, *tum karo*, *woh kare*. High-frequency irregular forms deserve practice as phrases: *main jaaoon*, *aap jaayen*, *main loon*, *aap len*, *main doon*, *aap den*, *main hoon* in the subjunctive context and *aap hon*. Roman spellings are a reading aid; let a trusted speaker model nasal vowels and syllable boundaries.

**Aap chaahen to hum jaldi nikal sakte hain** means if you wish, we can leave early. **Main chaahta hoon ki sab aaraam se baithen** means I want everyone to sit comfortably, with a male speaker. **Main chaahti hoon** is the female-speaker version. The first *chaahen* agrees with respectful *aap*; the second clause *baithen* refers to *sab*. In a longer sentence, identify who performs each action before choosing its ending.

**Kaash hum paas rehte** means I wish we lived nearby. It expresses a wish, not a logistical proposal. A listener may respond with sympathy rather than a plan. By contrast, **Kyun na hum agle hafte milen?**—why don't we meet next week?—invites a concrete choice. Use a wish when you want to share a feeling and a proposal when you want to agree on action.

## Comparing without losing the point

Comparison provides reasons for choosing. **Yeh raasta us raaste se chhota hai** means this route is shorter than that route. *Se* marks the standard of comparison. The adjective is an ordinary adjective; Urdu/Hindi does not add an English-style “-er.” Before *se*, masculine *raasta* becomes oblique *raaste*. The adjective *chhota* agrees with the subject route, not the word after *se*.

To compare cost, **Yeh jagah us jagah se sasti hai**: this place is cheaper than that place. *Jagah* is feminine, so *sasti* is feminine. For relative preference, **Mujhe pehla tareeqa zyaada munasib lagta hai** means the first method seems more suitable to me. *Munasib* does not change its ending. **Sabse qareeb** means nearest; **utna door nahin** means not that far; **dono taqreeban baraabar hain** means both are approximately equal.

State the dimension you actually mean. “Better” may mean cheaper, quieter, more accessible, or more comfortable for an older relative. **Yahaan shor kam hai, isliye baat karna aasaan hoga**—there is less noise here, so conversation will be easier—is more useful than insisting **Yahi behtar hai**, this is the better one. Reasons make negotiation possible because people can change a proposal while keeping the shared goal.

Equality uses *jitna ... utna* patterns already related to your relative-correlative work: **Jitna waqt aapko chahiye, utna le lijiye** means take as much time as you need. **Yeh utna mushkil nahin hai jitna lagta hai** means this is not as difficult as it seems. Begin with one familiar pattern and avoid building several nested comparisons before the listener knows the topic.

## Disagreement that keeps a conversation open

Disagreeing respectfully has three parts: show what you understood, identify the point you see differently, and offer a reason or alternative. **Aapki baat samajh mein aa rahi hai, lekin mujhe lagta hai ki thoda aur waqt chahiye** means I understand your point, but I think we need a little more time. Agreement with one reason does not commit you to the whole conclusion.

Useful frames include **Is baat se main sehmat hoon** (I agree with this point), **Main poori tarah sehmat nahin hoon** (I do not completely agree), **Mera matlab yeh nahin tha** (that was not what I meant), and **Ho sakta hai main galat samajh raha hoon** (perhaps I am misunderstanding, male speaker; *rahi hoon* for female). Say these at a measured pace. A soft phrase spoken with sarcasm is not automatically polite.

For concession, begin **Halaanki**: **Halaanki jagah door hai, phir bhi jaana achchha rahega** means although the place is far away, going would still be good. **Phir bhi** preserves the second point despite the first. **Lekin** introduces contrast. **Isliye** introduces a consequence. **Kyunki** introduces a reason. If a paragraph becomes difficult, reduce it to two clauses and choose the connector whose job matches your meaning.

Local **Kaiku?** asks why, but a bare “why?” can sound challenging in any dialect. When a relative's preference surprises you, try **Achchha, aap aisa kaiku keh rahe hain?**—oh, why do you say that?—if that wording fits your household. Shared **Aap aisa kyun keh rahe hain?** is equally useful. The respectful verb ending matters more than inserting a local word.

## Dialogue: choosing a family outing

**Sara:** Mere khayaal mein hum subah niklen to behtar hoga. — In my opinion, it would be better if we left in the morning.

**Imran:** Subah kaiku? Bachche der se uthte hain. — Why in the morning? The children get up late.

**Sara:** Hau, woh baat bhi theek hai. Lekin dopahar mein garmi zyaada hogi. — Yes, that is a fair point too. But it will be hotter in the afternoon.

**Imran:** Agar hum das baje niklen to kaisa rahega? — How would it be if we left at ten?

**Sara:** Theek rahega, basharte ki sab waqt par taiyaar hon. — That will work, provided everyone is ready on time.

**Imran:** Main sabko aaj hi bata deta hoon. — I'll tell everyone today itself.

*Basharte ki* means provided that; it is useful but more deliberate than ordinary *agar*. Sara accepts Imran's reason before returning to the heat. They change the time rather than arguing about who is right. Replay the dialogue and notice how the question intonation invites cooperation. In your own version, substitute rain, a working relative, or a long drive as the constraint.

## Dialogue: an older relative offers advice

**Khala:** Agar tum thoda jaldi so jaao to subah aasaani hogi. — If you go to sleep a little earlier, the morning will be easier.

**Nida:** Aap theek keh rahi hain. Masla yeh hai ki mera kaam der se khatam hota hai. — You are right. The problem is that my work finishes late.

**Khala:** To kya kaam ka waqt badal sakta hai? — So can the work schedule change?

**Nida:** Filhaal nahin. Lekin agar main pehle se khaana taiyaar kar loon to waqt bach sakta hai. — Not at present. But if I prepare food in advance, time could be saved.

**Khala:** Hau, yeh achchha khayaal hai. — Yes, that is a good idea.

Nida does not have to accept an impossible solution to show respect. She acknowledges the intention, explains the constraint, and proposes another way. In *kar loon*, *lena* suggests doing the preparation for her own practical benefit. Chapter ten develops these small shades of compound verbs.

## Dialogue: disagreeing about a general topic

**Aamir:** Mere hisaab se bachchon ko ghar mein sirf Urdu bolni chahiye. — As I see it, children should speak only Urdu at home.

**Farah:** Main samajh rahi hoon ki aap zubaan bachana chaahte hain. Main bhi yahi chaahti hoon. — I understand that you want to preserve the language. I want the same thing.

**Aamir:** Phir aap kis baat se sehmat nahin hain? — Then which point do you disagree with?

**Farah:** “Sirf” waali baat se. Agar unko sawaal poochhne ki azaadi ho to woh zyaada bolenge. — With the “only” part. If they have freedom to ask questions, they will speak more.

**Aamir:** Aapka matlab hai ki English bhi chal sakti hai? — You mean English can be acceptable too?

**Farah:** Zaroorat par, hau. Phir hum wohi baat Urdu mein keh sakte hain. — When necessary, yes. Then we can say the same thing in Urdu.

This is an original practice discussion, not a universal claim about child language policy. Its language skill is separating a shared goal from a disputed method. Retell both positions fairly before stating your own. That is harder and more useful than memorising one side's speech.

## Written workshop with explained answers

Translate before opening your notes. Accept a natural equivalent if it preserves time, person, politeness, and the relationship between the two clauses.

1. If you have time, please call me.
2. If it rains tomorrow, we will stay home.
3. If I lived nearby, I would come every day. Give both speaker genders.
4. If you had told me yesterday, I would have helped. Male speaker.
5. I understand your point, but I do not completely agree.
6. Although the place is far, it is quieter.
7. This route is shorter than that route.
8. Shall we meet a little earlier?
9. I wish we lived in the same city.
10. Why do you think that? Address an elder respectfully.

**Answer key and reasoning.** (1) **Agar aapke paas waqt ho to mujhe phone kar dijiye.** *Ho* leaves the availability open; *hai* is also possible for a present fact being checked. (2) **Agar kal baarish hui to hum ghar par rahenge.** The result is future despite the condition's perfective form. (3) **Agar main paas rehta/rehti to roz aata/aati.** Both agreeing forms change with the speaker. (4) **Agar aapne kal bataya hota to main madad karta.** A more explicitly completed result is *main madad kar deta*. (5) **Aapki baat samajh mein aa rahi hai, lekin main poori tarah sehmat nahin hoon.** Understanding is separate from agreeing. (6) **Halaanki jagah door hai, wahaan shor kam hai.** This expresses the intended practical contrast without mechanically translating “quieter.” (7) **Yeh raasta us raaste se chhota hai.** *Us raaste* is oblique before *se*. (8) **Kya hum thoda pehle milen?** A proposal uses the subjunctive. (9) **Kaash hum ek hi shehar mein rehte.** *Hi* focuses “the same/one.” (10) **Aapko aisa kyun lagta hai?** In a household using the local form, *kaiku* can replace *kyun*.

Now repair these sentences: **Agar mere paas gaari hota**; **Yeh jagah us jagah se sasta hai**; **Main aapki baat samajhta hoon isliye main sehmat nahin hoon** when you mean “but”; **Agar aapne bataya to main kal aa jaata** for a missed opportunity yesterday. The repairs are *gaari hoti*, *sasti*, *lekin*, and **Agar aapne kal bataya hota to main aa jaata**. The errors concern agreement, connector meaning, and the unrealised past—not the presence or absence of a fashionable phrase.

## Listening, speaking, and family transfer

For listening, ask a willing relative to record six short decisions. Three should be real possibilities and three imagined alternatives. On the first pass, identify the topic only. On the second, write the condition and result separately in Roman script. On the third, translate the relationship into English. Check whether you heard a prediction, a proposal, advice, or regret. A synthetic voice can help rehearse words but cannot certify local rhythm or intent.

For speaking, record a two-minute comparison of two familiar options. State the shared goal, two advantages, one limitation, a condition, and a proposed compromise. Listen once without reading your script. If you cannot follow the argument, shorten sentences and signpost the turns with *lekin*, *isliye*, and *phir bhi*. Then answer three unprepared follow-up questions from another person.

The family mission is a small real decision: when to call, what to cook, or where to meet. Ask permission to practise for ten minutes. Use a respectful clarification when you miss a point: **Aap keh rahe hain ki ...?**—are you saying that ...? Note one local expression exactly as your relative says it and ask when it sounds natural. Do not turn the conversation into correcting your relative's grammar.

## The 90-hour practice plan

| Work | Hours | Concrete evidence |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| Read, annotate, and rebuild worked grammar | 12 | Twenty condition/result pairs with explanations |
| Listening to conditions, reasons, and stance | 16 | Twelve short transcripts and corrected summaries |
| Sentence transformation and translation | 14 | Eighty checked transformations across time and gender |
| Guided discussions and role-play | 18 | Six recordings with a changed constraint each time |
| Vocabulary retrieval and spaced review | 10 | Opinion/negotiation words used in new sentences |
| Family conversations and local-form observation | 14 | Seven short conversations and a usage notebook |
| Exit task, feedback, and targeted repair | 6 | An unseen decision task repeated after feedback |
| **Total** | **90** | **725 cumulative hours** |

An hour means engaged work with breaks outside the count. Replaying a recording without attending to meaning does not automatically become listening practice. Spread this chapter over several weeks and revisit prior grammar during each task. If your partner changes one condition, respond to the change instead of delivering your prepared speech.

## Exit performance

Complete a fifteen-minute conversation about a family decision without reading. Sustain a view with two reasons, represent the other person's view fairly, compare alternatives, use one real condition and one hypothetical condition, and agree on a concrete next step. Ask for clarification when needed. Rate clarity, grammatical control, listening response, turn-taking, and appropriateness from 0 to 4; a 3 means mostly independent, with occasional repair that succeeds.

For readiness, seek at least 3 in clarity, listening response, and appropriateness, and no 0 in any category on two different topics. These are course practice criteria, not an official language certificate. If a single memorised topic succeeds but an unfamiliar one collapses, keep the same structures and vary the situation. Chapter ten adds shades of completion, benefit, emphasis, and responsibility so that your next discussion sounds more precise.

## Source checks

The conditional and subjunctive terminology was checked against the University of Texas Hindi Urdu Flagship's [conditional-sentence materials](https://hindiurduflagship.org/grammar-conditional-sentences/) and Rupert Snell's [Hindi-Urdu Verb guide](https://hindiurduflagship.org/wp/wp-content/uploads/2015/05/HU-Verb.pdf). The explanations and dialogues here are original Roman-script teaching material. The programme's B2 direction draws on the [Council of Europe CEFR descriptors](https://www.coe.int/en/web/common-european-framework-reference-languages/cefr-descriptors); this chapter's rubric is an informal adaptation for spoken family interaction.


<a id="chapter-10"></a>

# 10. Nuance and Hyderabadi conversation

**Time: 90 hours. Course total after this chapter: 815 hours. Target: flexible B2-oriented conversation.**

Your aunt says **Chai pi lijiye**. You know *chai*, tea, and *peena*, to drink. Why has *lena*, to take, appeared? Later someone says **Main bhool gaya**, I forgot. Nobody physically went anywhere. These everyday combinations are a major part of natural conversation. Their second verbs organise how the action feels: achieved, completed, undertaken for someone, or beyond the speaker's control.

This chapter teaches those meanings alongside emphasis and responsibility. It also makes an important distinction: common conversational grammar is shared widely, while particular Hyderabadi forms vary across households and situations. You will learn to notice what your family says without treating one family's speech as the only authentic version.

## A second verb changes the viewpoint

Begin with **Main kaam karta hoon**, I work/do work, for a male speaker. Now consider **Main yeh kaam kar leta hoon**, I'll do this task or I can get this done, depending on context. The first verb appears as the bare stem *kar*. The second verb, *lena*, carries the changing ending: *leta*, *leti*, *liya*, *loonga*. Its literal meaning “take” is no longer the main event.

Build the past sentence in stages. The action is **chai banaana**, making tea. The actor is *Nida*. A plain completed report is **Nida ne chai banaai**, Nida made tea. To present the tea-making as something she got done for herself or her side of the situation, say **Nida ne chai bana li**. *Chai* is feminine, and the final perfective *li* reflects that. Do not say *banaai li*: the first verb is a stem in this construction.

The self-benefit meaning is a tendency, not a rule that every sentence must literally benefit its grammatical subject. **Pehle sun lijiye** means please hear me out first. The respectful request invites the listener to complete the listening before deciding. **Main dekh leta hoon** means I'll take a look; **Aap baith jaaiye** means please sit down. English translation changes with the activity and situation.

Do not add a second verb to every action. **Main roz chai peeta hoon** is the clear way to state a habit. A compound may be natural in a particular exchange, but random addition creates strange emphasis. Learn whole combinations through meaning and context: *dekh lena*, *sun lena*, *likh lena*, *kar dena*, *bata dena*, *bhool jaana*, *so jaana*. Then vary person and time.

## Lena and dena in small acts of cooperation

Compare **Yeh number likhiye**, write this number, with **Yeh number likh lijiye**, note this number down. The second often suggests keeping it for your own use. Compare **Darwaaza band kijiye**, close the door, with **Darwaaza band kar dijiye**, please close the door for me/us. *Dena* can present an action as directed outward or as a completed service. It does not guarantee kindness: context and tone still determine how a demand sounds.

**Main aapko pata bhej deta hoon** means I'll send you the address, male speaker. A female speaker says *bhej deti hoon*. Here a present-looking compound often makes an immediate offer. **Maine aapko pata bhej diya** means I sent you the address. The presence of *ne* in this completed transitive sentence remains important. Changing the second verb changes viewpoint, not every other rule of the sentence.

For a feminine object, **Maine tasveer bhej di** means I sent the picture. For a masculine object, **Maine paighaam bhej diya** means I sent the message. When the object has *ko*, the shared-standard pattern normally uses the default masculine singular: **Maine unko dekh liya**, I saw them. You are reusing the agreement logic from chapter five with a longer verb phrase.

Now consider requests as relationships. **Zara bata dijiye** is a courteous request for information. **Bata do** is familiar. **Bata** is intimate or abrupt. A local word does not cancel the difference. With an older relative, retain **aap** and a respectful form unless that person explicitly invites another pattern. In your household notebook, record both the verb and who said it to whom.

## Jaana: completion, change, and lexical combinations

**Bachcha so gaya** means the child fell asleep. **Main thak gaya hoon**, for a male speaker, means I have become tired; a female speaker says **Main thak gayi hoon**. **Woh bhool gaye** can mean he respectfully forgot or they forgot. These combinations present a resulting state or completed transition. They are learned expressions, not commands to translate *gaya* as “went.”

The familiar compound **aa jaana** can mean to come over or arrive, depending on context. **Kal aa jaaiye** is please come tomorrow. **Mujhe samajh aa gaya** means I understood; here *samajh aana* is the main expression and *gaya* presents the understanding as achieved. **Mujhe baat samajh aa gayi** has feminine *baat*, the matter, so the final form changes. Reading only the English pronoun “I” will lead you to the wrong agreement.

Compare three stages: **Main samajh raha hoon**, I am understanding/following, male speaker; **Mujhe samajh aa raha hai**, it is becoming clear to me; **Mujhe samajh aa gaya**, I got it. A listener might use any of them naturally, but the viewpoint differs. Practice with an actual explanation: ask a cousin to explain a recipe or route, then choose the phrase that describes your understanding accurately.

In negative sentences, do not mechanically preserve every affirmative compound. **Maine nahin dekha**, I did not see it, is a useful neutral default. Compounds can occur in negatives when the meaning warrants them, but the choice is idiomatic. Learn **Main bhool nahin gaya**, I have not forgotten, as a meaningful denial rather than inventing a ban on all compounds with *nahin*.

## Beginning, continuing, and already finishing

Aspect describes the internal shape of an action: beginning, continuing, finishing, or happening repeatedly. You already know progressive **kar raha hai**. To say something begins, use an oblique infinitive followed by *lagna*: **Bachcha rone laga**, the child began to cry. *Rona* becomes *rone*. A girl **rone lagi**; children **rone lage**. The final verb agrees with the person beginning the activity.

**Main Urdu samajhne laga hoon** means I have begun to understand Urdu, male speaker; in this basic pattern, the feminine counterpart of *laga* is *lagi*. A female speaker says **Main Urdu samajhne lagi hoon**. Compare **Mujhe Urdu aasaan lagti hai**, Urdu seems easy to me. The word *lagna* has several constructions. Identify its neighbours: infinitive *samajhne* marks beginning, while adjective *aasaan* marks a judgement.

For continuity, **Woh bolta raha** means he kept talking. **Woh bolti rahi** means she kept talking. The habitual participle carries the repeated/continuing activity, and *rehna* carries the persistence. **Aap koshish karte rahiye** means keep trying, respectfully. Contrast **Aap koshish kar rahe hain**, you are trying, with **Aap koshish karte rahiye**, keep trying. One describes a current action; the other encourages its continuation.

**Main khaana kha chuka hoon** means I have already eaten, male speaker; **kha chuki hoon** for a female speaker. *Chukna* presents the action as already completed. In this pattern the construction is intransitive: **Main**, not **maine**, even though *khaana* alone can be transitive. For a neutral negative, say **Maine abhi tak khaana nahin khaaya**, I have not eaten yet. Avoid building a negative by copying the affirmative *chukna* mechanically.

## Describing an event without naming its agent

You may know that a meeting was cancelled without knowing who cancelled it. A passive sentence puts the affected thing in the foreground. Start with **Kisi ne meeting radd ki**, someone cancelled the meeting. To focus on the meeting, say **Meeting radd ki gayi**, the meeting was cancelled. *Meeting* is treated as feminine in this usage, so *ki gayi* is feminine.

A productive pattern is perfective participle plus *jaana*: **Yeh khaana yahaan banaaya jaata hai**, this food is made here. First choose the affected thing, masculine *khaana*. Use *banaaya*, made, then habitual *jaata hai*, is regularly done. For feminine *chai*, **Yeh chai yahaan banaai jaati hai**, this tea is made here. Every agreement choice belongs to the foregrounded thing in this simple construction.

Change time carefully: **Darwaaza khola gaya**, the door was opened; **Darwaaza khola ja raha hai**, the door is being opened; **Darwaaza khola jaayega**, the door will be opened. The opening is not literally travelling. Do not confuse this passive use with **Woh chala gaya**, he left, where *jaana* is part of an active motion expression.

Sometimes a separate intransitive verb is more natural: **Darwaaza khul gaya**, the door opened. Compare **Darwaaza khola gaya**, the door was opened. The first reports a change without necessarily presenting an unnamed agent. The second presents an action done to the door. In everyday speech, choose the version that conveys what matters instead of insisting every English passive needs a word-for-word passive translation.

## Getting someone else to do something

A causative says that someone causes or arranges an action. Compare **Main kapre dhota hoon**, I wash clothes, male speaker, with **Main kapre dhulwaata hoon**, I have clothes washed. In the second case, another person does the washing. The sentence does not identify that person until you add them: **Main dhobi se kapre dhulwaata hoon**, I have the laundry person wash the clothes.

Learn useful verb families as vocabulary, not as a universal spelling trick:

| Event/state | Direct action | Arrange/cause |
| --- | --- | --- |
| banna: to become/be made | banaana: to make | banwaana: to have made |
| khulna: to open | kholna: to open something | khulwaana: to have opened |
| padhna: to read/study | padhaana: to teach | padhwaana: to have someone read/study |
| seekhna: to learn | sikhaana: to teach | sikhwaana: to arrange teaching |

Meanings and available forms differ by verb. **Main darzi se kurta silwa raha hoon** means I am having a tailor make a kurta, male speaker. A female speaker uses *rahi hoon*. **Maine darzi se kurta silwaaya** is I had a kurta made by the tailor. *Ne* marks the arranger in the completed transitive pattern; *kurta* controls masculine agreement.

To describe letting someone act, use the oblique infinitive plus *dena*: **Bachchon ko khelne dijiye**, let the children play. This is not the same structure as the bare-stem compound **kar dijiye**. The *-ne* matters. **Mujhe bolne dijiye** means let me speak; **Mujhe bol dijiye** means tell me. Compare these aloud until the small difference has a clear practical meaning.

## Emphasis follows what you mean to highlight

Particles are short words that guide attention. **Main bhi aaunga**, male speaker, means I will come too. **Main aaj bhi aaunga** means I will come today too. **Main aaj hi aaunga** means I will come today itself, not another day. Move *bhi* or *hi* and the focus changes. A correct string of words with the wrong focus can answer the wrong question.

**Aap hi bataaiye** means you tell me, or you yourself tell me, depending on tone. **Yahi** combines *yeh* and focus: this very one. **Wahi** points to that same one. **Yahaan hi** means right here. In some Hyderabadi households, **yahaan ich** or **idhar ich** provides local emphasis comparable in many contexts to shared *hi*. Do not assume *ich* can be attached to any word without learning the household pattern.

**To** can mark contrast: **Chai to achchhi hai, lekin bahut garam hai**, the tea is good, but it is very hot. It can also introduce a consequence, as in *agar ... to*. The surrounding sentence tells you its job. **Na** at the end may seek agreement or soften a request: **Aap kal aa rahe hain na?**, you're coming tomorrow, aren't you? A sharp tone can make the same particle impatient.

Listen for emphasis in a short exchange. Question: **Kaun aayega?** Who will come? Answer: **Main hi aaunga**, I myself will come. Question: **Kab aaoge?** When will you come? Answer: **Aaj hi aaunga**, I will come today itself. The verb is unchanged; the focus answers a different question. Practise this with one set of people, places, and times before adding new vocabulary.

## Local forms as social knowledge

**Hau** is an affirmative in many Hyderabadi conversations. **Nakko** can decline an offer or express “don't” in a suitable construction. **Kaiku** asks why. **Mereku** and **tereku** may correspond to shared *mujhe/mujhko* and *tumhe/tujhe*, but the second-person form also carries familiarity and household-specific social meaning. These are useful recognition targets; there is no need to force all of them into every sentence.

For example, **Mereku maaloom nahin** means I do not know in a local style. Shared **Mujhe maaloom nahin** is an alternative. **Nakko, shukriya** can decline more food; shared **Nahin, shukriya** works more widely. **Fikr nakko karo** may be a familiar local “don't worry”; respectful shared **Fikr mat kijiye** is safer with an elder until you know the family's norms.

Recognise reductions without making them your spelling system. A relative may say a fast form resembling *aara* or *jaara* where a more separated model has *aa raha* or *jaa raha*. These spellings only approximate speech. First identify meaning and person. Ask **Aap isko dheere keh sakte hain?**, can you say that slowly? Write the family's actual phrase alongside the expanded form, rather than deciding that every speaker must reduce it identically.

Hyderabadi speech also includes variation in agreement and case marking. The course teaches shared-standard patterns explicitly so that you can understand wider Urdu/Hindi, then asks you to observe the family's patterns. Do not “correct” a native relative by quoting the textbook. Equally, do not conclude that one overheard sentence removes all distinctions involving *ne*, gender, or respect. A local pattern needs several real examples and a speaker's explanation.

## Dialogue: getting ready for guests

**Ammi:** Pehle paani bhar lo, phir mez saaf kar dena. — First fill the water, then clean the table.

**Nida:** Hau. Maine chai ke liye doodh bhi le liya hai. — Yes. I have bought milk for the tea too.

**Ammi:** Achchha. Darwaaza khula rehne do. — Good. Let the door stay open.

**Nida:** Theek hai. Bachche baahar khelne lage hain. — All right. The children have started playing outside.

**Ammi:** Unko thodi der khelne do. Mehmaan abhi nahin aaye. — Let them play for a little while. The guests have not arrived yet.

The actions form a practical sequence. *Bhar lo* is familiar preparation for the household; *kar dena* sets a later task; *le liya* presents milk-buying as achieved; *khelne do* permits an activity. Retell the exchange in respectful forms to an older relative helping you, and notice which endings must change.

## Dialogue: arranging a repair

**Aamir:** Pankha phir band ho gaya. — The fan has stopped again.

**Farah:** Kya aapne kisi ko dikhwaaya? — Did you have someone look at it?

**Aamir:** Hau, kal mistri ko bulaaya tha. Unhone kaha ki ek hissa badalna padega. — Yes, I called the repair person yesterday. They said one part would have to be changed.

**Farah:** To woh hissa kab badla jaayega? — So when will that part be replaced?

**Aamir:** Kal. Main waqt ki tasdeeq kar leta hoon. — Tomorrow. I'll confirm the time.

*Band ho gaya* reports a resulting state. *Dikhwaaya* means arranged showing/inspection, not merely “saw.” *Badla jaayega* foregrounds the part that will be changed. Rehearse this exchange with a phone, a tap, and a bag zip, learning any new repair terms your family uses.

## Dialogue: a missed meaning

**Khala:** Yeh cheez idhar ich rakh do. — Put this thing right here.

**Learner:** Maaf kijiye, “idhar ich” ka matlab “yahaan hi” hai? — Excuse me, does “idhar ich” mean “right here”?

**Khala:** Hau, yahaan hi. — Yes, right here.

**Learner:** Achchha, samajh aa gaya. Main yahin rakh deta hoon. — Oh, I understand. I'll put it here.

**Khala:** Aur yeh dabba bachchon ko kholne dena. — And let the children open this box.

**Learner:** Zaroor. Main khud nahin kholunga. — Certainly. I won't open it myself. Male speaker.

The learner asks about meaning without making the local phrase into a joke. The response provides a shared equivalent. This is the model for growing your own household dictionary: a real phrase, a real meaning, a social context, and permission to ask again.

## Translation and diagnosis workshop

Translate the following, then explain which person or noun controls the final ending.

1. Please note the address down. Respectful.
2. I sent the picture. Female or male speaker; feminine object.
3. The children started laughing.
4. Keep speaking slowly. Respectful.
5. I have already eaten. Give both speaker genders.
6. The tea is made here.
7. The door opened.
8. The door was opened.
9. I am having a tailor make a kurta. Female speaker.
10. Let me speak.
11. I will come today itself. Male speaker.
12. I will come too. Female speaker.

**Answers.** (1) **Pata likh lijiye.** *Lijiye* makes the request respectful. (2) **Maine tasveer bhej di.** The feminine object, not the speaker, controls *di*. (3) **Bachche hansne lage.** The plural group controls *lage*. (4) **Aahista bolte rahiye.** This encourages continuing, rather than merely describing current speech. (5) **Main khaana kha chuka/chuki hoon.** Use *main*, not *maine*, in this construction. (6) **Chai yahaan banaai jaati hai.** Feminine *chai* controls the passive forms. (7) **Darwaaza khul gaya.** An intransitive change. (8) **Darwaaza khola gaya.** A passive action. (9) **Main darzi se kurta silwa rahi hoon.** The speaker controls progressive *rahi*. (10) **Mujhe bolne dijiye.** The oblique infinitive marks the permitted action. (11) **Main aaj hi aaunga.** *Hi* focuses today. (12) **Main bhi aaungi.** *Bhi* adds the speaker to others who are coming.

Repair **Maine kha chuka hoon**, **Mujhe bol dijiye** when you mean “let me speak,” and **Chai banaaya jaata hai**. The repairs are **Main kha chuka hoon**, **Mujhe bolne dijiye**, and **Chai banaai jaati hai**. Finally, explain why *banaai li* does not match the intended compound pattern: the first verb should be a stem, so use *bana li*. Explain the repair aloud in English before producing another Urdu sentence.

## Listening and speaking laboratory

Collect twelve short consented household examples containing a second verb. Identify the basic action, then the contribution of the second verb. Do not force an English one-word label if the meaning depends on the situation. If you cannot identify it, write the whole exchange and ask the speaker what difference the shorter version would make. This comparison is more reliable than guessing from an isolated word.

Record a three-minute account of preparing for a visit. Include one task you did for yourself, one task you did for someone else, something that began, something that continued, and something already completed. Then describe one arrangement made through another person. Re-record only after checking whether the listener understood who did what; adding more complex verbs is not progress if responsibility becomes unclear.

For a listening dictation, have a partner choose from *khul gaya*, *khola gaya*, *khol diya*, *kholne diya*, and *khulwaaya*. Write the Roman phrase and its English meaning. Then put it into a complete sentence. The task tests meaningful contrasts, not whether your Roman spelling matches one rigid convention. In the companion, use synthetic audio as rehearsal and compare it against household recordings when available.

## The 90-hour practice plan

| Work | Hours | Evidence |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| Read and transform compound/aspect examples | 14 | A meaning-based notebook of thirty combinations |
| Passive, causative, and permission workshop | 12 | Forty contrasting sentence pairs |
| Local listening and careful transcription | 18 | Twelve explained household extracts |
| Pronunciation, shadowing, and focus practice | 12 | Six before/after recordings with changed emphasis |
| Guided speaking and practical role-play | 16 | Visits, repair, and cooperation scenes |
| Vocabulary and cumulative spaced review | 8 | New words used across three situations each |
| Family mission, feedback, and exit task | 10 | A household usage page and an unseen account |
| **Total** | **90** | **815 cumulative hours** |

Do not count the same recording session twice because it appears under listening and speaking. Allocate its actual minutes to the activity you are doing. Spend some vocabulary review time on older simple words; they are the words that carry most of the complex grammar you are learning.

## Exit performance

Explain a sequence of practical events to someone who did not witness it. The listener should be able to identify what happened by itself, what you did, what you arranged through someone else, and what someone was allowed to do. Then ask the listener to change one detail and respond naturally. Use emphasis to correct one misunderstanding without raising your voice.

On a second occasion, interpret three authentic local phrases with a family member. Give shared equivalents and describe when you would use the local forms. Readiness means you can ask sensible questions about unfamiliar usage, preserve respect, and avoid treating a machine's voice as the authority on your family's speech. Chapter eleven puts these skills into longer calls, visits, complaints, and collaborative conversations.

## Source checks

The chapter uses original examples and explanations. Verb labels and contrasts were checked against Rupert Snell's [Hindi-Urdu Verb](https://hindiurduflagship.org/wp/wp-content/uploads/2015/05/HU-Verb.pdf), sections on compound verbs, passive, causatives, and completion. Dakhni variation is treated as social context rather than a universal word-substitution system; the research ledger records the limited accessible inspection of Maria Casadei's [doctoral work in the Jagiellonian University repository](https://ruj.uj.edu.pl/bitstreams/d6c2a552-45b8-45b0-a89f-98aeace7ac4a/download). No claim is made that a single spelling or household pattern represents every Hyderabadi speaker.


<a id="chapter-11"></a>

# 11. Real-world conversation

**Time: 90 hours. Course total after this chapter: 905 hours. Target: sustained interaction approaching B2.**

A language exercise gives you a clear prompt and time to answer. A real call may begin while someone is cooking, include a changed plan, lose its connection, and end with an unexpected request. The problem is rarely one unknown verb. You must follow the purpose, take a turn, check a detail, and help the other person understand what happens next.

This chapter teaches conversation as a collaborative activity. Its grammar is mostly familiar; the new challenge is using it when you do not control the next question. You will practise family calls, practical arrangements, service problems, boundaries, apologies, and relaying information. You do not need elaborate vocabulary to manage these well. You need clear signposts and a willingness to repair misunderstandings.

## The four jobs of a useful turn

In an extended exchange, a turn can do four jobs: connect to what was said, add useful information, invite a response, and make the next action clear. Consider **Hau, samajh gaya. Main kal do baje aa sakta hoon. Kya woh waqt aapke liye theek hai?** A male speaker says: yes, I understand. I can come tomorrow at two. Is that time suitable for you? A female speaker uses *samajh gayi* and *aa sakti hoon*.

The first part acknowledges the previous speaker. The second supplies an availability. The question checks the other person's needs. You have not merely answered; you have helped the conversation progress. In practice, you will sometimes need only one or two of these jobs. The point is to know what your turn accomplishes, not to attach a formula to everything you say.

When you need thinking time, use a meaningful holding phrase: **Ek minute, main soch loon**, one moment, let me think; **Isko kaise kahoon?**, how shall I put this?; **Achchha sawaal hai**, that is a good question. Then actually answer. A long sequence of fillers can make the listener unsure whether you are finished or waiting for help.

To take a turn politely, **Kya main ek baat kahoon?** means may I say something? **Ek chhoti si baat** introduces a small point. To return the floor, **Aap kya kehte hain?** asks what do you think, respectfully. To include a quieter person, **Aapki kya raay hai?** asks for their opinion. These moves matter especially when several family members know each other's stories and speak quickly.

## Repair is a conversational skill

There are several different comprehension problems, and they need different questions. If you did not hear the sound, **Aakhri baat phir se kahenge?** asks would you repeat the last part? If the words were audible but unfamiliar, **“Munasib” ka matlab kya hai?** asks what does “suitable” mean? If the implication is unclear, **Aapka matlab hai ki hum kal nahin aayen?** asks do you mean we should not come tomorrow?

Avoid responding to every difficulty with **Kya?** It provides little information and may sound abrupt. Target the missing piece: **Do baje ya teen baje?**, at two or at three? **Aap kis Khala ki baat kar rahe hain?**, which aunt are you talking about? **Main waqt samajh gaya, lekin jagah nahin samjha**, male speaker: I understood the time but not the place. A female speaker uses *samajh gayi* and *samjhi*.

Confirm important information in your own words: **To hum jumme ko chaar baje aapke ghar mil rahe hain, theek?** So we are meeting at your house on Friday at four, correct? This tests the whole arrangement. A polite **ji** after every sentence does not prove you have understood it. For dates, numbers, names, and locations, read-back is a practical kindness.

When you made the mistake, repair it briefly: **Maaf kijiye, mera matlab teen baje tha, do baje nahin**—excuse me, I meant three, not two. Do not bury the corrected information under a long apology. If a plan has changed, signal the change before the detail: **Waqt badal gaya hai. Ab hum paanch baje milenge.** The time has changed. Now we will meet at five.

## A phone call has a beginning and an ending

Start by checking availability: **Assalaam alaikum, kya abhi baat karna munasib hai?** Hello, is this a suitable time to talk? A less formal shared option is **Kya aap abhi baat kar sakte hain?** Can you talk now? If someone is busy, **Main baad mein phone kar loon?** asks shall I call later? Do not interpret a short answer as rejection before checking the circumstances.

Give the purpose early: **Main kal ke programme ke baare mein poochh raha hoon**, I am calling to ask about tomorrow's plan, male speaker; *poochh rahi hoon* for female. Then ask the first specific question. With a weak connection, **Aapki aawaaz saaf nahin aa rahi** means your voice is not coming through clearly. **Thoda aahista boliye** asks please speak a little slowly. **Main dobara phone karta/karti hoon** says I'll call again.

To close, summarise one action: **Theek hai, main aaj shaam tak tasdeeq kar doonga**, male speaker: all right, I will confirm by this evening. Female: *kar doongi*. Then a relational closing: **Aapse baat karke achchha laga**, it was good talking to you. **Ghar mein sabko mera salaam kahiye**, please give my greetings to everyone at home. Use the greetings and closings your family prefers.

Do not say **Main rakhta hoon** before the arrangement is understood just because it is a familiar telephone closing. If someone is still giving an address, invite the rest: **Ji, aage bataaiye**, yes, please continue. The skill is coordinating attention, not performing a perfect scripted opening and exit.

## Dialogue: a family call with a changed plan

**Learner:** Assalaam alaikum, Khala. Kya aap abhi baat kar sakti hain? — Hello, Aunt. Can you talk now?

**Khala:** Hau, bolo. Kya haal hai? — Yes, tell me. How are you?

**Learner:** Sab theek hai. Main itwaar ke programme ke baare mein poochh raha hoon. Hum do baje aayen? — Everything is fine. I am asking about Sunday's plan. Should we come at two? Male speaker.

**Khala:** Do baje nakko, teen baje aao. Main do baje tak baahar rahoongi. — Not at two; come at three. I will be out until two.

**Learner:** Achchha, teen baje. Kya main kuchh le aaun? — All right, three. Shall I bring something?

**Khala:** Bas bachchon ko le aao. Aur pehle phone kar dena. — Just bring the children. And call beforehand.

**Learner:** Zaroor. To main nikalne se pehle phone karoonga. Aapko kal phir yaad dila doon? — Certainly. So I will call before leaving. Shall I remind you again tomorrow?

**Khala:** Nakko, yaad hai. Aaraam se aao. — No need, I remember. Come at your ease.

The local *nakko* appears naturally in refusal and “no need.” The learner maintains respectful forms while the aunt uses familiar ones. Retell the agreed plan in English, then in Roman Urdu. If you say “two” after hearing the dialogue, more grammar will not solve the problem; you need focused detail-checking.

## Travel and services: say the task before the story

At a service counter, begin with the desired action: **Mujhe apni booking badalni hai**, I need to change my booking. *Booking* is often treated as feminine in this usage, giving *badalni*. Then provide the necessary context: **Maine kal ke liye booking ki thi, lekin ab mujhe parson jaana hai**—I had booked for tomorrow, but now I need to go the day after tomorrow. Finally ask what is possible: **Kya waqt badal sakta hai?**

When asking for directions, split the information into stages. **Pehle kahaan jaana hai?** Where do I go first? **Uske baad?** After that? **Kya main paidal ja sakta/sakti hoon?** Can I go on foot? **Taqreeban kitna waqt lagega?** Approximately how long will it take? Repeat the turning point: **Bank ke baad daayen?** Right after the bank? Do not pretend to follow a long route just to appear fluent.

For delays, distinguish facts from estimates. **Gaari abhi nahin aayi** means the vehicle has not arrived yet. **Shaayad bees minute lagenge** means it may take twenty minutes. **Unhone bees minute kaha hai** means they have said twenty minutes. The last sentence attributes the estimate. This distinction helps you avoid promising certainty you do not have when relaying information to relatives.

Safety and urgency depend on the real situation, not on a language exercise. Learn clear requests such as **Mujhe madad chahiye**, I need help, and **Meherbaani karke kisi zimmedaar shakhs ko bulaaiye**, please call a responsible person. This course teaches communication; follow appropriate local emergency guidance in an actual emergency rather than trying to complete a practice dialogue.

## Complaints: observation, effect, and remedy

A useful complaint identifies what happened, why it matters, and what would resolve it. **Maine do kamron ki booking ki thi, lekin yahaan ek kamra diya gaya hai. Hamaare saath bachche hain. Kya doosra kamra mil sakta hai?** I had booked two rooms, but only one room has been provided here. We have children with us. Could we get the second room?

The grammar supports the purpose. *Ki thi* establishes the earlier booking. *Diya gaya hai* focuses on what was provided without guessing who made the error. The explanation names the effect on the group. *Mil sakta hai* asks about a remedy. You can be firm without beginning with an accusation about someone's character.

If the first remedy is unavailable, ask for alternatives: **Agar doosra kamra nahin hai to aur kya intezaam ho sakta hai?** If there is no second room, what other arrangement can be made? **Kya main zimmedaar shakhs se baat kar sakta/sakti hoon?** May I speak to the person responsible? Confirm any agreement before ending: **To aap bees minute mein bata denge?** So you will let me know in twenty minutes?

In practice, make the problem specific but low stakes: a wrong food order, a missed call, a changed time. Role-play both customer and service worker. The worker should have a genuine constraint, such as no available table until later. If one side can always say yes, you will not practise negotiation or attentive listening.

## Dialogue: a service problem with alternatives

**Guest:** Maaf kijiye, hamaari booking teen logon ke liye thi. Yahaan do kursiyaan hain. — Excuse me, our booking was for three people. There are two chairs here.

**Worker:** Main dekh leta hoon. Abhi doosri mez khaali nahin hai. — I'll take a look. No other table is free at the moment.

**Guest:** Koi baat nahin. Kya ek aur kursi yahaan rakhi ja sakti hai? — That's all right. Could one more chair be put here?

**Worker:** Yahaan raasta band ho jaayega. Paanch minute mein woh mez khaali ho jaayegi. — It will block the passage here. That table will be free in five minutes.

**Guest:** Theek hai, hum intezaar kar lenge. Meherbaani karke hamein bata dijiye. — All right, we will wait. Please let us know.

**Worker:** Zaroor. — Certainly.

The guest does not abandon the need; the worker explains a constraint. The solution meets the purpose with a short delay. Notice **rakhi ja sakti hai**, can be placed: feminine *kursi* controls the passive and ability forms. Practise a simpler alternative too: **Kya hamein ek aur kursi mil sakti hai?** Could we get another chair?

## Boundaries without unnecessary hostility

Family closeness does not remove the need to decline. **Aapki daawat ka bahut shukriya, lekin is baar main nahin aa sakta/sakti** means thank you very much for the invitation, but I cannot come this time. You can give a brief reason: **Pehle se ek zaroori kaam hai**, I already have an important commitment. You do not owe a detailed fictional story to make the sentence grammatically complete.

For a topic you prefer not to discuss, **Filhaal main is baare mein baat nahin karna chaahta/chaahti** means at present I do not want to discuss this. To soften while keeping the boundary, add **Umeed hai aap samjhenge**, I hope you will understand. If someone continues, repeat the main point calmly rather than inventing increasingly complicated excuses.

To decline food in a familiar local style, **Nakko, shukriya, pet bhar gaya** means no thanks, I am full. A respectful shared version is **Nahin, shukriya. Bahut mazedaar tha, lekin ab aur nahin kha sakta/sakti**—no thank you. It was delicious, but I cannot eat more now. Warmth and clarity can coexist. Match local forms to the relationship you actually have.

If you can offer an alternative, make it concrete: **Aaj nahin ho paayega, lekin kal shaam baat kar sakte hain**—it will not be possible today, but we can talk tomorrow evening. An alternative is useful when sincere; it should not become another promise you cannot keep. Practise declining once, being pressed once, and repeating the boundary respectfully.

## Apologies, responsibility, and repair

An apology works better when it names the action. **Maaf kijiye, main der se aaya/aayi** means forgive me, I arrived late. **Mujhe pehle bata dena chahiye tha** means I should have told you earlier. This acknowledges the missed responsibility. **Agli baar main nikalne se pehle phone karoonga/karoongi** gives a concrete repair for the future.

Compare **Agar aapko bura laga to maaf kijiye**, sorry if you felt bad, with **Meri baat se aapko takleef hui. Mujhe afsos hai**, my words hurt you. I am sorry. The first can sound as if the only problem was the other person's reaction. The second acknowledges an effect. There are situations for conditional apologies, but do not use the form to avoid a clear responsibility you already accept.

To respond to an apology, you can acknowledge it without pretending nothing mattered: **Shukriya, aapne bata diya. Bas agli baar pehle khabar kar dijiye**—thank you for telling me. Just let me know beforehand next time. **Koi baat nahin** means no problem, but choose it only if it matches your meaning. Vocabulary should help express your actual position.

Retell a small misunderstanding from two perspectives. First say what you intended. Then say what the listener could reasonably have understood. Use **Mera iraada ... tha**, my intention was ..., and **Lekin aapko shayad ... laga**, but perhaps it seemed ... to you. This is a language exercise in perspective-taking, not a requirement to share a private conflict.

## Relaying information fairly

Mediation means helping another person understand information or a viewpoint. Suppose a relative misses a call about a family gathering. Do not repeat every sentence. Identify the decision, reason, open question, and next step: **Programme itwaar ko hai. Waqt teen baje kar diya gaya hai kyunki Khala do baje tak baahar hongi. Hamein nikalne se pehle phone karna hai.** The event is on Sunday. The time has been moved to three because Aunt will be out until two. We need to call before leaving.

Attribute uncertain or personal information: **Unke khayaal mein**, in their opinion; **Unhone kaha ki**, they said that; **Abhi tasdeeq nahin hui**, it has not been confirmed yet. Do not convert a suggestion into a decision. **Unhone kaha ki hum shayad jaldi niklen** means they suggested we might leave early, while **Unhone kaha ki hum jaldi niklenge** reports a firmer future statement.
