When helping two speakers, check meaning before translating: **Kya aap chaahte hain ki main yeh baat unko samjha doon?** Do you want me to explain this to them? Keep the tone and degree of certainty. If a word has no exact match, explain the practical meaning. If you are unsure, say so. Being a useful language bridge includes recognising the limits of what you understood.

## Dialogue: coordinating two preferences

**Dadi:** Main der tak baahar nahin reh sakti. — I cannot stay out late.

**Cousin:** Lekin sab log shaam ko free hote hain. — But everyone is free in the evening.

**Learner:** Dadi keh rahi hain ki unko jaldi ghar aana hai. Aap keh rahe hain ki shaam sabke liye aasaan hai. — Grandmother is saying she needs to get home early. You are saying evening is easier for everyone.

**Dadi:** Hau, bas bahut der nakko ho. — Yes, just not too late.

**Learner:** To agar hum paanch baje milen aur saat baje tak waapas aa jaayen to theek rahega? — So if we meet at five and return by seven, will that work?

**Cousin:** Mere liye theek hai. Main baaqi logon se poochh leta hoon. — That works for me. I'll ask the others.

**Learner:** Achchha, abhi yeh tajweez hai. Tasdeeq hone par main Dadi ko bata doonga. — Good, this is a proposal for now. Once confirmed, I will tell Grandmother. Male speaker.

The learner represents both needs before proposing a compromise. The final line prevents a common mistake: treating a provisional plan as confirmed. Repeat the scene with new limits, such as transport or a child's bedtime, and avoid reading the same solution each time.

## Translation and interaction workshop

Write or speak a natural response for each situation, then compare the models.

1. You heard the time but not the location. Say this respectfully.
2. Ask whether now is a suitable time to talk.
3. Tell someone the time has changed to five.
4. Explain that you booked two rooms but received one.
5. Decline an invitation this time and thank the host.
6. Say you should have called earlier.
7. Tell someone the plan is not confirmed yet.
8. Ask permission to explain their point to another person.
9. Ask for the last part to be repeated.
10. Close a call after promising to confirm by evening.

**Models and reasoning.** (1) **Waqt samajh mein aa gaya, lekin jagah nahin samjhi.** A neutral alternative is **Waqt samajh aa gaya. Jagah phir se bata dijiye.** (2) **Kya abhi baat karna munasib hai?** This checks availability before the purpose. (3) **Waqt badal gaya hai. Ab paanch baje milenge.** Name the change first. (4) **Maine do kamron ki booking ki thi, lekin ek hi kamra diya gaya hai.** *Ek hi* highlights the shortage. (5) **Daawat ka shukriya, lekin is baar main nahin aa sakta/sakti.** Gender follows the speaker. (6) **Mujhe pehle phone kar dena chahiye tha.** The past obligation expresses regret. (7) **Programme ki abhi tasdeeq nahin hui.** Feminine *tasdeeq* controls *hui*. (8) **Kya main aapki baat unko samjha doon?** This asks permission rather than assuming it. (9) **Aakhri baat phir se kahenge?** A respectful request. (10) **Main shaam tak tasdeeq kar doonga/doongi. Aapse baat karke achchha laga.** State the next action before closing.

Now interpret **Unhone kaha hai ki shaayad der hogi**, **Unhone tasdeeq kar di hai**, and **Abhi sirf tajweez hai**. They mean: they have said there may be a delay; they have confirmed it; it is only a proposal for now. The contrast is certainty. Your listener must be able to distinguish these even if your accent is imperfect.

## Listening and speaking beyond the script

Use the companion's speaking recorder for a ninety-second voicemail. State who you are, why you are calling, two necessary details, and what response you need. Play it without reading. Could someone act on it? If not, revise the information order. A machine transcript can be useful when supported, but a recognition mismatch does not prove your pronunciation is wrong.

For listening, ask a willing partner to improvise a two-minute message containing a changed plan, one reason, and an unconfirmed detail. Listen once and say the main point. Listen again and write the Roman transcript and English summary. Mark uncertainty instead of filling gaps with guesses. Then ask one targeted question. This is closer to real listening than repeatedly recognising isolated familiar words.

For conversation, hold four fifteen-minute sessions with different purposes: a family catch-up, a practical arrangement, a complaint, and a difference of opinion. Give your partner a secret change to introduce halfway through. Afterward, review two successful repairs and one missed cue. Repeat the task with a new situation, not a memorised script. Your response to the unexpected turn is the evidence of growing independence.

## The 90-hour practice plan

| Work | Hours | Evidence |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| Study and rebuild interaction frames | 10 | A purpose-organised phrase bank |
| Calls and voicemail listening | 16 | Eight checked summaries with dates and actions |
| Services, travel, and complaint role-play | 16 | Six scenes containing genuine constraints |
| Boundaries, apologies, and mediation practice | 14 | Paired versions with respectful alternatives |
| Unscripted family conversations | 18 | Six conversations and short reflection notes |
| Vocabulary and cumulative grammar repair | 10 | Personal error log with new examples |
| Exit assessment and reattempt | 6 | Two unseen conversations with feedback |
| **Total** | **90** | **905 cumulative hours** |

The family conversations are invitations to practise, not obligations imposed on relatives. When nobody is available, record both sides of a scene, leave a gap, and answer it later without looking. Use live interaction when possible because your own recording cannot fully reproduce another person's timing and unexpected choices.

## Exit performance

Handle a fifteen-minute practical conversation in which the listener changes one important fact. Confirm names, numbers, and next steps; ask one targeted clarification; decline or modify one request appropriately; and close naturally. Then relay the agreed plan to a third person in under two minutes, keeping confirmed and unconfirmed details separate.

Score clarity, listening accuracy, independent repair, turn-taking, and register from 0 to 4. Seek mostly 3s on two different tasks, with no critical misunderstanding left uncorrected. This is an informal readiness check. It cannot certify B2 or replace a qualified proficiency interview. Chapter twelve combines the whole course into a final set of unfamiliar conversations and a plan for continuing beyond the core thousand hours.

## Source checks

The interaction goals draw on the [Council of Europe CEFR descriptor framework](https://www.coe.int/en/web/common-european-framework-reference-languages/cefr-descriptors). The University of Texas Hindi Urdu Flagship's [assessment overview](https://hindiurduflagship.org/students-alumni/huf-language-assessment-and-testing/) was inspected for its distinction between rehearsed knowledge and sustained communicative ability. Its ILR levels are not treated as interchangeable with CEFR levels. All scenes, wording, and course assessment tasks here are original.

# 12. B2 capstone and lifelong conversation

**Time: 95 hours. Course total after this chapter: 1,000 hours. Target: independent, B2-oriented spoken family and everyday interaction.**

The course began with a greeting and a way to ask someone to repeat. It ends with a much richer version of the same relationship: you can take part, show interest, make your meaning clear, and ask for help when needed. A confident speaker does not know every word. A confident speaker has ways to keep a meaningful exchange going while respecting the listener and the limits of their own knowledge.

The thousand hours are a study allocation, not a promise that every learner reaches the same level on the same date. The B2 target here concerns listening and conversation. Because you chose Roman script, this course does not claim B2 literacy in either Urdu or Hindi script. The final tasks provide evidence of spoken abilities and show what still needs practice; they are not an official qualification.

## What independence looks like

At this stage, a familiar topic should no longer require a full written script. You should be able to explain an experience, give reasons for a view, compare options, follow a sustained exchange, and repair a misunderstanding. Some searching, a noticeable accent, and grammatical mistakes may remain. The practical question is whether they repeatedly stop communication or whether you can recover and continue.

Independence also includes social judgement. An elder may prefer *aap*, a cousin may prefer *tum*, and a particular family may use local forms in some situations but shared forms in others. You should be able to adapt rather than announce that the course's example is the only correct way. Ask **Aap log ghar mein yeh kaise kehte hain?**—how do you say this at home?—and listen to the answer as language evidence.

The core thousand-word vocabulary is a foundation. B2 conversation requires flexible use of those words, recognition of many more words and combinations, and additional vocabulary tied to your life. You may need special terms for your work, family recipes, faith, hobbies, or a relative's memories. Build that personal layer during this chapter instead of treating a fixed word count as the finish line.

## Putting grammar into a speaking decision

Before a difficult sentence, identify four things: who is involved, when the event happens, how the action unfolds, and what you want the listener to do with the information. Suppose you want to explain a missed family visit. The people are you and Khala. The time is yesterday. The missed action is coming. The conversational purpose is an apology and a new arrangement.

Build it in stages. **Main kal nahin aa saka**, male speaker: I could not come yesterday. Female: *aa saki*. Add the reason: **Kyunki kaam der se khatam hua**, because work finished late. Add responsibility: **Mujhe pehle bata dena chahiye tha**, I should have told you earlier. Add repair: **Agar aap free hon to main itwaar ko aa jaaoon?** If you are free, shall I come on Sunday? You have used past ability, a reason, past obligation, and a respectful proposal to accomplish one social purpose.

Do not begin by asking which advanced tense you can display. Begin with the meaning. Short connected clauses can convey a precise argument more effectively than one long sentence with unstable agreement. Once the listener understands the structure, add detail. If you lose track, repair with **Main doosre tareeqe se kehta/kehti hoon**, let me put it another way.

## Inference and certainty

**Woh ghar par honge** can mean they will be at home or they are probably at home, depending on context. A future-looking form can support an inference. Anchor the meaning when it matters: **Ab tak woh ghar pahunch gaye honge**, by now they have probably reached home, with a respectful male or plural reference. For a woman respectfully, **pahunch gayi hongi** is a useful form. These are reasoned assumptions, not confirmed facts.

Compare **Woh aa gaye hain**, they have arrived; **Shaayad woh aa gaye hain**, perhaps they have arrived; and **Woh aa gaye honge**, they have probably arrived/will have arrived. The confidence differs. If you have a message, attribute it: **Unhone phone par kaha ki woh pahunch gaye hain**—they said on the phone that they have arrived. The listener can now distinguish evidence from your guess.

For reported information, **Mujhe bataya gaya ki waqt badal gaya hai** means I was told the time has changed. If you did not confirm it yourself, add **Lekin maine abhi tasdeeq nahin ki**, but I have not confirmed it yet. This is especially useful when several relatives are relaying a plan. Fluency should make uncertainty clearer, not hide it behind confident delivery.

## Making a point without overclaiming

Use **Aam taur par** for generally, **aksar** for often, **kabhi kabhi** for sometimes, and **har baar nahin** for not every time. **Hamaare ghar mein aksar aisa hota hai** means this often happens in our home. It is a more responsible claim than **Sab Hyderabad mein aisa karte hain**, everyone in Hyderabad does this, unless you truly have evidence for such a broad statement.

To qualify an argument, **Jahaan tak main jaanta/jaanti hoon** means as far as I know. **Mere tajurbe mein** means in my experience. **Iska yeh matlab nahin ki ...** means this does not mean that .... For example, **Mujhe Roman mein padhna aasaan lagta hai. Iska yeh matlab nahin ki doosra tareeqa bekaar hai**—I find reading in Roman script easy. That does not mean another method is useless. The sentence states your preference without dismissing someone else's.

Use these phrases where the distinction matters. Repeating “as far as I know” before every ordinary fact can make you sound hesitant. A good extended answer has a clear main point, supporting reasons, an example, and an acknowledgement of limits. The listener should be able to restate your view in one sentence.

## Capstone scene one: a family history conversation

Read the whole scene once for meaning. Then listen to it, if a willing speaker can record it, without looking. The aim is following a story and asking questions that grow from the answer. The speaker's childhood is fictional, so you can replace details without claiming they describe your own family.

**Learner:** Dadi, jab aap chhoti thin to aapka mohalla kaisa tha? — Grandmother, what was your neighbourhood like when you were little?

**Dadi:** Ghar chhote the, lekin log ek doosre ko achchhi tarah jaante the. Shaam ko sab baahar baithte the. — The houses were small, but people knew one another well. Everyone used to sit outside in the evening.

**Learner:** Kya bachche bhi wahin khelte the? — Did the children play there too?

**Dadi:** Hau. Hum gali mein khelte rehte the. Kisi ke ghar se aawaaz aati to khaane ke liye chale jaate the. — Yes. We would keep playing in the lane. When a call came from someone's house, we would go for food.

**Learner:** Aapko sabse zyaada kis cheez ki yaad aati hai? — What do you miss most?

**Dadi:** Logon ke paas waqt tha. Ab sab masroof hain. Lekin ab suhulat bhi zyaada hai. — People had time. Now everyone is busy. But there are also more conveniences now.

**Learner:** To aapke khayaal mein pehle rishton ke liye waqt zyaada tha, lekin ab rozmarra ke kaam aasaan hain? — So in your view there was more time for relationships before, but everyday tasks are easier now?

**Dadi:** Hau, kuchh aisa hi. Har cheez pehle behtar nahin thi. — Yes, something like that. Not everything was better before.

**Learner:** Koi aisa din yaad hai jab poora mohalla saath tha? — Do you remember a day when the whole neighbourhood was together?

**Dadi:** Ek baar bahut baarish hui thi. Padosiyon ne ek doosre ki madad ki. Us din samajh aaya ki padosi kitne zaroori hote hain. — Once there was heavy rain. The neighbours helped one another. That day we understood how important neighbours are.

Notice the sequence: an open question, a relevant follow-up, a question about personal significance, a fair summary, and a request for a concrete example. The learner does not assume that every memory proves the past was better. For transfer, ask a relative about school, a journey, or a family custom. Do not require them to revisit distressing memories. Offer **Agar aap bataana chaahen**, if you wish to tell me.

Your performance task is to conduct a ten-minute interview and then give a two-minute summary. Preserve the difference between a repeated childhood habit and one particular event. Ask the speaker whether your summary represents their meaning. With permission, save one short excerpt for listening practice; otherwise take notes after the conversation.

## Capstone scene two: organising a gathering

**Farah:** Is baar sabko ghar bulaayen? — Shall we invite everyone home this time?

**Aamir:** Khayaal achchha hai, lekin jagah ka masla hoga. — It is a good idea, but space will be a problem.

**Learner:** Kitne log aa sakte hain? Pehle yeh dekh lete hain. — How many people might come? Let's check that first.

**Farah:** Taqreeban bees. Agar bachche bhi aaye to pachchees. — About twenty. If the children come too, twenty-five.

**Aamir:** Itne logon ke liye kursiyaan kahaan se aayengi? — Where will chairs come from for so many people?

**Learner:** Kursiyaan kiraaye par mil sakti hain. Lekin khaana kaun banaayega? — Chairs can be rented. But who will make the food?

**Farah:** Kuchh ghar mein bana lenge, kuchh baahar se mangwa lenge. — We will make some at home and order some from outside.

**Aamir:** Mujhe lagta hai ki saara kaam aap par aa jaayega. — I think all the work will fall on you.

**Learner:** Yeh baat theek hai. Kaam baant dete hain. Main logon se tasdeeq kar leta hoon, Aamir kursiyon ka intezaam karen, aur khaane ka faisla hum milkar karen. — That is a fair point. Let's divide the work. I'll confirm with people, Aamir can arrange the chairs, and we can decide the food together.

**Farah:** Hau, lekin pehle kharch ka andaaza laga len. — Yes, but first let's estimate the expense.

**Learner:** To abhi teen kaam hain: logon ki tadaad, kursiyaan, aur kharch. Hum kal shaam tak dobara baat karen? — So there are three tasks for now: number of people, chairs, and expense. Shall we speak again by tomorrow evening?

The learner does not solve every problem alone. They structure the decision, share tasks, and identify what remains open. A strong B2-oriented task includes this coordination. For transfer, your partner changes one constraint: the budget is smaller, an elder cannot climb stairs, or half the guests can only come later. Respond to the new information without restarting a memorised plan.

Prepare a spoken summary containing confirmed decisions, assigned tasks, and unresolved questions. Ask another person to act only from your summary. If they cannot identify their task or deadline, revise the message. Grammar matters because it supports responsibility and time, not because a long sentence receives extra credit.

## Capstone scene three: a disagreement with common ground

**Cousin:** Aajkal sab phone par rehte hain. Koi baat hi nahin karta. — These days everyone stays on their phone. Nobody talks.

**Learner:** Main samajh raha hoon ki aapko saath baithkar baat karna yaad aata hai. — I understand that you miss sitting together and talking. Male speaker.

**Cousin:** Hau. Khaane ke waqt bhi phone! — Yes. Phones even at mealtimes!

**Learner:** Khaane ke waqt phone alag rakhna achchha khayaal hai. Lekin phone ki wajah se hum door rehkar bhi baat kar paate hain. — Putting phones aside at meals is a good idea. But because of phones we can talk even while living far apart.

**Cousin:** Woh bhi theek hai, magar hadd honi chahiye. — That is true too, but there should be a limit.

**Learner:** To shaayad masla phone nahin, uska istemaal hai. Agar hum roz kuchh waqt sirf ghar waalon ke liye rakhen to kaisa rahega? — So perhaps the problem is not the phone but its use. What if we set aside some time every day just for the family?

**Cousin:** Kitna waqt? — How much time?

**Learner:** Pehle bees minute. Agar kaam na kare to tareeqa badal sakte hain. — Twenty minutes to begin with. If it does not work, we can change the approach.

**Cousin:** Theek hai. Lekin aap bhi phone door rakhenge! — All right. But you will put your phone away too!

**Learner:** Zaroor. Qaaida sabke liye ek hi hona chahiye. — Certainly. The rule should be the same for everyone.

The practice skill is qualifying an absolute claim and finding a workable proposal. You do not have to agree with the example's conclusion. Argue for a different approach using two reasons and a concession. Your partner should interrupt once with a fair objection. Respond to the objection rather than repeating your original paragraph more loudly.

## Capstone scene four: helping after a misunderstanding

**Khala:** Maine samjha tha ki aap log kal aa rahe hain. Main intezaar karti rahi. — I thought you were coming yesterday. I kept waiting.

**Learner:** Maaf kijiye. Hamein jumme ka waqt bataya gaya tha. Lagta hai paighaam mein galatfehmi ho gayi. — I am sorry. We were told Friday's time. It seems there was a misunderstanding in the message.

**Khala:** Kisi ne mujhe badla hua waqt nahin bataya. — Nobody told me the changed time.

**Learner:** Hamein aapko pehle bata dena chahiye tha. Main abhi sab se tasdeeq kar leta hoon. — We should have told you earlier. I'll confirm with everyone now.

**Khala:** Ab jaldi kaiku kar rahe ho? Pehle saaf batao ki aaoge kab. — Why are you rushing now? First tell me clearly when you will come.

**Learner:** Aap theek keh rahi hain. Hum jumme ko chaar baje aayenge. Kya woh waqt aapke liye theek hai? — You are right. We will come Friday at four. Is that time suitable for you?

**Khala:** Hau, woh theek hai. — Yes, that is fine.

**Learner:** Main yahi waqt sabko likhkar bhej deta hoon. Aur nikalne se pehle aapko phone karoonga. — I'll send everyone this same time in writing. And I'll call you before leaving. Male speaker.

**Khala:** Theek hai. Bas ab galatfehmi nakko ho. — All right. Just let there be no more misunderstanding now.

This exchange does not require the learner to prove who caused the mistake before repairing it. They acknowledge the effect, clarify the agreed time, and give a next step. For transfer, remove the script and have your partner change both the day and one person's understanding. Reconcile the information accurately before closing.

## The final listening assessment

Ask a proficient speaker to prepare three unfamiliar recordings: a two-minute everyday message, a three-minute personal story, and a four-minute discussion with two viewpoints. They should use natural, clear speech, including household forms when appropriate. The course's printed scenes are rehearsal material; the final recordings should change details and wording. If only synthetic speech is available, record that limitation and seek human listening evidence later.

Listen to each recording once for the main purpose. Give a short English or Roman Urdu summary without seeing a transcript. On the second pass, note people, times, reasons, changes, and uncertainty. Write a Roman transcription of a selected thirty-second section and translate it into English. Then compare with the speaker's intended meaning. Score meaning rather than one rigid Roman spelling.

Use twenty content points across the three recordings, chosen before the attempt. Award one point for each correct point, half for an incomplete but nonmisleading point, and zero for a wrong or absent point. Mark critical details such as a changed day separately. Aim for at least sixteen points and no uncorrected critical misunderstanding after one opportunity to ask a clarification. This is a local course threshold, not a validated CEFR cut score.

## The final speaking assessment

Complete four tasks with a partner over two sessions. First, hold a ten-minute personal conversation with follow-up questions. Second, describe a past event for three minutes and answer questions for five. Third, negotiate a practical decision for twelve minutes with one surprise constraint. Fourth, discuss a familiar general issue for ten minutes, giving a view, reasons, an example, and a fair response to an objection.

Do not read or memorise a full response. You may use five keywords per task. The partner should let you seek clarification naturally and should not supply every missing word. Record with consent. If consent is absent, take brief notes after the task. Evaluate performance on two occasions with different topics, because a single familiar script can conceal a major transfer gap.

Rate five dimensions from 0 to 4:

| Dimension | What a 3 looks like |
| --- | --- |
| Range | Enough words and structures to explain, compare, narrate, and give reasons; paraphrase fills many gaps. |
| Control | Errors occur but most intended relationships remain clear; several recurring errors can be corrected. |
| Fluency and coherence | Connected speech with manageable pauses; ideas follow a recognisable order. |
| Interaction and listening | Responds to actual turns, asks targeted clarification, takes and returns the floor. |
| Appropriateness and intelligibility | Generally understandable pronunciation and suitable respect/register; local forms fit the relationship. |

A 0 means the task could not be attempted meaningfully; 1 means heavy support; 2 means partial independence with frequent breakdown; 3 means mostly independent performance; 4 means confident, flexible performance for this task. Seek at least 15/20, no dimension below 2, and no critical breakdown left unresolved on both occasions. A qualified teacher can provide stronger external judgement. These numbers guide practice and do not confer certification.

## Grammar and translation audit

Translate these integrated prompts, then compare the models and explain the choices.

1. I should have told you earlier, but I thought you already knew.
2. If everyone agrees, we can meet at five instead of four.
3. The time has not been confirmed yet.
4. I had the clothes washed because I was busy.
5. As far as I know, they have probably arrived by now.
6. Although I understand your point, I prefer the other method.
7. Let me explain what happened before you decide.
8. The people who are coming tomorrow should be told the new time.

**Models.** (1) **Mujhe aapko pehle bata dena chahiye tha, lekin mujhe laga ki aapko pehle se maaloom hai.** Past responsibility is separate from the earlier assumption. (2) **Agar sab raazi hon to hum chaar ke bajaaye paanch baje mil sakte hain.** The subjunctive leaves agreement open. (3) **Waqt ki abhi tasdeeq nahin hui.** *Tasdeeq* is feminine. (4) **Maine kapre dhulwaaye kyunki main masroof tha/thi.** The causative says someone else washed them. (5) **Jahaan tak mujhe maaloom hai, woh ab tak pahunch gaye honge.** Match the forms to the referent; for a woman respectfully, *pahunch gayi hongi*. (6) **Halaanki aapki baat samajh mein aa rahi hai, mujhe doosra tareeqa zyaada pasand hai.** Understanding does not entail agreement. (7) **Aap faisla karne se pehle mujhe bataane dijiye ki kya hua.** *Bataane dijiye* permits explaining. (8) **Jo log kal aa rahe hain, unko naya waqt bata dena chahiye.** The relative-correlative connection identifies the affected people.

Translate back into English without looking at the original prompts. If a phrase changes meaning, identify whether the problem concerns time, agency, agreement, certainty, or social purpose. That diagnosis tells you what to revisit. Correcting every sentence by memorising one model does not reveal the underlying pattern.

## Building a personal continuation vocabulary

Choose five domains in your actual life. For each, collect twenty useful words or phrases from real conversations, giving you one hundred personal additions. Each entry should contain a Roman form, meaning, one complete sentence, who used it, and a note about register or context. Ask for a recording only with consent. The aim is usable knowledge, not a larger spreadsheet.

Practise each new item in three different turns: a statement, a question, and a response to someone else's view. For example, a new word about a hobby should help you explain what you enjoy, ask your cousin about it, and respond when they disagree. If a specialised term remains hard to retrieve, practise a simpler explanation using familiar words. Circumlocution is a core conversation skill.

Keep recognising more than you actively produce. You may understand a very local phrase after one family explanation but choose a shared form until you hear its social setting several times. That is a sensible learning choice. The long-term goal is belonging in real exchanges, not displaying the maximum number of local markers in one sentence.

## The final 95 hours

| Work | Hours | Evidence |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| Integrated grammar and personal error repair | 12 | Twenty error patterns explained and re-used correctly |
| Four capstone scenes with changed constraints | 16 | Eight improvised scene recordings or notes |
| Unfamiliar human listening and transcription | 18 | Three assessed recordings plus follow-up practice |
| Extended family conversations and interviews | 18 | Six conversations with fair summaries |
| Personal hundred-item vocabulary extension | 10 | Context-rich entries used in new turns |
| Final listening and speaking assessments | 9 | Two occasions with separate topics and scores |
| Feedback, targeted practice, and reattempt | 8 | A documented response to the weakest dimensions |
| Continuation plan and personal audio portfolio | 4 | A realistic next eight-week plan |
| **Total** | **95** | **1,000 cumulative hours** |

The complete sequence is 60 + 70 + 80 + 80 + 85 + 85 + 85 + 90 + 90 + 90 + 90 + 95 = **1,000 hours**. The cumulative totals are 60, 130, 210, 290, 375, 460, 545, 635, 725, 815, 905, and 1,000. If you need extra practice, log it honestly as additional time rather than changing a completed assessment score to match the planned endpoint.

## An eight-week continuation cycle

After the course, aim for a sustainable weekly rhythm: two real conversations, three short listening sessions, one focused repair session, and a little vocabulary retrieval most days. Choose one changing theme each fortnight. One fortnight might centre on childhood memories; another on food preparation; another on work and study; another on discussing a film or a general issue. The theme gives vocabulary a purpose while grammar keeps recurring.

Save a two-minute recording every two weeks using a new prompt. Compare it with an earlier recording for clarity, response to questions, and the number of successful repairs. Do not judge progress only by accent. A more intelligible sentence, a fairer summary, or a well-handled disagreement is real improvement. Ask a trusted speaker for one actionable observation instead of a general verdict about whether you “sound native.”

If you want external validation, seek a qualified teacher or assessor who understands your spoken goals and your Roman-only constraint. Explain that your target is conversation and listening, not script literacy. Continue learning from your family while respecting their time and preferences. The course has given you a structure; ongoing relationships provide the changing language that no fixed book can exhaust.

## Source checks and interpretation

The spoken target is informed by the [Council of Europe CEFR descriptors](https://www.coe.int/en/web/common-european-framework-reference-languages/cefr-descriptors). The numerical rubrics, timings, dialogues, and tasks in this chapter are original course tools and have not undergone official CEFR validation. The [Hindi Urdu Flagship assessment overview](https://hindiurduflagship.org/students-alumni/huf-language-assessment-and-testing/) informed the emphasis on sustained, unrehearsed communicative performance; no automatic ILR-to-CEFR equivalence is claimed. The source ledger distinguishes inspected material from inaccessible or partially accessible research.


<a id="course-plan"></a>

# Your 1,000-hour conversation path

Twelve substantial chapters take you from no prior knowledge toward B2 conversational abilities. Every hour allocation includes work with the material, not just reading time. The chapter plans describe their own listening, speaking, grammar, vocabulary, review, and assessment tasks.

| Chapter | Focus | Hours | Cumulative | Suggested milestone |
| --- | --- | ---: | ---: | --- |
| 01 | Sounds, first exchanges, respect and repair | 60 | 60 | Enter a short family exchange |
| 02 | Family, gender, description and possession | 70 | 130 | Introduce relatives and describe a room |
| 03 | Routines, ongoing actions, place and time | 80 | 210 | Explain a normal day and today’s changes |
| 04 | Food, buying, polite requests and directions | 80 | 290 | A2-oriented everyday transactions |
| 05 | Past events, stories and agreement | 85 | 375 | Tell a coherent past story |
| 06 | Plans, wishes, ability and obligation | 85 | 460 | Arrange a visit with a complication |
| 07 | Relationships, feelings, care and advice | 85 | 545 | Respond thoughtfully to a relative |
| 08 | Connected stories and explanations | 90 | 635 | B1-oriented connected interaction |
| 09 | Opinions, conditions and negotiation | 90 | 725 | Defend a view and respond to another |
| 10 | Aspect, nuance, particles and local speech | 90 | 815 | Understand choices beyond literal meaning |
| 11 | Calls, services, events and real-world interaction | 90 | 905 | Sustain a conversation through disruption |
| 12 | Integrated B2 capstone and independent learning | 95 | **1,000** | B2-oriented transfer tasks with human feedback |

## A realistic calendar

Ten hours each week takes about 100 study weeks; twenty hours each week takes about fifty. Holidays, illness and repeated stages extend the calendar. Choose a rhythm you can sustain. A ten-hour week could contain four ninety-minute sessions, two one-hour sessions, and one two-hour conversation-and-review block. Take breaks within longer blocks. A passive video left running does not count as focused listening practice.

For an ordinary ninety-minute session, spend ten minutes retrieving yesterday’s phrases without looking, twenty on a small explanation and its worked examples, twenty listening and shadowing, twenty changing and producing messages, ten on feedback, and ten on a short spoken retelling. Change the balance when the chapter task needs it. A session spent in a real family exchange can replace the invented dialogue work; later review what you did not understand.

The hour tables inside the chapters are the detailed allocation, and sum to the hours shown here. Do not double-count a conversation as both speaking and listening time in the log. Keep a single record for the actual session. Check a chapter after its performance task, independently of how many hours you needed.

## Where the vocabulary belongs

The 1,000-entry list is a curated conversational core with example sentences, word classes, noun gender where appropriate, and topic labels. It is not a statistically ranked corpus list. Roman spellings and regional frequencies vary, and B2 requires additional vocabulary for your own family, work, interests, and experiences. Learn roughly ten new entries at a time, and retrieve them in sentences over several days. Recognition in a flashcard is an early step; using a word appropriately while listening to someone is the larger goal.

The list’s chapter tag identifies a suggested home for a word. It is not an assertion that every inflection or subordinate construction in its example has already been taught. Beginners can treat an example as a meaningful chunk and revisit its grammar later. The chapter explanations introduce prerequisites in order.

## Four gates that need a person

After chapter 2, hold a three-minute introduction without reading and ask three follow-up questions. After chapter 4, complete a five-minute food or travel transaction with one unexpected change. After chapter 8, tell a two-minute story, then discuss it for eight minutes while your partner asks for clarification. At the endpoint, complete the listening, narration, opinion and mediation tasks in chapter 12 with a patient but natural-speaking partner.

The website’s scored tests check selected translation and grammar answers. They cannot establish B2 speaking. Use fresh topics and have a speaker judge intelligibility, interaction, range, grammatical control, register, and repair. Retake after a few days with new material rather than memorizing a single successful script.

## Acceptance criteria for this edition

- Twelve complete chapters with prerequisite-first grammar, original dialogues, worked examples, practice keys, and chapter hour plans totaling 1,000.
- Roman-only learner-facing Urdu/Hindi; no script-learning requirement.
- Hyderabadi forms integrated throughout and compared with shared alternatives; no claim that every household speaks identically.
- Exactly 1,000 distinct curated vocabulary headwords/lexical entries, with genuine example sentences and English meanings.
- 360 authored practice items across both translation directions, grammar, listening and speaking, plus vocabulary review derived from the lexical list.
- Client-side scoring with explanatory feedback; listening asks for a Roman transcript and English meaning; spontaneous speaking remains a human-assessed skill.
- Selection playback, system voice capability reporting, local family audio imports, voice recording and replay, optional local recognition, and a supported-language Chrome AI reflection tool.
- A complete single-volume book, chapter sources, a Starlight website, build output, setup instructions, source ledger, verification report, and portable archive.

Native Hyderabadi recordings and a native-speaker editorial review are not bundled or claimed. The audio library and family missions let you add that household-specific evidence as you study. A thousand hours is a practice budget, not a guaranteed learning outcome.


<a id="grammar-reference"></a>

# Grammar at the kitchen table

This desk reference accompanies the explanations in the twelve chapters. It is a memory aid after reading, rather than a substitute for those explanations. Examples use shared conversational grammar; local family patterns may vary. A slash separates alternatives, not words to say together.

## Who, what, then the verb

Ordinary neutral order is subject, other information, verb: **Main chai peeta hoon** — I drink tea (masculine speaker). **Main chai peeti hoon** uses feminine speaker agreement. Questions usually preserve the sentence’s main frame: **Aap kya peete hain?** — What do you drink? A yes/no question can begin with **kya** or use question intonation. A question word inside the sentence retains its own meaning; beginning a sentence with **kya** does not turn every following question word into a yes/no marker.

| Person | Present “be” | Short model |
| --- | --- | --- |
| main, I | hoon | Main ghar par hoon. — I am at home. |
| tum, familiar you | ho | Tum theek ho. — You are well. |
| aap, respectful you | hain | Aap theek hain. — You are well. |
| yeh/vo, singular this/that person | hai | Vo yahan hai. — That person is here. |
| hum, we | hain | Hum taiyar hain. — We are ready. |
| ye/vo, plural or honored reference | hain | Vo yahan hain. — They / the honored person are here. |

Do not equate respectful **hain** with biological plurality. Do not replace **aap ... hain** with **aap ... ho** as the beginner default; learn an actual household convention before adopting it.

## Nouns, description, and possession

Nouns have grammatical gender. Many masculine nouns ending in **-aa** change to **-e** in the direct plural and before a postposition in the singular. Other noun classes behave differently. **Ladka** → **ladke**; **ladke ko** means to/the boy, while **ladkon ko** refers to boys. Learn the noun’s full pattern instead of assuming every word ending in a vowel follows it.

Changing adjectives agree: **achha ladka**, **achhi ladki**, **achhe ladke**. Many adjectives remain unchanged, for example **saaf kamra** and **saaf mez**. The possessor does not control **ka/ki/ke**; the possessed noun does: **Ammi ki chai**, **Abbu ka kamra**, **mere dost**. **Meri kitab** is “my book” regardless of the speaker’s gender because **kitab** is feminine. Before a postposition, the whole possessed phrase takes its appropriate oblique form: **mere bhai ke saath** — with my brother.

## Postpositions and pronouns

Postpositions follow their phrase: **ghar mein**, in the house; **mez par**, on the table; **Ammi ke saath**, with Ammi; **bazaar tak**, as far as the market. They commonly require oblique forms. Pronouns have special forms: **main → mujh-**, **tum → tum-**, **vo → us-**, **yeh → is-**, with fused forms such as **mujhe**, **use**, or **ise** in suitable constructions.

**Ko** can mark a recipient, an experiencer, or a specific/animate direct object. It is not an English “to” replacement in every sentence. **Mujhe chai chahiye** — I want/need tea. **Maine Ammi ko dekha** — I saw Ammi. In the second sentence, **ko** affects perfective agreement as explained below. Local **mereku** is a family-dependent counterpart in some dative contexts; it does not authorize adding **ku** to every English “me.”

## Habit, action in progress, and the past

| Meaning | Construction | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| habitual present | stem + ta/ti/te + present be | Main roz bolti hoon. — I speak every day (f.). |
| ongoing present | stem + raha/rahi/rahe + present be | Main bol raha hoon. — I am speaking (m.). |
| ongoing past | stem + raha/rahi/rahe + past be | Main bol rahi thi. — I was speaking (f.). |
| past habit | habitual participle + tha/thi/the | Main roz bolta tha. — I used to speak every day (m.). |
| completed intransitive | perfective form | Samira aayi. — Samira came. |
| completed transitive | often subject + ne + object + perfective | Ammi ne chai banayi. — Ammi made tea. |
| result relevant now | appropriate perfective + present be | Ammi aa gayi hain. — Ammi has arrived (respectful reference). |

Perfective **ne** is not a general past-tense marker. In the shared pattern, many transitive perfective verbs mark the subject with **ne**; an unmarked direct object can then control agreement: **Maine chai pi**, because **chai** is feminine; **Maine paani piya**, because **paani** is masculine. If the object is marked with **ko**, it does not control this agreement and a default masculine singular form commonly appears: **Maine Ammi ko dekha**. Intransitive **Main gayi** has no **ne**. Local Dakhni case/agreement patterns can differ; chapter 5 separates the shared production model from what a learner may hear at home.

Important irregular completed forms include **karna → kiya**, **jaana → gaya/gayi/gaye**, **lena → liya/li**, **dena → diya/di**, and **hona → hua/hui/hue**. Learn these as meaningful short sentences, not as a mechanical ending attached to the infinitive.

## Wanting, needing, ability, and obligation

**Mujhe paani chahiye** describes need or desire for water. **Main paani peena chahta/chahti hoon** expresses wanting to drink water. **Main aa sakta/sakti hoon** means I can come; **Main aa paya/payi** means I managed to come. **Mujhe jaana hai** often expresses a plan or obligation; **Mujhe jaana padta hai** describes having to go; **Aapko aaram karna chahiye** is advice that you should rest. These are different meanings, not interchangeable “modal verb” slots.

Infinitival complements and agreement depend on the construction. For a first usable pattern, learn **Mujhe chai peeni hai**, with **chai** feminine, and compare **Mujhe paani peena hai**. Do not infer that every infinitive after every modal agrees with the object. **Sakna** takes the bare verbal stem: **Main bol sakti hoon**, not **Main bolna sakti hoon**.

## Requests and negation

Use **boliye**, **suniye**, **aaiye**, **baithiye**, **lijiye**, and **dijiye** as respectful request forms. **Zara dheere boliye** — please speak a little slowly. **Kya aap dobara keh sakte hain?** — could you say it again? A feminine referent can take **sakti hain**, depending on reference and household usage; **aap ... hain** retains respect.

**Nahin** negates ordinary statements: **Mujhe maloom nahin** — I do not know. **Mat** is common in negative commands: **Fikr mat kijiye** — do not worry. Local **nakko** commonly appears in refusals/unwanted things and prohibitions, with pragmatic effects depending on tone. It is not a universal replacement for **nahin**: learn **Mereku chai nakko** as a contextual family pattern, alongside **Mujhe chai nahin chahiye**. A blunt refusal may need softening with thanks or a reason.

## Future, uncertainty, and conditions

Future endings reflect person, gender and number: **Main aaunga/aaungi**, I will come; **Hum aayenge**, we will come; **Aap aayenge/aayengi?**, will you come? Shared infinitive **aana** changes its stem; memorizing **-ga** alone is insufficient. The subjunctive makes possibilities, wishes, and conditions: **Aap aayein to achha ho** — it would be good if you came. A future can also express inference in context: **Vo ghar par honge** may mean they will be at home or they are probably at home.

**Agar baarish hui, to hum ghar par rahenge** — If it rains, we will stay home. A more remote hypothesis uses different forms: **Agar mere paas waqt hota, to main aata/aati** — If I had time, I would come. A past counterfactual needs a completed-past frame: **Agar aapne bataya hota, to main aa gaya/gayi hota/hoti** — If you had told me, I would have come. Agreement and discourse context matter; chapter 9 works through the changes rather than treating all English “if” sentences as the same rule.

## Connecting two thoughts

**Jo ... vo** connects an identifying relative and its partner: **Jo chai aapne banayi thi, vo bahut achhi thi** — The tea you made was very good. **Jab ... tab** connects times; **jahan ... vahan** connects places; **jaisa ... vaisa** connects manner or similarity. Use a complete example before trying to substitute every English “who” into **jo**.

**Mujhe lagta hai ki ...** — I think that ...; **Unhone kaha ki ...** — they said that ... . Pronouns and time references depend on whose perspective is being reported. **Kyunki** gives a reason, **isliye** gives a consequence, **lekin/magar** introduce contrast, **haalanki** introduces concession, and **phir bhi** means nevertheless/even so. A conjunctive **kar/ke** links actions, often with a shared subject: **Chai pi kar hum nikle** — Having drunk tea, we left. Use a separate clause if the subjects change and the short construction would be ambiguous.

## Nuance to recognize and then use

**Main kaam kar raha hoon** says work is in progress. **Main kaam kar chuka hoon** emphasizes completion. **Main kaam karne laga** says I began working. **Main kaam karta raha** says I kept working. Vector verbs affect aspect or attitude: **likh lena**, write for one’s own benefit/complete it; **bata dena**, tell/convey it; **so jaana**, fall asleep. They are not always literal taking, giving, or going.

In causatives, another participant causes an action: **banana**, make; **banwana**, have something made. In a passive, the affected thing can become central: **Darwaza khola gaya** — the door was opened. Both alter the information structure and agreement requirements. Conversation often prefers a named person and an active verb when responsibility matters.

**Bhi** includes, **hi** focuses, **to** can establish contrast or a topic, and **na** can request confirmation or soften/press a turn. Local **ich** often has an emphatic/focusing role, but exact placement and tone need actual household examples. **Aaj hi** — today itself/today specifically; **aaj bhi** — today too/still today. Changing the particle changes the intended message, even when the rest of the sentence is identical.

## Repair phrases to overlearn

**Zara aahista boliye.** — Please speak a little slowly. **Aakhri baat samajh nahin aayi.** — I did not understand the last point. **Aapka matlab ... hai?** — Do you mean ...? **Isko Urdu mein kya kehte hain?** — What do you call this in Urdu? **Ek misaal dijiye.** — Give an example, please. **Main doosre tareeqe se bolta/bolti hoon.** — Let me say it another way. Repair is part of conversational skill, not evidence that the conversation has failed.


<a id="field-guide"></a>

# Listen for your family’s Hyderabad

## A local form carries a relationship

The aim is comfortable family participation. Shared grammar and local speech work together: a speaker may say **hau** in one turn and **haan ji** in another, or switch to English for a topic while retaining local rhythm. The course does not prescribe a comic accent or treat all people from the Deccan as interchangeable. Dakhni’s labels and relationship with Urdu and Hindi are also identity questions; respect the name a speaker uses for their own language.

| Local form to recognize | Shared counterpart | What to check at home |
| --- | --- | --- |
| hau | haan / haan ji | An affirmative; listen for vowel quality and respectful replies. |
| nakko | nahin chahiye / mat, in appropriate contexts | Refusal, unwantedness or prohibition; not every statement negation. |
| kaiku | kyun | Why; tone can sound curious, teasing or challenging. |
| mereku / mere ko | mujhe | Dative/experiencer contexts; not a word-for-word substitute in every construction. |
| tereku / tere ko | tujhe / tumhe, with register differences | Familiarity matters. Do not address an elder this way by default. |
| ich | hi, in many focus contexts | Placement, attachment and emphasis vary; collect whole phrases. |
| kidhar | kahan | A place question heard beyond Hyderabad too; not exclusively Dakhni. |
| idhar / udhar | yahan / vahan | Here/there or this/that direction; shared colloquial usage also exists. |

**Hau, chai chahiye** — yes, I would like tea. **Shukriya, abhi chai nakko** — thank you, I do not want tea now (local, contextual). **Aap kaiku poochh rahe hain?** — why are you asking? That last question can sound confrontational even with **aap**; a softer context and tone matter. A useful repair is **Main samajhne ke liye poochh raha/rahi hoon** — I am asking to understand.

## Local grammar is more than a few words

You may hear contractions, auxiliaries left unspoken where shared textbook speech includes them, different case/agreement patterns, and aspect forms shaped by regional contact. There is no one mechanical conversion that turns shared Urdu into every household’s Dakhni. This course teaches the shared pattern explicitly, points out selected local alternatives, and asks you to collect complete family examples. Do not remove every **ne**, merge every auxiliary, or attach **ich** at random.

A grammatical form can be productive for one relative and something another relative only recognizes. Keep these two abilities separate. A beginner can understand **mereku** while answering respectfully with **mujhe**. Recognition comes before imitation when social meaning is uncertain.

## Recognize a few deeper local patterns

Some Dakkhini varieties put **bol ke** after a quoted message, where shared speech often introduces the message with **ki**. Treat **“Kal aao” bol ke kaha** as a recognition model: someone said “come tomorrow.” Do not translate its **bol ke** as a separate journey or action. This is different from mechanically inserting **bol ke** after every clause.

Research also describes a relative use of **so** after a verbal phrase: a frame like **kal aaye so ...** can identify the person who came yesterday. Shared speech might use **jo kal aaye the ...**. Do not assume the shared **so** meaning “sleep!” applies here. Collect a complete example from your family before producing this pattern.

These are selective recognition notes, not universal conversion rules. [Subbarao’s supplementary syntax material](https://drupal-s3fs-prod.s3.eu-west-1.amazonaws.com/resources/academic/3513/6680/1739/Subbarao_web_material_9780521861489.pdf), printed pages 52–58 and 193–197, discusses these contact-related Dakkhini constructions. Its documented varieties do not establish every household’s current usage.

## A family recording session that respects people’s time

Ask a willing speaker for five minutes, not a performance of an entire dictionary. Say: **Main apni Urdu behtar karna chahta/chahti hoon. Kya aap meri madad karenge?** — I want to improve my Urdu. Will you help me? Explain that the recording stays on your device unless you choose to share it. Let them decline or stop.

Choose five phrases the speaker actually says: welcoming someone, offering tea, checking a plan, refusing gently, ending a call. Ask for one natural-speed version and one clear version. Keep the whole sentence with its situation. Write a Roman transcript, English meaning, speaker relationship, and date. Do not ask the speaker to make a sentence “more Hyderabadi” by adding a stereotype.

In the speaking studio, upload a reference clip with its exact Roman transcript. Selection playback looks for that transcript before attempting synthetic speech. Select a smaller word only if you also have a recording for that word; a sentence recording cannot automatically be cut into accurate word clips. Keep the original files because browser storage can be cleared.

## A productive question after every conversation

Ask **Aap aam taur par isko kaise bolte hain?** — How do you usually say this? Then ask who they would use that version with. Record both the shared frame and the family version. A correction without its situation is easy to misuse. Over the course, collect at least fifty such pairs, ten polite refusals, ten ways to gain a turn, and ten short stories retold by two different relatives. These are personal expansion targets, not extra words counted toward the 1,000-entry core.

## What is verified and what needs a speaker

The shared grammar was cross-checked against institutional Hindi-Urdu materials. The source ledger identifies inspected and partially accessible dialect research. This is an authored course, not a native-speaker-reviewed dialect grammar. The local examples are cautious learning models, and native family review is still needed for your household’s pronunciation, acceptability, register, and preferred vocabulary. The website makes those corrections usable by letting you add recordings and keep a personal notebook.


<a id="word-core"></a>

# The 1,000-word conversational core

Curated lexical entries rather than a statistical frequency ranking. Some established expressions are learned as a unit. Gender marks noun agreement, and a dash means not applicable or not assigned for that entry. Examples are models; expand beyond this core for your life.

| # | Roman | Meaning · class · gender | Example | English |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | main | I · function · - | Main yahan hoon. | I am here. |
| 2 | hum | we · function · - | Hum tayyar hain. | We are ready. |
| 3 | tum | you; familiar · function · - | Tum kahan ho? | Where are you? |
| 4 | aap | you; respectful · function · - | Aap kaise hain? | How are you? |
| 5 | yeh | this; this person · function · - | Yeh mera ghar hai. | This is my house. |
| 6 | vo | that; he; she; they · function · - | Vo ghar par hain. | They are at home. |
| 7 | mujhe | to me; me · function · - | Mujhe paani chahiye. | I need water. |
| 8 | humein | to us; us · function · - | Humein waqt chahiye. | We need time. |
| 9 | tumhe | to you; familiar · function · - | Tumhe kya chahiye? | What do you need? |
| 10 | aapko | to you; respectful · function · - | Aapko chai chahiye? | Would you like tea? |
| 11 | usko | to him; to her · function · - | Usko phone kijiye. | Call him or her. |
| 12 | unko | to them; respectful him or her · function · - | Unko bata dijiye. | Tell them. |
| 13 | mera | my; masculine · function · - | Mera naam Ali hai. | My name is Ali. |
| 14 | hamara | our; masculine · function · - | Hamara ghar paas hai. | Our house is nearby. |
| 15 | tumhara | your; familiar masculine · function · - | Tumhara naam kya hai? | What is your name? |
| 16 | aapka | your; respectful masculine · function · - | Aapka bag yahan hai. | Your bag is here. |
| 17 | uska | his; her; its masculine possession · function · - | Uska ghar door hai. | His or her house is far. |
| 18 | unka | their; respectful possession · function · - | Unka naam Sara hai. | Her name is Sara. |
| 19 | apna | one's own; masculine · function · - | Apna bag lijiye. | Take your own bag. |
| 20 | khud | self; personally · function · - | Main khud karunga. | I will do it myself. |
| 21 | kaun | who · function · - | Kaun aaya? | Who came? |
| 22 | kya | what; question marker · function · - | Yeh kya hai? | What is this? |
| 23 | kahan | where · function · - | Aap kahan hain? | Where are you? |
| 24 | kab | when · function · - | Aap kab aayenge? | When will you come? |
| 25 | kyun | why · function · - | Aap kyun gaye? | Why did you go? |
| 26 | kaise | how · function · - | Yeh kaise hota hai? | How does this happen? |
| 27 | kitna | how much; masculine · function · - | Kitna paani chahiye? | How much water is needed? |
| 28 | kaisa | what kind; masculine · function · - | Kaisa khaana chahiye? | What kind of food do you want? |
| 29 | kiska | whose; masculine · function · - | Yeh kiska bag hai? | Whose bag is this? |
| 30 | kaunsa | which; masculine · function · - | Kaunsa kamra hai? | Which room is it? |
| 31 | haan | yes · function · - | Haan, main aaunga. | Yes, I will come. |
| 32 | nahin | no; not · function · - | Main ghar par nahin hoon. | I am not at home. |
| 33 | ji | respectful acknowledgment · function · - | Ji, batayiye. | Yes, please tell me. |
| 34 | aur | and; more · function · - | Chai aur paani lijiye. | Have tea and water. |
| 35 | lekin | but · function · - | Main aaunga lekin der se. | I will come, but late. |
| 36 | ya | or · function · - | Chai ya paani? | Tea or water? |
| 37 | toh | then; so · function · - | Toh hum chalein? | Then shall we leave? |
| 38 | bhi | also; even · function · - | Main bhi aaunga. | I will come too. |
| 39 | hi | restrictive emphasis; only · function · - | Main hi gaya tha. | I was the one who went. |
| 40 | sirf | only · function · - | Sirf ek chahiye. | Only one is needed. |
| 41 | ka | of; masculine possession · function · - | Ali ka ghar hai. | It is Ali's house. |
| 42 | ki | of; feminine possession; that · function · - | Yeh Ammi ki kitaab hai. | This is Ammi's book. |
| 43 | ke | of; plural or oblique possession · function · - | Yeh mere bhai ke joote hain. | These are my brother's shoes. |
| 44 | ko | to; object marker · function · - | Ammi ko phone karo. | Call Ammi. |
| 45 | se | from; with; by · function · - | Main ghar se aaya. | I came from home. |
| 46 | mein | in · function · - | Paani glass mein hai. | The water is in the glass. |
| 47 | par | on; at · function · - | Kitaab mez par hai. | The book is on the table. |

# Sources, authorship, and browser support

This edition contains original explanations, dialogues, tasks and examples. Institutional sources cross-check the shared grammar. Partial inspection is identified below; an index or search result is not treated as a fully read book. No full copyrighted source is redistributed. The vocabulary is curated, not a published frequency ranking. Native Hyderabadi editorial and pronunciation review remains outstanding.

## Browser and audio behavior

The course text and quizzes are static and client-side. Microphone recording needs HTTPS or localhost and browser permission. Chrome’s current Prompt API lists English, Japanese, Spanish, German and French for text; it does not list Urdu. The optional AI coach therefore works with an English reflection on learning, not an Urdu transcript or pronunciation grade. Language-model and recognition availability are checked on your device; downloads start only through their buttons.

System Urdu/Hindi voices vary by device. Where a voice exists, mapped machine-only native spelling supplies pronunciation input while the interface stays Roman. Some voices are network services; their local/provider status is shown. No recorded native audio ships with this edition. Uploaded family audio is stored locally and takes priority for exact transcript matches. Unmapped selections ask for a family recording unless approximate Roman TTS is explicitly enabled.

The journal uses local browser storage; family audio uses IndexedDB. No account, API key, analytics service, or course server receives your journal or microphone recordings. Your browser’s voice provider may process synthetic speech text when the selected voice is not local. Keep backups and original recordings.

## Source ledger

# Using the included companion

The complete Starlight site source and built website are included with this book. See [setup instructions](README.md) and the [portable server guide](companion/README.md). The practice engine source is [studio.mjs](public/studio.mjs), with [grading and review logic](public/core.mjs) and [audio handling](public/audio.mjs). No external API key is required.
